## THE FOURTH BOOK.

---

### *Of the life of Natural things.*

<margin>The purpose of air in relation to the life of all things.</margin>
**N**o one can deny that <term>air</term> <note>original: "Aire"; here used in the sense of a vital spirit or "pneuma" that sustains existence</note> gives life to all things, bodies, and substances that are produced and generated from the Earth. Now you must know what the life of every thing in particular is, and what kind of thing it is: it is nothing other than a spiritual essence—a thing that is invisible and intangible <note>original: "impalpable"</note>, a spirit, and spiritual.
<margin>What the life of things consists of.</margin>
Therefore, there is no physical <note>original: "corporeall"</note> thing which does not have a spirit lying hidden within it, as well as a life, which, as
<margin>Which things possess life.</margin>

<meta type="catchword">I</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>Of the Nature of Things. BOOK IV.</header>
<meta>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the essential nature of life and matter.</meta>

...as I said before, is nothing but a spiritual thing. For life belongs not only to that which moves and stirs, such as men, animals, vermin of the earth, birds in the air, and fish in the sea, but also to all corporeal and substantial things. For here we must know that God, in the beginning of the creation of all things, created no body at all without its spirit, which it secretly contains within it.

<margin>What is the difference between the Spirit and the Body.</margin>
For what is the body without a spirit? Nothing at all. Therefore, the spirit—and not the body—secretly contains the virtue <note>original: "vertue"; here meaning the inherent power or essential property</note> and power of the thing. For in the body there is death, and the body is the subject of death; nothing else is to be sought for in the body but death.

<margin>The Spirit never dies.</margin>
For the body may be destroyed and corrupted in several ways, but the spirit cannot. For the living spirit remains forever and is also the subject of life, preserving the body alive. But in the ruin of the body, the spirit is separated from it, leaves behind a dead body, and returns to its place from which it came—namely, into the Chaos <term>the primordial state of matter or the atmosphere</term> and the air of the upper and lower firmament <note>the heavens or the expanse of the sky</note>. Hence it appears that there are diverse spirits, just as there are diverse bodies.

<margin>The division of the Spirit according to the variety of bodies.</margin>
For there are celestial, infernal, human, metallic, and mineral spirits, as well as spirits of salts, gems, marcasites <term>a mineral similar to pyrite, often called "white iron pyrite"</term>, arsenics, drinkable things, roots, juices, flesh, blood, bones, and so on. 

<margin>The Spirit is the life of all corporeal things.</margin>
Therefore, know also that the spirit is most truly the life and balsam <note>original: "balsome"; a life-preserving or healing essence</note> of all corporeal things. But now we will proceed to the species, and briefly describe to you here the life of every natural thing in particular.

<summary>This page argues that all matter, including minerals and plants, possesses a "spirit" which is the true source of life and power, while the physical body is merely the subject of death. Upon the body's destruction, this eternal spirit returns to the primordial Chaos or the firmament.</summary>
<keywords>spirit vs body, natural philosophy, Chaos, Firmament, balsam, vitalism, alchemy, marcasite</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "life" or essential spirit within different categories of matter, moving from general human life to the specific "lives" of metals and Mercury.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
### BOOK IV. Of the Nature of Things.

<margin>What the life of man is.</margin>
The life, therefore, of all men is nothing else but an <term>astral balsam</term> <note>original: "Astrall balsome"; in alchemy, this refers to a celestial life-force or spiritual substance that preserves the body from decay.</note>, a balsamic impression, a celestial invisible fire, an enclosed air, and a <term>tinging spirit of salt</term> <note>original: "tinging spirit of Salt"; a concentrated essence of salt believed to possess the power to change or "tinge" the properties of other substances.</note>. I cannot name it more plainly, although it is described by many names. And since we have declared the best and most important, we shall remain silent regarding those which are less significant.

<margin>What the life of metals is.</margin>
The life of metals is a <term>hidden oiliness</term> <note>original: "secret fatnesse"; this refers to the flammable or fusible principle within a substance.</note> which they have received from <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the principle of combustibility, fluid "fatness," and the "soul" of a substance.</note>. This is made manifest by their melting; for everything that melts in the fire does so because of that hidden oiliness within it. Unless that were in it, no metal could melt—as we see in iron and steel, which have less Sulphur and oiliness than all the other metals. Therefore, they are of a drier nature than all the rest.

<margin>What the life of Mercury is. Mercury is like a garment made of skins.</margin>
The life of <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of volatility, liquidity, and the "spirit" of a substance.</note> is nothing else but internal heat and external coldness—that is, it makes the internal part of its body hot and the outward part cold. Therefore, it might well be compared to a garment made of skins, which, just like Mercury, provides both heat and cold. For if a man wears such a garment, it makes him warm and keeps him from the cold; but if the smooth part of those skins is placed against his naked body, it makes him cold and is good against excessive heat. Therefore, it was a custom in ancient times (and still is in some places) to wear skins in both summer and winter: as a protection against cold in the one, so against heat in the other. In summer, they turn the smooth side inward and the hairy side outward; and in winter, the hairy side inward, and...

<summary>This page defines the "life" or essential vital principle of humans, metals, and Mercury using alchemical terms like "astral balsam" and "secret fatness." The author explains that a metal's ability to melt comes from its sulphur-derived oiliness and compares Mercury’s dual nature to the insulating properties of animal skins.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, astral balsam, sulphur, mercury, metals, physiology, secret fatness, spirit of salt</keywords>

# On the Nature of Things. Book 4.

...the smooth side outward. Therefore, just as you have heard about the garment of skins, the same can also be said of mercury.

<margin>What the life of sulfur is.</margin>
The life of sulfur is a combustible, stinking fatness <note>original: "fatnesse"; in alchemy, this refers to the flammable, oily essence of a substance</note>. For as long as it burns and smells, it can be said to be alive.

<margin>What the life of salts is.</margin>
Now, the life of all salts is nothing other than the spirit of *aqua fortis* <note>original Latin: "strong water," referring to nitric acid</note>. For when that water is drawn from them, whatever remains at the bottom is called "dead earth."

<margin>What the life of gems and corals is.</margin>
The life of gems and corals is only their color, which can be taken from them using spirit of wine <note>original: "spirit of Wine"; high-proof alcohol or ethanol used as a solvent</note>.

<margin>What the life of pearls is.</margin>
The life of pearls is nothing but their luster <note>original: "splendor"</note>, which they lose during calcination <term>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its state or remove impurities</term>.

<margin>What the life of the lodestone is.</margin>
The life of the lodestone <term>a naturally magnetized mineral</term> is the spirit of iron, which may be extracted and removed with spirit of wine.

<margin>What the life of flints is.</margin>
The life of flints is a mucilaginous <term>thick or jelly-like</term> matter.

<margin>What the life of marcasites is.</margin>
The life of marcasites <note>iron sulfide minerals</note>, cachymia <note>an alchemical term for metallic ores that are not yet fully matured into metals</note>, talc, cobalt, zimri <note>an archaic term likely referring to a specific mineral impurity or slag</note>, garnets <note>original: "granata"</note>, bismuth <note>original: "wismat"</note>, and antimony is a coloring metallic spirit.

<margin>The life of arsenics.</margin>
The life of arsenics, auripigment <note>orpiment, a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, orpiment <note>original: "operment"</note>, realgar <note>a ruby-red arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, and similar substances is a mineral and solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note> poison.

<margin>The life of excrement.</margin>
The life of excrement—that is, of human or animal dung—is its stinking smell. For once this is lost, they are dead.

<margin>The life of aromatic things.</margin>
The life of aromatic things—namely musk, ambergris <note>original: "Amber Gryse"; a waxy substance from sperm whales used in perfume</note>, civet <note>a musk-like scent from the civet cat</note>, and whatever else yields a strong, pleasant, and sweet smell—is nothing but 그 pleasing odor. For if they lose this, they are dead and of no use.

<summary>This page defines the "life" or vital essence of various inanimate substances, identifying their primary characteristics—such as the scent of perfumes, the magnetism of lodestones, or the color of gems—as the source of their power and utility.</summary>

<margin>The life of sweet things.</margin>
The life of sweet things, such as *sugar*, *honey*, *manna*, *cassia*, and the like, lies in their coloring <note>original: "tinging"</note> and subtle sweetness. If that sweetness is taken from them by distilling or subliming, they are dead, useless, and worth nothing.

<margin>The life of resins.</margin>
The life of all *resins* <note>original: "rozzens"</note>, such as *amber*, *turpentine*, and *gum*, is the sticky <note>original: "muciliginous"</note>, shining oiliness which gives an excellent varnish to them all. When they no longer produce varnish and lose their shine, they are dead.

<margin>The life of plants.</margin>
The life of *herbs*, *roots*, *apples*, and other such fruits is nothing else but the moisture <note>original: "liquor"</note> of the Earth, which they lose naturally if they simply lack water and soil.

<margin>The life of wood.</margin>
The life of *wood* is a certain *resin*. Any wood that lacks resin can live no longer.

<margin>The life of bones.</margin>
The life of *bones* is the <term>liquor of mummy</term> <note>In alchemy, "mumia" or "mummy" referred to a vital balsamic essence found in living things, or a medicinal preparation derived from preserved remains.</note>.

<margin>The life of flesh</margin>
The life of *flesh* and *blood* is nothing else but the <term>spirit of salt</term> <note>While modern "spirit of salt" is hydrochloric acid, here the author refers to the essential salt-principle that prevents decay.</note>, which preserves them from smelling and rotting, and is itself like water separated from them.

<margin>The life of every element.</margin>
Now, concerning the life of the *elements*, you must know that the life of *water* is its flowing. For when it is frozen into ice by the coldness of the *sky* <note>original: "firmament"</note>, it is dead, and its dangerousness <note>original: "mischievousnesse"</note> is taken from it, so that no one can be drowned in it.

<margin>What the life of fire is.</margin>
The life of *fire* is *air*, for *air* makes fire burn with greater intensity and heat. Also, a kind of *air* <note>Likely referring to the rising heat or smoke.</note> comes forth from all *fire* which will blow out a candle and lift up a feather, as you may see daily before your eyes. Therefore,

### OF THE

### THE FIFTH BOOK.

#### *Of the Death, or ruin of all things.*

<margin>What death is</margin>
The death of all natural things is nothing else but an alteration and destruction of their powers and virtues <note>original: "vertues"; in alchemy and early science, this refers to the inherent medicinal or active properties of a substance</note>, a predominance <note>original: "predominancy"</note> of that which is evil, and an overcoming of what is good—an abolishing of the former nature and the generation of a new and different nature. For you must know that there are many things that, while they are alive, have within them several virtues, but when they are dead retain little or nothing of

<summary>This page introduces Book Five, defining death not as total extinction, but as the destruction of a thing's original "virtues" and its transformation into a new state or nature.</summary>
<keywords>Death, transformation, virtues, alchemy, natural philosophy, Paracelsus, generation, alteration</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion on how the "death" or "corruption" of a substance can actually reveal its hidden powers, particularly in the context of alchemy.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

# Of the Nature of Things. BOOK V.

their power, but become distasteful and unprofitable. Conversely, many things are harmful while they are "alive," but after they are dead and "corrupted" <note>In alchemy, "corruption" refers to the process of breaking down a substance's natural state to refine it into something more potent.</note>, they manifest a manifold power and virtue and are very useful. We could provide many examples to confirm this, but that does not belong to our present purpose. 

But so that I may not seem to write based only on my own opinion, but out of my actual experience, it will be necessary for me to produce one example with which I shall silence those <term>Sophisters</term> <note>original: "Sophisters"; here referring to academic critics or those who use clever but fallacious arguments.</note> who say that we can receive nothing from "dead" things, and that we must neither seek nor expect to find anything in them. The reason they say this is because they have no respect for the preparations of <term>alchemists</term>, through which many such great secrets are discovered. 

For look upon <term>mercury</term>, crude <term>sulfur</term>, and crude <term>antimony</term> as they are taken out of their mines—that is, while they are "living"—and see what little power there is in them, and how slowly they demonstrate their virtues. Indeed, they do more hurt than good and are more like poison than a medicine. But if, through the industry of a skillful alchemist, they are "corrupted" in their first substance and wisely prepared—

<margin>The preparation of mercury, sulfur, and antimony.</margin> namely, if <term>mercury</term> is <term>coagulated</term> <note>the process of turning a liquid into a solid or semi-solid state.</note>, <term>precipitated</term>, <term>sublimed</term> <note>the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note>, dissolved, and turned into an oil; if <term>sulfur</term> is sublimed, <term>calcined</term> <note>reduced to powder or ash by roasting or strong heat.</note>, <term>reverberated</term> <note>heated in a furnace where the flames are reflected back onto the substance.</note>, and turned into an oil; and also if <term>antimony</term> is sublimed, calcined, reverberated, and turned into an oil—you shall see how useful they are, how much strength and power they have, and how quickly they demonstrate their effectiveness. No man is able to speak enough in praise of these results or even to describe them. For their virtues are many—indeed, more than will ever be found out by any man. Therefore,

He refutes critics who claim that dead or decomposed substances hold no value, citing the transformative results of alchemical techniques like sublimation and calcination.</summary>

Let every faithful alchemist and physician spend their whole lives searching into these three: for they will abundantly recompense them for all their labor, study, and costs.

<margin>What the death of man is.</margin>
But to come to particulars, and to write specifically of the death and destruction of every natural thing—and what the death of every thing is, and in what manner every thing is destroyed—you must know first that the death of man is, without doubt, nothing else but an end of his daily work, the removal of the air, the decaying of the natural balsam <note>original: "balsome"; in Paracelsian medicine, this refers to an internal life-preserving fluid that prevents decay</note>, the extinguishing of the natural light, and the great separation of the three substances—namely, the body, soul, and spirit—and their return to where they came from. Because a natural man is made of the earth, the earth is also his mother, to which he must return. There he must lose his natural earthly flesh, and so be regenerated at the Last Day in a new celestial and purified flesh, as Christ said to Nicodemus when he came to him by night. For this is how those words regarding regeneration must be understood.

<margin>What the destruction of metals is.</margin>
The death and destruction of metals is the disjoining of their bodies and sulphurous fatness <note>original: "sulphureous fatnesse"; an alchemical term for the combustible principle or "soul" of a metal</note>, which may be done in several ways, such as by calcination, reverberation, dissolution, cementation, and sublimation <term>calcination, reverberation, dissolution, cementation, and sublimation</term> <note>These are primary alchemical laboratory processes involving the application of heat, acid, or chemical reactions to break down and purify substances.</note>.

<margin>The calcination of metals is of many types.</margin>
But the calcination of metals is not of only one kind: for one is made with salt, another with sulfur, another with *aqua fortis* <note>Latin for "strong water," referring to nitric acid</note>, another with common sublimate <note>usually mercury sublimate or mercuric chloride</note>, and another with quicksilver <note>liquid mercury</note>.

<margin>Calcination with sulfur.</margin>
Calcination with sulfur is when the metal is made into thin plates, sprinkled with sulfur, and reverberated <note>heated in a furnace where the flames are reflected back onto the material</note>.

<margin>Calcination with *aqua fortis*.</margin>
<term>Calcination</term> <note>Original: "Calcinaion"</note> with *aqua fortis* <note>original: "Aqua fortis"; nitric acid</note> is when the metal is broken into very small pieces, dissolved in the acid, and then precipitated within it.

<margin>Calcination with mercury sublimate.</margin>
Calcination with sublimed mercury <note>mercury(II) chloride</note> is done like this: the metal is made into thin plates, and the mercury is placed in an earthen vessel that is narrow at the top and wide at the bottom. Then, let it be set over a gentle coal fire, which must be blown slightly until the mercury begins to fume and a white cloud emerges from the mouth of the vessel. Then, let the metal plate be placed at the top of the vessel so that the sublimed mercury penetrates the metal and makes it as brittle as a piece of coal.

<margin>Calcination with quicksilver.</margin>
Calcination with quicksilver <note>liquid mercury</note> is when the metal is made very small and thin and is amalgamated with the quicksilver. Afterward, the quicksilver is strained through leather, and the metal remains in the leather like chalk or sand.

<margin>Various other sorts of mortification of metals.</margin>
Now, besides these mortifications <note>the chemical "killing" or alteration of a metal's primary form</note> of metals and the destruction of their "lives," know also that there are even more. For rust is the death of all iron and steel, and all vitriol <note>metal sulfates</note> and burnt brass is mortified copper. All precipitated, sublimated, or calcined cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide</note> is mortified mercury; all ceruse <note>white lead</note> and minium <note>red lead</note> is mortified lead; all lazure <note>a blue pigment or mineral</note> is mortified silver. Also, all gold from which its tincture, quintessence, resin, crocus <note>a fine powder produced by calcination</note>, vitriol, or sulfur is extracted, is

<margin>A two-fold preparation of Crocus Martis <note>Saffron of Mars, or iron oxide</note>.</margin>
But let us proceed to show how metals may be further mortified. First, concerning iron, know that it is mortified and reduced into <term>Crocus</term> <note>original: "Crocus" – a term used by early chemists for a fine metallic powder, usually an oxide, named for its saffron-like color</note> in this way: 

Form steel into very thin plates. Heat these plates until they are red hot and quench them in the best wine vinegar. Do this repeatedly until the vinegar has acquired a significant redness. Then, distill off the vinegar until only a dry powder remains. This is a most excellent <term>Crocus Martis</term> <note>Iron oxide</note>.

There is also another way of making <term>Crocus Martis</term> which partly exceeds the former method and is achieved with far less cost and effort; it is as follows:

Strew equal quantities of sulfur and tartar upon the steel plates; then <term>reverberate</term> <note>to heat a substance in a furnace where the flames are turned back or deflected onto the material</note> them. This will produce a most excellent <term>Crocus</term>, which must then be scraped off the plates.

You should also know that every plate of iron or steel, if it is dissolved with <term>Aqua fortis</term> <note>Nitric acid</note>, will also make a very fine <term>Crocus</term>. It can similarly be made with <term>oil of vitriol</term> <note>Sulfuric acid</note>, <term>spirit of salt</term> <note>Hydrochloric acid</note>, alum water, the water of <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>Ammonium chloride</note>, and of <term>saltpeter</term> <note>Potassium nitrate</note>, as well as with <term>sublimated mercury</term> <note>Mercury(II) chloride</note>. All of these mortify iron and turn it into a <term>Crocus</term>. 

However, none of these latter methods are comparable to the first two, for these are only used in alchemy and not at all in medicine <note>original: "Physicke"</note>. Therefore, for medical purposes, use only the first two methods and ignore the rest.

<margin>The mortification of copper.</margin>
The mortification <term>mortification: in alchemy, this refers to the process of "killing" or changing a metal's outward physical form to reveal its inner properties or transform it into a new substance</term> of copper—that is, how it may be reduced into vitriol <note>original: "Vitriall"; copper sulfate, often used in medicine and chemistry</note> or verdigris—can be done in many ways. There are several processes for this, though some are far better and more profitable than others. Therefore, it is most convenient here to describe the best and most profitable method and remain silent about the rest. The best, easiest, and most exact way of reducing copper into vitriol is as follows:

<margin>The vitriol of copper is made in two ways.</margin>
Dip plates of copper in **spirit of salt** <note>original: "spirit of Salt"; modern: hydrochloric acid</note> or **saltpeter**, and hang them in the air until they begin to turn green, which will happen quickly. Wash off this greenness with clear spring water, dry the plates with a cloth, and wet them again with the spirit of salt and saltpeter. Repeat this until the water is visibly green or until much vitriol is floating on the surface. Then pour away the water or evaporate it, and you will have a most excellent vitriol for medicine. In alchemy, there is no vitriol more beautiful, excellent, or better than that which is made with *aqua fortis* <note>original: "Aqua fortis"; modern: nitric acid</note>, *aqua regia* <note>original: "Aqua regis"; a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold</note>, or the **spirit of sal ammoniac** <note>original: "spirit of Salt Armoniacke"; ammonium carbonate or liquid ammonia</note>. The process is this:

Let plates of copper be dissolved <note>original: "melted"; in this context, the author means chemically dissolved by the acids rather than melted by heat</note> with one of the aforementioned waters. As soon as the greenness is extracted and the plates have dried, let the greenness be wiped off with the foot of a **hare**, or by any other method you prefer, just as white lead <note>original: "Ceruse"; a white pigment made from lead</note> is taken off from plates of lead. Wet them again as before until the plates are entirely consumed. This creates a most magnificent vitriol that you cannot help but marvel at.

<margin>How water of saltpeter and sal ammoniac is made.</margin>
The water of saltpeter is made like this: purify and powder it; afterward, dissolve it by itself in a bladder <note>animal bladders were used as flexible containers for "digesting" or dissolving substances in a gentle heat, such as a water bath</note>...

<margin>How verdigris to be used in medicine is to be prepared.</margin>

You will see that the matter sticking to the plates is very black, but do not let that trouble you. For if you set those plates in the open air, all the black matter will become green in a few days and become a most excellent verdigris. This may be called the <term>balsam of copper</term> <margin>The balsam of copper.</margin> and is recommended by all physicians. Nevertheless, do not wonder that this verdigris becomes green in the air, or that the air can change the black color into such a beautiful green.

<margin>Air changes the colors of burnt things.</margin>
For you must know that daily experience in alchemy shows that any dead earth, or <term>caput mortuum</term> <note>Latin: "dead head"; the useless residue left after distillation or sublimation</note>, quickly takes on another color as soon as it comes out of the fire and into the air, leaving behind the color it acquired in the fire. <meta>This page continues the discussion of how colors manifest during the chemical transformation or "death" of matter, specifically focusing on the residues left after fire treatment and the preparation of copper-based pigments.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
# Of the Nature of things. BOOK V.

...of those colors are various. For as the matter is, so are the colors that are produced, although for the most part they flow from the blackness of the "dead earth" <note>original: "dead earth"; referring to the *caput mortuum*, or the useless chemical residue remaining after distillation or sublimation</note>. For you who are skilled in alchemy <note>original: "Alchymie"</note> see that the dead earth of nitric acid <note>original: "Aquafortis"</note> comes out black from the fire; and the more ingredients there are in it, the more variously the colors show themselves in the air. Sometimes they seem red, as vitriol <note>original: "Vitriall"; a term for metal sulfates</note> makes them; sometimes yellow, white, green, or blue; and sometimes mixed, as in the rainbow or the peacock's tail <note>original: "Peacocks taile"; a traditional alchemical term for a stage where many colors appear in the vessel</note>. All those colors show themselves after the death, and by the death, of the matter. For in the death of all natural things, other colors are seen which are changed from the first color into different colors, every one according to its nature and property.

<margin>The preparation of verdigris <note>original: "Verdegrease"</note> to be used in alchemy.</margin>
Now we will speak of that verdigris which is to be used in alchemy. The preparation and process of it is this:

Make very thin plates of copper; sprinkle <note>original: "strow"</note> upon them salt, sulfur, and tartar <note>potassium bitartrate residue from wine making</note>—ground and mixed together, an equal quantity of each—in a large calcining <note>heat-resistant</note> pot. Then reverberate <note>heat in a furnace where the flames are directed back onto the material</note> them for twenty-four hours with a strong fire, but in such a way that the plates of copper do not melt. Then take them out, break the pot, and set the plates with the matter that sticks to them into the air for a few days. The matter upon the plates will be turned into a beautiful verdigris. In all sharp corroding waters, "waters of exaltation" <note>purifying or strengthening solutions</note>, in cements <note>pastes used to refine metals</note>, and in the coloring of gold, this substance tinges gold and silver with a most deep color.

<margin>How *aes ustum* <note>Latin: "burnt copper"</note>, or Crocus of Copper, is to be made.</margin>
Now to turn copper into *aes ustum* <note>original: "Æs ustum"; Latin for "burnt copper"</note>, which is called the *Crocus* of Copper <note>a reddish-brown copper oxide powder</note>, the process is this:

<page-num>43</page-num>
### BOOK V. Of the Nature of Things.

Let copper be made into thin plates and be smeared over with salt made into a paste with the best vinegar. Then, let it be put into a great crucible and set in a wind furnace <note>a furnace that uses a bellows or natural draft to reach high temperatures</note>, and be burnt in a strong fire for a quarter of an hour, but in such a way that the plates do not melt. Let these plates, while red hot, be quenched in vinegar in which <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "Salt Armoniack"; ammonium chloride, used as a flux</note> is dissolved—always half an ounce in a pint of vinegar. Let the plates be made red hot again and quenched in vinegar as before, always scraping or knocking off the scales which stick to the plates after quenching into the vinegar. Do this until the copper plates are largely consumed by this means. Then, distill off the vinegar, or let it evaporate away in an open vessel and be coagulated into a very hard stone. Thus you have the best <term>Crocus of Copper</term> <note>original: "Crocus of Copper"; a red powder of copper oxide used in alchemical operations</note>, the use of which is in <term>alchemy</term>. Many make <term>Crocus of Copper</term> by extracting it with the spirit of wine or vinegar, as they do <term>Crocus Martis</term> <note>literally "Saffron of Mars"; a reddish-brown iron oxide</note>; but I recommend this way far above it.

<margin>The sublimation of Quicksilver.</margin>
Now, the mortification <note>the process of altering a substance's natural state, often by "killing" its metallic form</note> of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>mercury</note> so that it may be sublimed is done with <term>vitriol</term> <note>sulfate minerals</note> and salt, with which it is mixed and then sublimed. In this way, it becomes as hard as crystal and as white as snow. But to bring it to a precipitate, the process is this:

<margin>How to make a fixed diaphoretic precipitate.</margin>
Let it first be <term>calcined</term> <note>heated to a high temperature to remove volatile substances</note> with the best <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>literally "strong water"; nitric acid</note>. Then, distill off the <term>aqua fortis</term> and repeat this about five times until the precipitate becomes a beautiful red color. <term>Dulcify</term> <note>to wash or neutralize a substance to remove acidic or salty residues</note> this precipitate as much as you can. Lastly, pour upon it the best rectified spirit of wine <note>highly concentrated alcohol</note> you can get and distill it off eight or nine times, or until the substance stays red hot in the fire and does not fly <note>original: "fly"; to vaporize or evaporate away</note>. Then you have a <term>diaphoretic precipitated mercury</term> <note>a mercury-based preparation used to induce sweating for medicinal purposes</note>.

# Of the Nature of Things: Book V

<margin>How to make a sweet precipitate.</margin>
Furthermore, you must take note of a great secret concerning precipitated <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: Quicksilver</note>. If, after it is colored, it is "dulcified" <note>dulcified: neutralized or sweetened by washing away acidic salts</note> with water made from salt of <term>Tartar</term> <note>salt of Tartar: potassium carbonate</note>, by pouring the water upon it and distilling it off repeatedly—until the water no longer rises from the <term>precipitate</term> with a sharp or acidic taste, but is clearly sweet—then you have a precipitate as sweet as sugar or honey. This is such an excellent secret for all wounds, ulcers, and venereal disease that no <term>physician</term> could desire a better one.

<margin>And the use of it.</margin>
Besides this, it is a great comfort to despairing <term>alchemists</term>. For it augments gold and can be integrated into gold; with it, gold remains stable and of good quality. Although much pain and labor are required to create this precipitate, it will sufficiently repay you for your effort and costs. It will yield more profit than can be gained by any other art or trade whatsoever. You may well rejoice in this, therefore, and give thanks to God and to me for it.

<margin>How quicksilver may be coagulated.</margin>
Now, concerning the coagulation of <term>quicksilver</term>, I previously stated that it must be done in "sharp" <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>aqua fortis: "strong water," or nitric acid</note>, which must be drawn off by <term>distillation</term>, at which point the <term>precipitate</term> is formed.

<margin>How quicksilver may be turned to cinnabar.</margin>
But if you wish to turn <term>quicksilver</term> into <term>cinnabar</term> <note>cinnabar: a bright red mineral, mercury sulfide, used as a pigment</note>, you must first "mortify" <note>mortify: to chemically alter a metal so it loses its metallic appearance</note> it and melt it with salt and yellow <term>sulphur</term>. Bring it into a white powder, then place it in a gourd-shaped vessel and attach an <term>aludel</term> <note>aludel: a pear-shaped pot or condenser used in the sublimation process</note> or a head. Sublime it using the strongest heat <note>original: "greatest flux"</note> you can, as is the custom. In this way, the cinnabar will ascend into the <term>aludel</term> and stick as hard as the stone <term>hematite</term> <note>original: "Hæmatites"</note>.

<margin>There are two kinds of ceruse. The preparation of them.</margin>
The mortification of <term>lead</term> to turn it into <term>ceruse</term> <note>ceruse: white lead, used both as a pigment and in medicinal ointments</note> is twofold: one method is for medicine, and the other is for <term>alchemy</term>. The preparation of <term>ceruse</term> for medicine is as follows:

Hang lead plates in a glazed pot over strong wine vinegar, ensuring the pot is tightly sealed so that the vapors <note>original: "spirits"</note> do not escape. Place this pot in warm ashes, or into a furnace during the winter. After ten or fourteen days, you will always find high-quality <term>ceruse</term> <note>white lead pigment or lead carbonate</note> sticking to the plates. Scrape this off using a hare’s foot, then place the plates over the vinegar once more until you have gathered enough ceruse.

The second method of preparing <term>ceruse</term> for <term>alchemy</term> is similar to the first, except that a large quantity of the finest and purest <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>ammonium chloride salt; original: "Salt Armoniack"</note> must be dissolved in the vinegar. By this means, you will obtain a very pure and beautiful ceruse for the purification of tin and lead, and for the whitening of copper.

<margin>The preparation of <term>minium</term> from lead.</margin>
If we wish to make <term>minium</term> <note>red lead, or lead tetroxide</note> from lead, we must first calcine it <note>reduce to a powder by heat</note> with salt into a <term>calx</term> <note>a powdery metallic oxide</note>. Then, burn it in a glazed vessel, stirring it constantly with an iron rod until it turns red. This is the highest quality <term>minium</term>, and it is used in both medicine <note>original: "physick"</note> and <term>alchemy</term>. However, the variety that merchants <note>original: "mercers"</note> sell in their shops is worthless. It is made only from the dross <note>original: "ashes"</note> that remains after melting lead, which potters also use to glaze their vessels. Such minium is suitable for painting, but not for medicine or alchemy.

<margin>The <term>crocus of lead</term>.</margin>
To bring lead to a yellow color, the preparation is not unlike that of <term>minium</term>. In this case, lead must be calcined with salt and reduced to a <term>calx</term>. Afterward, it should be stirred with an iron rod in a broad basin—the kind used by mineral assayers—over a gentle coal fire. One must be careful to ensure the heat is not too intense,

<margin>How the Azure color is made of silver.</margin>
The mortification <note>In alchemy, "mortification" refers to the process of breaking down or "killing" a substance's outer form to change its state or release its inner virtues.</note> of Silver, so that the *Azure color* (or something like it) may be made from it, is as follows:

Take plates of silver and mix them with *quicksilver* <note>mercury</note>, and hang them in a glazed pot over the best *vinegar* in which "gilt-heads" <note>likely a reference to the Gilthead seabream, used in alchemical recipes for the specific organic salts or proteins it provided</note> have been first boiled, and afterward *sal ammoniac* <note>ammonium chloride</note> and calcined *tartar* <note>potassium bitartrate, or cream of tartar, that has been heated until it becomes a powdery residue</note> have been dissolved. <margin>The mortification of Gold.</margin>
The mortification of Gold, so that it may be brought into its *Arcana* <note>singular: Arcanum; the secret, essential "healing principle" or "essence" of a substance</note>—such as into a *Tincture*, *Quintessence*, *Resin*, *Crocus*, *Vitriol*, and *Sulphur*, and many other excellent *Arcana*—involves many preparations. But because we have for the most part sufficiently treated such *Arcana* in other books—such as the extraction of the *Tincture of Gold*, the *Quintessence of Gold*, the *Mercury of Gold*, the *Oil of Gold*, *Potable Gold* <note>Gold in a drinkable, liquid form, considered a universal medicine</note>, the *Resin of Gold*, the *Crocus of Gold*, and in the *Archidoxis* and elsewhere—we conceive it needless to repeat them here. 

But what *Arcana* were omitted there, we shall set down here: specifically, the *Vitriol of Gold* and *Sulphur of Gold*, which indeed are not the least among them and ought very much to cheer up every physician.

<margin>How the sulfur and the vitriol of gold are made.</margin>
Take two or three pounds of pure <term>gold</term>, which you should beat into thin plates. Suspend them over <term>boys' urine</term> mixed with grape skins and seeds <note>original: "stones of grapes"</note> in a large gourd-shaped glass vessel <note>a cucurbit</note> that is well sealed. Bury this in a hot heap of grape marc <note>the remains of grapes after pressing</note> just as they come from the press. After it has stood for fourteen days or three weeks, open it and you will find a very fine pigment, which is the <term>vitriol of gold</term> <note>a metallic salt or crust</note> sticking to the plates of <term>gold</term>. Remove this with the <term>foot of a hare</term> <note>traditionally used as a soft brush by alchemists and artists</note>, just as you have heard concerning other metals: such as the <term>Crocus Martis</term> <note>Saffron of Mars; iron oxide</note> from iron plates, the <term>vitriol of copper</term> and <term>verdigris</term> from copper plates, <term>ceruse</term> <note>white lead</note> from lead plates, and the <term>azure color</term> from silver plates. All of these are achieved through a similar process, though not with the exact same manner of preparation.

When you have enough of the <term>vitriol of gold</term>, boil it well in distilled rainwater, stirring it constantly with a spatula. Then the <term>sulfur of gold</term> is driven up to the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the water like a film of fat; skim this off with a spoon. Repeat this process with more <term>vitriol</term>. After all the <term>sulfur</term> is taken off, evaporate that rainwater until it is completely dry, and the <term>vitriol of gold</term> will remain at the bottom. You may easily dissolve this on its own by placing it on a marble slab in a moist place. The <term>diaphoretic virtue</term> <note>the power to induce sweating to expel disease</note> lies within these two <term>Arcana</term> <note>powerful medical secrets</note>: the <term>vitriol of gold</term> and the <term>sulfur of gold</term>. I shall not list their specific medicinal powers here, for we have described them at length in the book *<term>Metallic Diseases</term>* and in other volumes.

The <term>mortification of sulfur</term> <note>the process of "killing" or stripping away the external qualities of a substance to reach its essence</note>—done so that the flammable and foul-smelling oiliness may be removed and the substance brought into a stable, fixed state—is performed as follows:

### Of the Nature of Things. Book 5.

<margin>The mortification and fixation of Sulphur.</margin>
Take common yellow sulphur finely powdered, and distill *Aqua fortis* <note>original: "Aqua fortis"; literally "strong water," a powerful nitric acid used to dissolve metals</note>—which must be very sharp—through it. Do this three times. Then, take the black sulphur remaining at the bottom and sweeten <note>original: "dulcifie"; to wash away acids or salts until the substance is neutralized</note> it with distilled water until the water comes away sweet and the sulphur no longer has its stench. Next, heat this sulphur in a closed reverberatory furnace <note>A furnace that reflects heat onto the substance being processed</note>—just as you do with antimony—until it turns first white, then yellow, and finally as red as cinnabar <note>A bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide</note>. When it reaches this state, you may rejoice, for it is the beginning of your riches. This processed sulphur tints silver so deeply that it turns into the most excellent gold, and it restores the human body to perfect health. This processed and fixed <note>original: "fixed"; made non-volatile or stable when heated</note> sulphur has more power than is lawful to speak of.

<margin>The Mortification of Salts.</margin>
The mortification of all salts, and anything salty, consists of removing and distilling away their water content, oiliness, and spirit. Once these are removed, the remaining material is called the "dead earth" or *Caput Mortuum* <note>original: "Caput Mortuum"; literally "dead head," the useless residue left after distillation</note>.

<margin>The Mortification of Gems.</margin>
The mortification of gems and corals is to calcine <note>To heat to a high temperature to cause chemical change</note>, sublime <note>To heat a solid until it turns into a gas and then solidifies again</note>, and dissolve them into a liquid, like crystal. The mortification of pearls is to calcine them and dissolve them in sharp vinegar until they take the form of milk.

<margin>The Mortification of the Lodestone.</margin>
The mortification of the lodestone <note>A naturally magnetized mineral</note> is to anoint it with the oil of mercury or to place it in quicksilver <note>Liquid mercury</note>; afterward, it will not attract iron at all.

<margin>The Mortification of flints and stones.</margin>
The mortification of flints and stones is to calcine them.

<margin>The Mortification of Marcasites.</margin>
The mortification of marcasites <note>Iron sulfide minerals</note>, cachymas <note>original: "Cachyma's"; an old term for ores that have not yet reached a metallic state</note>, talc, cobalt, zinc <note>original: "Zinri"</note>, garnets <note>original: "Granuti"</note>, tin-ores <note>original: "Zunitter"</note>, bismuth <note>original: "Unifmut"</note>, and

<header>Book V. Of the Nature of Things.</header>
<sig>Gg</sig>

The sublimation <note>original: "Sublimation"; the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then cools back into a solid</note> of antimony is performed by subliming it with salt and vitriol <note>original: "Vitriall"; sulfate minerals, such as copper or iron sulfate</note>. During this, its "life"—which is a metallic spirit—ascends together with the spirit of salt. Then, let whatever remains in the bottom of the sublimation vessel be washed so that the salt may be dissolved from it. You are then left with a "dead earth" in which there is no power <note>original: "vertue"</note>.

<margin>The mortification of realgar.</margin>
The mortification of arsenics, auripigment, orpiment <note>original: "Operment"</note>, and realgar <note>types of arsenic sulfide minerals</note>, etc., occurs when they are melted with saltpeter <note>original: "Salt Nitre"</note> and turned into an oil or liquid upon a marble slab, and are thus fixed.

<margin>The mortification of excrements.</margin>
The mortification of excrements is the coagulation <note>thickening or solidifying</note> of air.

<margin>The mortification of aromatic things.</margin>
The mortification of aromatic things is the removal of their pleasant smell.

<margin>Of sweet things.</margin>
The mortification of sweet things is to sublime and distill them with corrosive substances.

<margin>Of resins.</margin>
The mortification of ambers, resins, turpentine, gums, and similar substances is to turn them into oil and varnish.

<margin>Of herbs and roots.</margin>
The mortification of herbs, roots, and similar things is to distill off their oil and water, press out their liquor with a press, and also to extract their alkali <note>the soluble salts left in the ashes of plants</note>.

<margin>Of wood.</margin>
The mortification of wood is to turn it into charcoal <note>original: "Coales"</note> and ashes.

<margin>Of bones.</margin>
The mortification of bones is their calcination <note>reducing a substance to powder by high heat</note>.

<margin>Of flesh.</margin>
The mortification of flesh and blood is the removal of the spirit of salt.

<margin>Of water.</margin>
The mortification of water is by fire; for all heat dries up and consumes water.

<margin>Of fire.</margin>
The mortification of fire is by water; for all water quenches fire and takes away its power and force.

# *Of the Resurrection of Natural things.*

<margin>
The raising again of natural things.
</margin>
**T**HE resurrection <note>original: "Resurrection"; in this context, the author refers to palingenesis, the alchemical or natural process of restoring a substance or organism from its remains or ashes</note> and renewing of natural things is not the least, but a profound and great secret in the nature of things, and is rather divine and angelic than human and natural.

I desire to be understood here with great distinction <note>meaning with clear and precise definitions</note>, and no otherwise than my opinion is, and as nature daily and manifestly shows, and experience proves, lest I should be exposed to the lies,

### On the Nature of Things. Book 6.

...and slanders of mountebanks <note>original: "Mountebankes"; a person who deceives others, especially a seller of fraudulent medicines</note>, my enemies (who interpret everything I do in the worst possible way), as if I would usurp divine power and attribute this to Nature—something she was never able to perform. Therefore, we must carefully consider that there is

<margin>Death is twofold.</margin>
a twofold death: namely, violent and natural <note>original: "voluntary"; in this context, it refers to a death occurring through natural processes rather than external force</note>. The one can be raised again, while the other cannot. Therefore, do not believe those sophists <note>original: "Sophisters"; people who use clever but false arguments</note> who say that a thing once dead or mortified <note>term: in alchemy, "to mortify" means to kill, change, or strip a substance of its original outward properties to prepare it for transformation</note> can never be raised again, and who disregard the resuscitation and restoration of things; this is certainly not their smallest error. Indeed, it is true that whatever dies or perishes by a natural death—and whatever Nature mortifies because of her predestination—God alone can raise again, or it must necessarily be done by His command. Thus, whatever Nature destroys, man cannot restore.

<margin>What things may be raised again.</margin>
But whatever man destroys, he can restore again—and once restored, he can spoil it again. Man has no further power of his own; if he should attempt to do anything more, he would be arrogating God's power to himself. He would labor in vain and be confounded unless God assisted him, or he had enough faith to move mountains. Even for such a man, this is possible, and even greater things than this, because the Scripture says it and Christ himself spoke it:

> If you have faith as small as a grain of mustard seed, and you say to this mountain, "Move from here to there," it will move; and all things will be possible for you, and nothing impossible.

But to return to our purpose: what difference is there, then, between dying and being mortified, and

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>Book VI. Of the Nature of Things.</header>

<margin>What things cannot be raised again.</margin>
Regarding which of these may be raised again, they should be understood in this way: whatever dies naturally has its end by predestination, as the will and decree <note>original: "ordination"</note> of God permits. However, it also happens that death occurs through various <note>original: "divers"</note> diseases and various accidents <note>original: "casualties"</note>, and these cases can never be raised again. Neither is there any preservative to be used against predestination and the natural term of life. But that which is mortified <note>In alchemy, "mortification" refers to a process where a substance is stripped of its outward form or "killed" so it can be transformed into a higher state.</note> may be both raised again and revived, which may be proved by various arguments that we shall set down at the end of this book.

<margin>It is one thing to die, and another thing to be mortified.</margin>
Therefore, there is a great difference between dying and being mortified; they must not be taken as one and the same thing under the same name. For in actual examples, they are very different. For look upon a man who dies a natural and predestined death: what further good or profit is there in him? Nothing; he is only cast into the earth for worms. For he is a stinking carcass <note>original: "carkasse"</note> belonging to the earth.

But the same is not to be understood of a man who is slain with a sword or dies by some similar violent death. For his whole body is profitable and good, and may be prepared into a most precious mummy <note>In early modern medicine, "mummy" (mumia) was a substance derived from human remains—often those who died violently—believed to contain a concentrated life force used for healing.</note>. For although the spirit of life went out of such a body, yet the balsam <note>original: "balsome"; a vital, life-preserving essence or fluid thought to reside in the body.</note> in which the life lies remains, which indeed, like a balsam, preserves other men's bodies.

So you may see in metals: when a metal is about to die, it begins to be overcome with rust, and as much as is thus overcome is dead. When all the metal is devoured with rust, it is all dead, and such rust can never be reduced into true metal again.

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

but it becomes only *dross*, and not a *Metal*. For it is dead, and death is in it; neither has it any more *balsam of life*, but is quite destroyed in itself.

<margin>The difference between the Calx of Metals and their ashes.</margin>
Now the *Calx* <note>original: "Calx"; a powder produced by heating a metal or mineral to high temperatures, known today as an oxide</note> of *Metals* and their *ashes* are two different things. There is a great difference between these two, for one may be revived and brought back again into a *Metal*, but the other cannot. The one is *volatile*, the other *fixed*; the one is *dyed*, the other *mortified* <note>original: "mortified"; in alchemy, this refers to the "killing" or changing of a substance's outward form to reach its inner essence</note>.

<margin>What the Ashes of Metals are.</margin>
The *Ashes* are *volatile* and cannot be brought back into a *Metal*, but only into *glass* and *dross*. But the *Calx* of *Metals* is *fixed* and may be brought back into its own *Metal*.

<margin>What the Calx of Metals is.</margin>
To understand the difference and the cause, know that in the *Ashes* there is less *fatness* <note>meaning oiliness or combustible essence</note> and more *dryness* than in the *Calx*, which indeed makes it *fluxible* <note>original: "fluxil"; able to be melted or made to flow</note>. But the *Calx* is *fatter* and *moister* than the *Ashes* and still retains its *resin* and *fluxibility*, and especially the *Salt*, which naturally is *fluxible* and makes *Metals* flow and restores them. From this it follows that the *Salt* must be extracted out of the *Ashes* of *Metals* so that they cannot be brought back into a *Metal*; then they are perfectly *volatile*. This difference and this rule are chiefly to be taken notice of, for much depends upon it.

<margin>The errors of Mountebanks concerning Gold.</margin>
For among *Mountebanks* <note>original: "Mountebankes"; traveling quacks or fraudulent sellers of medicine</note> this is no small error: instead of *Potable Gold* <note>original: "Potable Gold"; or Aurum Potabile, a legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold in liquid form</note>, the *Quintessence* of *Gold*, or the *Tincture* of *Gold*, etc., they have given to men an impure *Calx* of *Gold*, not considering the difference and the evil that follows from it. For there are two remarkable and necessary things to be taken notice of here, namely:

First, <term>gold calcined</term> <note>original: "Gold calcined"; gold reduced to a fine powder through intense heat or chemical treatment</note>, or powdered, if it is given to men, either gathers into one heap in the stomach or passes out again with the dung. In this way, it is taken in vain without doing any good. Alternatively, if it is reduced by the great inward heat of a man's body, it coats and hardens into a crust over both the bowels and the stomach. Because of this, the <term>concoction</term> <note>original: "concoction"; the archaic term for digestion</note> of the stomach is hindered, which leads to many various sicknesses and, eventually, death itself.

<margin>When metallic remedies are to be taken internally.</margin>
As you have heard regarding gold, so must it also be understood for all other metals: namely, that you should not take any <term>Metallic Arcanum</term> <note>original: "Metallike Arcanum"; a secret, powerful alchemical medicine</note> or medicine into your body unless it is first made "volatile" <note>converted into a vaporous or easily evaporated state</note> and is no longer in a solid metallic form.

<margin>How to make metals drinkable and irreversible.</margin>
Therefore, the first step and the beginning of preparing <term>Potable Gold</term> <note>original: "Potable Gold"; gold made into a liquid form for medicinal consumption</note> is this: the gold must be made volatile so that it can afterwards be dissolved in <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "spirit of Wine"; high-proof alcohol or brandy</note>. In this state, both may evaporate together and be made volatile and inseparable. Just as you prepare gold, you may also prepare drinkable silver, copper, iron, tin, lead, and mercury.

<margin>The resurrection of things is proved by the lion's whelps.</margin>
But to return to our purpose, let us prove by examples and sufficient reasons that things which are <term>mortified</term> <note>original: "mortified"; in alchemy, a substance whose original form has been destroyed or "killed" through chemical processing</note> are not truly dead or forced to remain in death. Instead, they may be restored, raised again, and revived—and this can be achieved by man, following the course of Nature. Consider lions: see how all of them are born dead and are first of all brought to life by the horrible roaring of their parents. Just as someone who is asleep is awakened by a noise, so also are lions raised. <margin>How lions are brought to life.</margin> It is not that they are merely asleep in a natural way (for those who sleep a natural sleep must necessarily wake up again on their own, which these lions do not do). For if they were not raised...

# Of the Nature of things. LIB. VI.

By this roaring, they would remain dead, and life would never be perceived in them. Therefore <note>original: "Wheerefore"</note> it is apparent that by this roaring they receive their life.

<margin>The reviving of dead flies.</margin>
So also you see in all animals which are not produced by mating <note>original: "ingendered"</note>, but proceed from decay <term>putrefaction: the process of rotting, which early modern thinkers believed could spontaneously generate new life</term>, such as flies; if they are drowned in water so that no life at all is perceived in them, and if they were left so, they would continue dead and never return to life of themselves any more. But if you cast salt upon them and put them in the warm sun, or behind a warm furnace, they will recover their former life, and this truly is a raising of them up again. For if this were not done, they would continue dead forever.

<margin>The generation of many serpents from one.</margin>
So also you see in a serpent, if he be cut into pieces, and these pieces are put into a glass jar <term>gourd glass: a vessel shaped like a gourd, used in alchemical distillation and digestion</term> and allowed to decay in horse manure <note>original: "putrefied in Horse-dung"</note>, the whole serpent will become living again in the glass, in the form either of worms or the spawn of fishes. And if those worms are, in a fitting manner, brought out of decay and nourished, many hundred serpents will be bred out of one serpent, each of which will be as big as the first; which is done only by decay. And as it is said of the serpent, so also many other animals may be raised and restored again.

<margin>The attempts of Hermes and Virgil to raise themselves after death.</margin>
According to this process, *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical father of alchemy</note> and *Virgil* <note>The Roman poet Virgil, who in the Middle Ages and Renaissance was legendary for being a great magician</note> have attempted (by the assistance of necromancy <term>negromancy: an archaic spelling of necromancy, the magic involving the dead or the conjuring of spirits</term>) to renew and raise themselves after death, and to be born again as infants; but it succeeded not according to their purpose.

<margin>The restoration of metals is twofold.</margin>
But to set aside examples and begin the practical application <note>original: "Praxis"</note> of resurrection and restoration, it is necessary and most convenient to begin with metals, because metallic bodies often resemble human bodies.

<margin>The reducing of metals into quicksilver.</margin>
We must know, therefore, that the resurrection and restoration of metals is twofold. The first reduces calcined <term>calcined: a substance reduced to powder or "ash" through high heat or chemical reaction</term> metals back into their metallic state. The other reduces metals into their "first matter"—that is, into quicksilver <note>original: "Mercury"</note>.

The process for the latter is this: Calcine a metal with common quicksilver, and put this powder <note>original: "Calx"</note> and an equal amount of quicksilver into a sublimation vessel <note>original: "Sublimatory"</note>. Let them stand until both are joined together into an amalgam. Then, sublime the mercury away from the powder, grind it again with the metallic powder, and sublime it as before. Do this repeatedly until the metallic powder melts like wax or ice over a candle; then it is completed. Put this metal in digestion <note>digestion: a chemical process of slow, continuous heating in a sealed vessel</note> for a period of time, and it will all be turned into quicksilver—that is, into its "first matter." This "mercury of metals" is indeed called the "Mercury of the Philosophers," which many alchemists have sought, but few have found. In this manner, quicksilver may be prepared out of all metals: namely, gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, and lead.

<margin>The reduction of the sublimate, and its highest purification.</margin>
Now, the raising again or restoration of coagulated mercury is done by distillation in a retort <note>retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-curving neck used for distillation</note>; for quicksilver alone rises into cold water, while the ashes of lead, copper, or sulfur are left behind.

The raising again or restoration of sublimed mercury <note>sublimed mercury: mercury that has been purified by being heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid</note> is done in boiling hot water; however, it must first be ground very small, so the hot water will separate it...

Take wood, which must first be made into charcoal, then ashes. <margin>The phlegm of wood is its Mercury, the fat is its sulphur, and the ashes are its salt.</margin>
The phlegm is **Mercury**, the fat is **Sulphur**, and the ashes are **Salt**. For whatever fumes and evaporates in the fire is **Mercury**; whatever flames and is burnt is **Sulphur**; and all ash is **Salt**.

Now, seeing that you have these three principles together, put them in horse dung <note>a common alchemical method for providing steady, gentle heat through natural fermentation</note> and let them putrefy <term>putrefie</term> <note>to rot or decompose, a necessary stage in alchemical transformation</note> for a time. If that matter is afterward placed and buried in rich <note>original: "fat"</note> soil, you shall see it live again, and a small tree will spring from it, which truly is far more excellent in its power <note>original: "vertue"</note> than the first one. This tree or wood is called, and is, **Regenerated Wood**—renewed and restored. Although it was originally wood, it was mortified <term>mortified</term> <note>in alchemy, to "kill" a substance by changing its physical form, such as burning it to ash</note>, destroyed, and reduced to charcoal, ashes, and almost to nothing; yet out of that nothing, it is remade and renewed. In the light of nature, this is truly a great mystery: namely, that a thing which had utterly lost its form and was reduced to nothing should recover its form and be made into something from nothing, eventually becoming much more excellent in power and effectiveness than it was at first.

### THE SEVENTH BOOK.

#### *Of the Transmutation of Natural things.*

**I**f we write of the *transmutation* <note>The alchemical or physical process of changing one substance into another.</note> of all *natural* things, it is fit and necessary that in the first place we show what *transmutation* is. Secondly, what are the degrees to it. Thirdly, by what *mediums* <note>original: "Medium's"; the means, instruments, or intermediate substances used to effect a change.</note>, and how it is done.

<margin>What *transmutation* is.</margin>
*Transmutation* therefore is when a thing loses its form and is so altered that it is altogether unlike its former substance and form, but assumes another

<margin>What calcination is and its various kinds.</margin>
The first degree, therefore, is <term>calcination</term> <note>the process of reducing a substance to a powder or ash through high heat</note>, under which <term>reverberation</term> <note>heating a substance in a furnace where the flame is deflected or "reverberated" onto the material</note> and <term>cementation</term> <note>a process of altering a metal by heating it while surrounded by a powdered chemical "cement"</note> are also included. Now, what is calcined is not further reverberated or cemented.

For just as in distillation the water rises from all phlegmatic <note>original: "flegmatick"; here meaning watery or dull</note> and liquid things and is separated from its body, so in sublimation, that which is spiritual is raised from what is physical <note>original: "corporeall"</note>. The volatile is refined <note>original: "subtilized"</note> from the fixed, specifically in dry things like minerals, and the pure is separated from the impure.

Besides sublimation, many beneficial properties and wonderful things are discovered in minerals. Many things are made "fixed" and become stable <note>original: "constant"</note> so as to withstand fire, and it is done in this manner:

<margin>Rules concerning Salt Armoniack <note>original: "Salt Armoniack"; sal ammoniac or ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to make metals volatile</note>.</margin>
Here also we must take notice that every metal which is brought into sublimation by Salt Armoniack may afterward in the cold, or in the air, be brought into an oil, and again be coagulated into a stone in the fire. This indeed is one of the chiefest and greatest transmutations in all natural things: namely, to transmute metal into a stone.

<margin>What Solution is, and its kinds.</margin>
The third degree is Solution, under which are to be understood dissolution and resolution. This degree most commonly follows sublimation and distillation; that is, that the matter which remains in the bottom [of the vessel] be resolved.

Now, Solution is twofold: one of cold, the other of heat; one without fire, the other in fire.

A cold dissolution dissolves all salts, all corrosive things, and all calcined <note>calcined: substances reduced to a powder or ash through the application of high heat</note> things. Whatever is of a salt and corrosive quality is by it dissolved into oil, liquor, or water. And this is [done] in a moist, cold cellar, or else in the air on a marble [slab], or in a glass. For whatever is dissolved in the cold contains an airy spirit of salt, which it often acquires and assumes in sublimation or distillation. And whatever is dissolved in the cold, or in the air, may again by the heat of the fire be coagulated into powder or a stone.

<summary>This page explores the alchemical processes of sublimation and solution, detailing how substances can change their sensory properties and how metals can be transmuted into stones. It also defines the "third degree" of alchemy, Solution, distinguishing between methods using heat and those using cold or ambient air.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, salt armoniack, transmutation, solution, distillation, calcination, alchemy, chemistry</keywords>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>BOOK VII. Of the Nature of Things.</header>
<warning>Visible bleed-through from the reverse side of the page; text remains legible.</warning>

<margin>What things a hot solution dissolves.</margin>
But a hot <term>solution</term> <note>original: "solution"—the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</note> dissolves all fatty and sulfurous things. And whatever the heat of the fire dissolves, the same does coldness congeal into a mass.

<margin>A double solution—namely, of heat and cold.</margin>
And whatever heat solidifies (coagulates), is dissolved again by cold or in the air. Here we must also understand that whatever the air or the cellar liquefies (resolves) is of an extreme dryness and has a secret, corrosive fire hidden within it. Conversely, whatever is dissolved in fire, or by its heat, possesses a sweetish coldness when removed from the fire. This is the only way that <term>solution</term> is to be understood.

<margin>What putrefaction is, and its types.</margin>
<term>Putrefaction</term> <note>original: "putrefaction"—in alchemy, the process of breaking down a substance through rot or fermentation to release its inner essence</note> is the fourth degree, which also includes <term>digestion</term> and <term>circulation</term>.

Now then, <term>putrefaction</term> is one of the principal degrees. It could rightfully have been the very first step, were it not against the true order and <term>mystery</term> <note>original: "mystery"—a hidden or divine secret of the alchemical craft</note> hidden here and known to few. For these degrees must follow one another like links in a chain or rungs on a ladder, as has been previously stated.

<margin>The previously mentioned order of degrees must be observed when making tinctures.</margin>
For if one of the links were removed, the chain would be broken and the prisoners <note>The "prisoners" refers to the spiritual essences or "tinctures" trapped within raw matter that the alchemist seeks to liberate</note> would escape and run away. Likewise, if a rung were removed from the middle of a ladder and placed at the top or bottom, the ladder would be broken; many would fall headlong, risking their bodies and lives.

So you must understand the matter this way: these degrees must follow one another in a precise order. Otherwise, the entire work of our <term>mystery</term> would be ruined, and our labor and efforts would be in vain and fruitless.

<meta>catchword: Now</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus explains the relationship between heat and cold in alchemical "solution" and introduces "putrefaction" as the essential fourth stage of transformation. He emphasizes that the sequence of these stages is immutable, comparing them to the rungs of a ladder that must be climbed in a specific order to succeed.</summary>

# Of the Nature of Things. Book VII.

<margin>The power of putrefaction.</margin>
Now putrefaction is of such efficacy that it abolishes the old nature and brings in a new one. All living things are killed in it, all dead things putrefied in it, and all dead things recover life in it.

Putrefaction removes from all corrosive spirits the sharpness of the salt and makes them mild and sweet; it changes their colors and separates the pure from the impure, placing the pure above and the impure beneath.

<margin>The nature and types of distillation.</margin>
Distillation is the first step toward the transmutation of all natural things. Under it are included ascension, lavation <note>original: "lavation"; the process of washing or cleansing a substance</note>, and fixation.

By distillation, all waters, liquors, and oils are refined. From all fatty substances, oil is extracted; from all liquors, water is drawn; and from all phlegmatic <note>original: "flegmaticke"; in alchemy, this refers to watery, dull, or non-reactive substances</note> things, water and oil are separated.

<margin>Cohobation. Fixation through distillation.</margin>
Furthermore, many things in distillation are "fixed" <note>rendered solid or non-volatile</note> by cohobation <term>the process of repeatedly distilling a liquid from its own solid residue to increase its potency</term>, especially if the substances to be fixed contain water within them, as vitriol <term>a sulfate of a metal, such as iron or copper</term> does. If vitriol is fixed, it is called *colcothar* <term>the red oxide of iron remaining after the distillation of sulfuric acid from iron sulfate</term>.

Alum, if it is fixed with its proper water, is called the "Sugar of Alum." This is also resolved into a liquor which, if it is putrefied for a month, produces a water with the sweetness of sugar. This water is of great virtue and is an excellent secret in medicine <note>original: "Physicke"</note> for extinguishing any metallic heat <note>likely referring to inflammation or chemical "burning" caused by metallic remedies</note> in man, as we have written more extensively in our book *Of Metallic Diseases*.

<summary>This page explains the transformative power of putrefaction and distillation in alchemy, describing how these processes purify substances and create medicinal remedies from minerals like vitriol and alum.</summary>
<keywords>putrefaction, distillation, alchemy, transmutation, cohobation, vitriol, colcothar, alum, medicinal chemistry</keywords>

<lang>English</lang>
<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>Book VII: Of the Nature of Things.</header>
<sig>I i 2</sig>
<meta>The previous page discussed the properties of vitriol and alum; this page continues exploring mineral fixation and chemical processes. Catchword: But.</meta>

And as you have heard of <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "Vitriall"; sulfate minerals</note> and <term>alum</term> <note>original: "Allum"; a double sulfate of aluminum used in tanning and medicine</note>, so also <term>saltpeter</term> <note>original: "Salt nitre"; potassium nitrate</note> and other watery minerals may be fixed by <term>cohobation</term>.

<margin>What cohobation is.</margin>
Now <term>cohobation</term> is when the <term>dead head</term> <note>the residue remaining after distillation, also known as caput mortuum</note> is often soaked with its own liquid, and that liquid is again drawn off by <term>distillation</term>.

<margin>The power of distillation in substances to be transmuted.</margin>
Moreover, in <term>distillation</term>, many bitter, harsh, and sharp things become as sweet as honey, sugar, or manna <note>a sweet substance obtained from certain plants</note>; and on the contrary, many sweet things, such as sugar, honey, or manna, may be made as harsh as oil of vitriol <note>sulfuric acid</note> or vinegar, or as bitter as gall or gentian <note>a bitter herb used in medicine</note>, or as sharp <note>original: "eager"</note> as a <term>corrosive</term> substance.

Many waste products <note>original: "excrementitious things"</note> lose their foul stench during <term>distillation</term>, as the smell is carried off into the water.

Many aromatic things lose their pleasant fragrance.

And as <term>sublimation</term> alters things in their quality and nature, so also does <term>distillation</term>.

<margin>What coagulation is, and its types.</margin>
<term>Coagulation</term> is the stage of being fixed. There is a twofold coagulation: one by cold and the other by heat—that is, one of the air and the other of the fire. Each of these is again divided in two, so that there are four sorts of coagulations: two of cold and two of fire.

The coagulations of fire are permanent <note>original: "fixed"</note>, while those of cold are not.

One is done only by common air, or without fire. The other is caused by the higher <term>firmament</term> of the winter stars, which together freeze waters into snow and ice.

<summary>This page defines and explains the alchemical processes of cohobation, distillation, and coagulation, illustrating how these techniques can radically transform a substance's flavor, scent, and physical state. It specifically distinguishes between the permanent "fixation" achieved through heat and the temporary solidification caused by cold or atmospheric conditions.</summary>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the definitions or initial types of coagulation (solidification), as this page continues that discussion by focusing specifically on the role of fire in the process.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
# Of the Nature of Things. Book 7.

But the coagulation caused by fire, which is the only type to be noted here, is produced by the artificial and gradual fire of the alchemists, and it is fixed and permanent. For whatever such a fire coagulates remains in that state.

The other type of coagulation is performed by the *Etnean* <note>original: Ætnean; referring to volcanic or subterranean heat, named after Mount Etna</note> and mineral fire within mountains, which the *Archeius* <term>the internal vital force or "master workman" of nature that governs physical processes</term> of the Earth governs and regulates in a manner not unlike that of the alchemists. Whatever is coagulated by such a fire is also fixed and constant, as you see in minerals and metals. These are initially a mucilaginous <term>thick, sticky, or glue-like</term> matter and are coagulated into metals, stones, flints, salts, and other bodies by the Etnean fire in the mountains, through the *Archeius* of the Earth, the operator of Nature.

<margin>What things cannot be coagulated.</margin>
We must also know that fire cannot coagulate water or moisture, but only the liquids and juices of all natural things.

Furthermore, no phlegm <term>in alchemy, the watery or tasteless distillate of a substance</term> can be coagulated unless it was originally a corporeal <note>original: corporeall; having a physical body or substance</note> matter, to which it may return through the industry of a skillful alchemist.

Likewise, any mucilaginous matter or spermatic sliminess <note>original: spermaticke flimynesse; referring to the "seed" or primordial fluid from which matter originates</note> may be coagulated by the heat of fire into a body and corporeal matter, but it can never be dissolved into water again.

And just as you have heard regarding coagulation, you should also know regarding solution <note>original: Solution; the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid</note>—namely, that no corporeal matter can be dissolved into water unless it was water in the beginning; and so it is with all minerals.

<meta>catchword: Tincture</meta>

<summary>This page explains how "fire"—both the artificial heat of alchemists and the natural volcanic heat (Etnean fire) of the Earth—solidifies liquids into permanent minerals and metals. It also establishes the alchemical principle that a substance can only be dissolved into water if it was originally water-based.</summary>
