<page-num>69</page-num>
# BOOK VII. Of the Nature of Things.
<sig>Ii 3</sig>

<margin>What Tincture is, and its kinds.</margin>
Tincture is the seventh and last degree, which concludes the entire work of our mystery for transmutation <note>the alchemical process of changing one substance into another, typically base metals into gold</note>. It makes all imperfect things perfect, transmutes them into a most excellent essence and a most perfect health, and alters them into another color.

*Tincture, therefore, is a most excellent matter, by which mineral and human bodies are tinged <note>original: "tinged" — dyed, permeated, or transformed</note>, and are changed into a better and more noble essence, and into the highest perfection and purity.*

For Tincture colors all things according to its own nature and color.

<margin>All things that are to be tinged must be fluid.</margin>
Now there are many tinctures, intended not only for metallic but also for human bodies. This is because everything which penetrates another matter—or tinges it with another color or essence so that it is no longer like it was before—may be called a tincture.

Therefore, there are many and various sorts of tinctures: namely of metals, minerals, men's bodies, waters, liquids, oils, salts, all fatty substances, and indeed of all things which may be brought to flux <term>flux: a state of melting or becoming fluid under heat</term>, whether outside the fire or in the fire.

For if a tincture is to tinge, it is necessary that the body or matter which is to be tinged be opened and remain in a state of flux; unless this were so, the tincture could not operate. It would be as if someone were to cast saffron <note>a plant used in alchemy for its intense yellow-orange pigment</note> or any other color upon coagulated water or ice; it would not tinge the ice with its color as quickly as it would if cast into liquid water. And even if it did tinge the ice, it would at the same time dissolve the ice back into water.

<summary>This page defines "Tincture" as the final stage of alchemical transformation, explaining that a substance must be in a fluid state (flux) to be successfully transformed or "tinged" by it.</summary>
<keywords>Tincture, Transmutation, Alchemy, Flux, Metallurgy, Medicine, Saffron, Seven Degrees</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmutation of metals and the application of tinctures to change their properties.</meta>

# Of the Nature of Things. Book 7.

Water. Therefore, those metals that we wish to tinge <note>original: "tinge"; here meaning to dye or transmute the color and substance of a metal</note> must first be melted in the fire and be freed from coagulation <note>solidification</note>.

And here we must understand that the stronger the fire required for their melting, the faster the tincture spreads through them. This is similar to how leaven <note>yeast or sourdough starter</note> penetrates and infects a whole mass of dough with sourness. Furthermore, the better the mass is covered and kept warm, the better it is fermented and the better the bread it produces; for fermentation is essentially the "tincture" of dough and bread.

<margin>Sediments are of a more stable nature than their liquid extracts.</margin>
We must also note that all sediment <note>original: "feces"; refers to the dregs or solid remains of a substance</note> is of a more stable <note>original: "fixed"</note> substance than its liquid form. It is also of a sharper and more penetrating nature. You can see this in the "spirit of wine" <note>alcohol/ethanol</note> which is made from the dregs of wine, and in <term>Aqua vitæ</term> <note>literally "water of life," a concentrated alcoholic spirit</note>, which is distilled out of the grounds of beer. It burns like spirit of wine and is easily inflamed like sulfur.

<margin>The preparation and nature of distilled vinegar.</margin>
Also, if another vinegar is distilled from the dregs of vinegar—just as spirit of wine is commonly distilled—it will result in a vinegar of such a fiery and sharp nature that it consumes all metals, stones, and other things, acting like <term>Aqua fortis</term> <note>literally "strong water," a historical term for nitric acid</note>.

<margin>How the tinctures of metals must be prepared.</margin>
Moreover, it is necessary that tinctures be of a stable, fluid <note>original: "fluxil"</note>, and fireproof nature. If a small piece of a red-hot metal plate is cast into them, they should immediately flow like wax without any kind of smoke at all. They penetrate metals just as oil penetrates paper or water penetrates a sponge, and they tinge <note>transmute</note> all metals into white or red—that is, into silver or gold.

<summary>This page explains the process of transmuting metals using "tinctures," comparing the chemical reaction to the way leaven affects bread or how spirits are distilled from dregs. It emphasizes that a potent tincture must be stable, fluid, and capable of penetrating solid metal to transform it into silver or gold.</summary>

# BOOK VII. On the Nature of Things.

Now these are the tinctures of metals, which must necessarily be turned into a fine powder <note>original: "Alcool"; in this context, it refers to a "pure" or "subtle" powder rather than liquid alcohol</note> by the first degree of calcination. Then, by the second degree of sublimation, they must obtain an easy and light flux <note>a state of melting or flowing easily</note>. And lastly, by the degrees of putrefaction and distillation, they are made into a fixed and incombustible tincture of an unchangeable color.

<margin>The Tinctures of Men.</margin>
Now, the tinctures of men's bodies are those that tinge <note>to infuse or transform the body's state</note> them into the highest perfection of health, so that all diseases are expelled from them and their lost strength and color are restored and renewed. These substances are, namely: gold, pearls, antimony, sulfur, vitriol <note>a sulfate mineral, usually copper or iron sulfate, used in many alchemical processes</note>, and such like, whose preparation we have taught in various ways in other books; therefore, it does not seem necessary for us to repeat them here.

<margin>On Dyeing and Painting.</margin>
We shall write no more of tinctures, seeing that every extracted color may be called a tincture, which indeed colors things with a permanent pigment—either for items that do not go into the fire, or to preserve colors fixed within the fire.

All these are in the hands and power of the dyer and painter, who prepares them according to his pleasure.

<margin>How many degrees of the alchemist's fire there are.</margin>
It is very necessary in this book to know the degrees of fire, which may be graduated and intensified <note>original: "intended"</note> in many ways. Every degree has a peculiar operation, and no one produces the same effect as another, as every expert alchemist knows through daily experience and the exercise of the art.

For one is a living and flaming fire, which reverberates and calcines <note>to reduce a substance to a powder or ash by heat</note> all bodies. Another is the fire

<page-num>72</page-num>
# Of the Nature of Things. BOOK VII.

...of a candle or lamp, which fixes all volatile bodies. Another is a fire of coals, which cements, colors, and purges metals from their dross <note>original: "drosse"; waste matter or impurities</note>, exalts gold and silver to a higher purity, whitens copper, and, in short, renews all metals.

Another fire is that of a red-hot iron plate, in which the tinctures <note>alchemical solutions used to change the color or nature of a substance</note> of metals are tested; this is also useful for other things.

Iron filings heat in one way, sand in another, ashes in another, and a <term>water bath</term> <note>original: "Balneum Mariæ," literally "Mary's Bath"; a double boiler used for gentle heating</note> in another. In these, manifold distillations, sublimations, and coagulations are performed.

A <term>vapor bath</term> <note>original: "Balneum roris," literally "bath of dew"</note> is another method, in which many solutions of physical substances are made.

Horse-dung is yet another, in which the most important processes of decay and digestion <note>in alchemy, "digestion" refers to the slow heating of a substance over a long period</note> are carried out.

<margin>The Celestial fire.</margin>
The invisible fire works in yet another way. By this, we mean the rays of the sun, especially those focused through a glass lens or crystal. This fire shows operations and effects about which the ancients wrote nothing at all. By using this fire, the three principles <note>the Paracelsian "Tria Prima": Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note> of every physical thing can be separated.

This fire is of such wonderful force that it can melt metals. All fatty and fluid substances, along with all combustible things, can be reduced to coals and ashes right on a table without any visible fire.

Therefore, now that I have proposed and explained to you the stages of the art of <term>alchemy</term> and the different degrees of alchemical fire, I will further show and declare to you the various transformations of natural things in general—starting first with metals,

->### BOOK VII. Of the Nature of Things.<-

Secondly, we will speak of stones, and thirdly of various things in general. The transmutation of metals, therefore, is a great secret in nature, and it is difficult to achieve because of many impediments and resistances. Yet it is not against nature, nor against God’s law, as many falsely claim.

<margin>The Transmutation of Metals into Silver and Gold.</margin>
But for the five lesser and impurer metals—namely Mercury (☿), Tin (♃), Lead (♄), Iron (♂), and Copper (♀)—to be transmuted into the greater, purest, and most perfect metals—namely Gold (☉) and Silver (☽)—it cannot be done without the <term>Tincture</term> <note>original: "tincture"; in alchemy, a potent substance or "medicine" capable of transforming the quality of a metal</note>, or the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term>.

Now, since we have previously revealed the secrets of Tinctures in the seven degrees and described them there, it is unnecessary that we spend any further effort on this. Rather, let us be satisfied with those things which we have written in other books concerning the transmutations of metals.

<margin>The Transmutation of Iron into Copper.</margin>
Now there are other transmutations of imperfect and impure metals, such as the transmutation of Iron (♂) into Copper (♀), which may be done in several ways.

If plates of iron are boiled in water of <term>Vitriol</term> <note>original: "vitriall"; a sulfate of a metal, used here as a corrosive or transformative agent</note>, or are bonded with calcined vitriol, or while red-hot are quenched in oil of vitriol:

In these three ways, iron may be transmuted into very good and heavy copper, which indeed flows well and has the same weight as any natural copper.

<margin>The Transmutation of Iron into Lead.</margin>
Plates of iron may be, as it were, reduced and transmuted into lead, so that the metal is as soft as natural lead, though it does not flow as easily. The process is this:

Take filings of Iron (♂) and an equal amount of the powder of <term>Borax</term> <note>a mineral used as a flux to help metals melt and join</note>, mix them well together, and put them in a crucible... <note>catchword: crucible</note>

<meta>This page continues instructions for the alchemical transmutation of metals, specifically finishing a process involving iron (Mars) and moving on to copper, lead, and tin.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>

### Of the Nature of Things. BOOK VII.

into a crucible and into a wind furnace. Let there be a strong fire, but do not let the Mars <note>original: ♂; the alchemical symbol for iron</note> melt <note>original: flow</note> immediately. Instead, let it sit as if in a cementation <term>cement</term> <note>a process of heating a solid with a powder to change its properties without melting it</note> for a whole hour. Then increase the fire so that it becomes red hot and melts. Then let the crucible cool by itself, and you shall find the metal button <term>regulus</term> <note>the metallic part of a mineral that sinks to the bottom during smelting</note> of lead at the bottom of the crucible, as soft and malleable as natural lead can be.

<margin>The Transmutation of Copper into Lead.</margin>
But to transmute Venus <note>original: ♀; the alchemical symbol for copper</note> into Saturn <note>original: ♄; the alchemical symbol for lead</note>, the process is this: First of all, treat copper with mercury sublimate and "fixed" arsenic until it becomes white—as white as the Moon <note>original: ☾; the alchemical symbol for silver</note>. Then beat it into small pieces. Take this and the powder of borax, using an equal quantity of each. First, cement it; then let it be melted into a metal button, and you will have a true button of lead.

<margin>The Transmutation of Lead into Copper.</margin>
Now, on the contrary, it is easy to transmute lead into copper; it does not require much effort, and it is done like this:
Take plates of lead and strew them over with calcined vitriol <note>sulfate minerals that have been heated to a powder</note> or *Crocus of Venus* <note>a reddish-brown oxide of copper</note>. Cement them and then melt them, and you shall see natural lead transmuted into good, heavy <note>original: ponderous</note>, and malleable copper.

<margin>A Metallic mixture like Gold.</margin>
Now, if this copper—or any other copper—is beaten into plates and strewed over with *Tutia* <term>tutty</term> <note>zinc oxide</note> or *Lapis Calaminaris* <term>calamine</term> <note>a mineral ore of zinc</note> and is cemented and finally melted, it will be transmuted into an excellent reddish *Electrum* <note>historically a gold-silver alloy, but here referring to a gold-like brass</note> similar to gold.

<margin>To make English Tin out of Lead.</margin>
If you want to turn Saturn <note>lead</note> into Jupiter <note>original: ♃; the alchemical symbol for tin</note>, make plates of lead and strew them with Salt Ammoniac <note>original: Salt Armoniack; ammonium chloride</note>. Cement and melt them as described above. By doing this, all the blackness and darkness will be taken away from the lead, and it will be as white as beautiful English tin.

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>BOOK VII. Of the Nature of Things.</header>
<sig>K k 2</sig>

Now, as you have briefly heard of some mutations of metals, know also that there are transmutations of gems, which are indeed varied and not at all alike.

<margin>Oil of sulfur transmutes gems.</margin>
For you see what great transmutations of gems lie in oil of sulfur. For any crystal may be tinted and transmuted in it, and in time be enhanced with various colors, so as to be made like a hyacinth <note>original: Hyacinth; a yellowish-red or orange zircon</note>, garnet, or ruby.

<margin>To transmute the loadstone into great strength.</margin>
Know also that the loadstone <note>A naturally magnetized piece of the mineral magnetite</note> may be transmuted into a tenfold greater power and virtue, and it is done thus:

Take the loadstone and heat it very hot in coals, but in such a way that it does not catch fire; then immediately quench it in the "oil" of *Crocus Martis* <note>original: *Crocus Martis*; an iron oxide powder used in alchemy and medicine</note> made of the best Carinthian steel <note>High-quality steel from Carinthia, a region in modern-day Austria known for its iron mines</note>, so that it may absorb as much as it can.

By this means, you shall make the loadstone so powerful that you may pull nails out of a wall with it, and do other such wonderful things with it that the common loadstone could never do.

Moreover, in the transmutation of gems, you must know that the world is placed in two degrees of tinting <note>original: Tincture</note> and coagulation <note>The process of a liquid becoming solid</note>.

<margin>To transmute the white of an egg into amber of any color.</margin>
For just as the white of an egg may be tinted with saffron and then be solidified into a beautiful yellow amber; with the smoke of a pine tree into black amber; with verdigris <note>A green pigment obtained from the weathering of copper</note> into green, like *Lapis Armenius* <note>Armenian stone; a blue-green carbonate of copper, likely azurite</note>; with green juice into amber, like the emerald; and with the azure stone <note>Lapis lazuli</note> into blue amber, like a sapphire:

# Of the Nature of Things. Book VII.

...with the wood called red wood, into red, like a *garnet* <note>original: "Granat"</note> or *ruby*; with a purple color, like an *amethyst*; with *ceruse* <note>white lead pigment</note>, like *alabaster*.

<margin>How counterfeit pearls are made.</margin>
So also may *pearls* be made like true *pearls* in form, so that for splendor and beauty they can hardly be discerned from the true ones. And they are made thus:

Cleanse the white of *eggs* through a sponge as purely as may be, then mingle with it the fairest white *talc*, or *mother of pearl*, or *mercury* coagulated with *tin* and brought into an *alcohol* <note>In alchemy, "alcohol" often referred to a very fine, purified powder rather than a liquid spirit.</note>. Then grind them all together on a *marble* slab so that they become a thick *amalgam*, which must be dried in the *sun* or behind a furnace until it is like *cheese* or *liver*.

Then from this mass, make *pearls* as big as you wish. Hang them upon the bristles of a *hog*, and being thus bored through, dry them like *amber*; then you have finished them.

If they are not beautiful enough, anoint them over with the white of an *egg* and dry them again, and they will be most goodly pearls, in form like the natural, but not in virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to the inherent medicinal or magical properties believed to reside in natural stones.</note>.

In the like manner are *corals* made, with which men endeavor to deceive one another as they do with *pearls*. The process is this:

<margin>How counterfeit corals may be made.</margin>
Take **cinnabar** <note>original: "Cinnabar"; a bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide</note>, grind it on a marble slab with the white of an egg for the space of an hour. Then dry it, just as potters do their clay. Afterward, shape it into whatever form you please. Then dry them as much as possible, coat them over with the white of an egg—just as you did with the pearls—and dry them by themselves again.

In this way, you shall have **coral** similar to the natural kind in form, but not in virtue <note>original: "vertue"; refers to the inherent medicinal or supernatural properties believed to reside in natural materials</note>.

<margin>A golden or silver varnish.</margin>
You must also know that the white of an egg can, by itself, be coagulated <note>thickened or solidified</note> into a very clear **varnish**. During this coagulation process, silver or gold leaf may be sprinkled into it.

There are also many other various **transmutations** <note>transformations of one substance into another</note> of natural things. I will briefly set down and describe those which I know and have experienced.

<margin>How wood is turned into stone.</margin>
First of all, know that any wood, if it is placed for a certain time into water containing **halite** <note>original: "Salt Gemme"; naturally occurring rock salt</note>, is transformed—to great wonder—into a stone.

<margin>Coals from stones.</margin>
Also, stones in the **volcanic fire** <note>original: "Ætnean fire," referring to the fires of Mount Etna</note> are transmuted into **coals**, which are called stony coals <note>likely a reference to mineral coal, as opposed to charcoal made from wood</note>.

<margin>Glue from skins.</margin>
**Glue** is also boiled out of animal skins.

<margin>Paper from linen cloth.</margin>
**Paper** is made from linen cloth.

<margin>Silk from flax.</margin>
**Silk** is made from flax that has been boiled in a harsh lye <note>original: "Lie"; a strongly alkaline solution</note> made from wood ashes.

<margin>Feathers may be spun.</margin>
Also, the feathery parts pulled off from quills and boiled in that lye may be spun and woven like cotton.

### *Of the Separation of Natural Things.*

<margin>Chaos as the matter of the world.</margin>

# Of the Nature of things. BOOK VIII.

Water is the cold part, and Earth is the dry part of the greater world <note>The "greater world" refers to the macrocosm or the universe as a whole.</note>.

<margin>The type of separation discussed here.</margin>
To briefly understand the purpose of this eighth book, you must know that I do not intend to treat the elements of all natural things here. I have already sufficiently discussed those secrets <note>original: "Arcana"</note> in the *Archidoxis* <note>A major work by Paracelsus concerning the "archidoxes" or primary teachings of medicine and alchemy.</note> regarding the separation of natural things. In that work, I explained how every thing is set apart and distinctly divided materially and substantially—that is, when two, three, four, or more things are mixed into one body and yet appear as only one substance. It often happens that the physical matter of such a thing cannot be identified or given a specific name until a separation is performed. Then, sometimes two, three, four, five, or more substances emerge from a single matter, as is shown by daily experience in the **Art of Alchemy.**

<margin>The nature of Electrum.</margin>
For example, consider *electrum* <note>In an alchemical context, electrum usually refers to a specific alloy of the seven primary metals, believed to have magical or medicinal properties.</note>. In itself, it is not a single metal, yet it hides all metals within one "metal." If it is analyzed <note>original: "anatomized"</note> and separated through the industry of alchemy, all seven metals—namely *gold, silver, copper, tin, lead, iron, and quicksilver* <note>mercury</note>—emerge from it in a pure and perfect state.

<margin>Defining separation.</margin>
To understand what separation is, note that it is nothing other than the severing of one thing from another, whether there are two, three, four, or more substances mixed together. I am speaking of the separation of the three principles—such as Mercury, Sulphur, and Salt—and the extraction of the pure from the impure, or the pure, excellent spirit and quintessence <term>the "fifth essence," or the most concentrated and purified form of a substance</term> from a

<margin>The different kinds of separation.</margin>
Now, there are many kinds of separation, many of which are unknown to us. Those which we have experienced in elementary, dissolvable natural things shall be described here according to their types.

<margin>The separation of the Microcosm.</margin>
The first separation of which we speak must begin with man, because he is the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>original: Microcosme; literally "small world," the Renaissance idea that man reflects the structure of the entire universe.</note>, or little world, for whose sake the <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>The original text repeats "Microcosme," but contextually refers to the "greater world," or Macrocosm.</note> or greater world was made—namely, that he might be its separator.

<margin>The body of man is twofold after death.</margin>
Now, the separation of the Microcosm begins at death. For in death, the two bodies of man are separated from one another: namely, his celestial and terrestrial bodies—that is, the sacramental and the elementary. One of these ascends on high like an eagle; the other falls downward to the earth like lead.

<margin>What the elementary body is.</margin>
The elementary body is putrefied <note>original: putrefied; rotted or decayed.</note>, consumed, and becomes a putrid, stinking carcass. Once buried in the earth, it never comes forth or appears again.

<margin>What a sacramental body is.</margin>
But the sacramental—that is, the sidereal <note>original: Syderiall; relating to the stars or the "astral body."</note> or celestial—body is never putrefied or buried; neither does it occupy any physical space. This body appears to men and is seen even after death.

*Hence ghosts, visions, and supernatural apparitions.*

Hence, by the ancient magicians, the <term>Kabbalistic</term> <note>original: Cabalisticall; here referring to the mystical study of spirits and the spiritual body.</note> Art...

# Of the Nature of Things. BOOK VIII.

Art took its beginning, of which we shall treat more at length in the books of the Kabbalah <note>original: "Cabalie"; refers here to the mystical and alchemical traditions regarding the hidden nature of reality</note>.

After this separation is made, then after the death of the man, three substances—namely, body, soul, and spirit—are divided from one another. Every one goes to its own place, specifically to its own fountain from which it had its origin: the body to the Earth, to the first matter of the elements; the soul into the first matter of sacraments; and lastly, the spirit into the first matter of the airy chaos.

<margin>The separation of the Macrocosm <note>the universe or "great world"</note>.</margin>
What has now been spoken concerning the separation of the Macrocosm, the same may also be understood regarding the greater world which the great ocean has divided into three parts. Thus, the universal world is severed into three parts—namely, *Europe, Asia, and Africa*—which separation is a certain representation of the three principles that can be separated from any earthly <note>original: "Terrene"</note> or elementary thing. These three principles are **Mercury, Sulphur, and Salt**, from which three the world was made and composed.

<margin>Three parts of the world.</margin>
<margin>The separation of metals.</margin>
The next thing to be known is the separation of metals from their mountains—that is, the separation of metals and minerals.

By virtue of this separation, many things come forth out of one matter. As you see, out of minerals come forth: *the dross of metals, glass, sand, pyrites <note>original: "Piipitis"</note>, marcasite, garnet <note>original: "Granatus"</note>, cobalt <note>original: "Cobaltum"</note>, talc, <term>cachimia</term> <note>original: "Cachinna"; an alchemical term for an ore that has not yet become a "perfect" metal</note>, zinc <note>original: "Zinetum"</note>, bismuth <note>original: "Bisemutum"</note>, antimony, litharge <note>lead oxide</note>, sulphur, vitriol <note>sulfate minerals</note>, verdigris <note>a green pigment formed on copper</note>, chrysocolla <note>a copper-bearing mineral</note>, the azure stone <note>lapis lazuli</note>, orpiment <note>original: "Auripigmentum"; an arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, arsenic, realgar <note>an arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide</note>, iron clay, <term>spath</term> <note>original: "Spathus"; a mineral such as feldspar or fluorspar</note>, gypsum <note>original: "Gyphus"</note>, ochre*, and many more like these, as also the waters, oils, and re-

...fines, <term>calxes</term><note>original: calces; powders or oxides produced by heating a metal or mineral to high temperatures</note>, *mercury, sulfur, and salt, etc.*

<margin>On vegetables.</margin>
*Vegetables*, in their separation, yield *waters, oils, juices, resins, gums, <term>electuaries</term><note>pastes made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</note>, powders, ashes, mercury, sulfur, and salt.*

<margin>On animals.</margin>
*Animals*, in their separation, yield *water, blood, flesh, fat, bones, skin, body, hair, mercury, sulfur, and salt.*

<margin>What a good separator ought to be.</margin>
He, therefore, who boasts of being able to separate all natural things in this manner must necessarily have long experience and perfect knowledge of all natural things.

Moreover, he must be a skillful and well-practiced <term>alchemist</term>, so that he may know what is combustible and what is not; what is <term>fixed</term><note>stable and resistant to change when heated</note> and what is not; what will flow and what will not; and which things are heavier than others. Also, he must be experienced in the natural color, smell, acidity, harshness, sourness, bitterness, sweetness, the degree, <term>complexion</term><note>the specific balance of qualities or "humors" within a substance</note>, and quality of every thing.

<margin>The degrees of separation: what and how many they are.</margin>
He must also know the stages of separation, such as **distillation**, **resolution**, **putrefaction**, **extraction**, **calcination**, **reverberation**, **sublimation**, **reduction**, **coagulation**, **powdering**, and **washing**.

<margin>What distillation separates.</margin>
By **distillation**, water and oil are separated from all physical things.

<margin>What resolution separates.</margin>
By **resolution**<note>the process of dissolving or breaking a compound into its parts</note>, metals are separated from minerals, and one metal from another; salt is separated from the other principles; and fat and light substances are separated from heavy ones.

<margin>Putrefaction.</margin>
By **putrefaction**, fat is separated from lean, the pure from the impure, and the decayed from the fresh.

<margin>Extraction.</margin>
By **extraction**, the pure is separated from the impure, the spirit and <term>quintessence</term><note>the "fifth essence" or most concentrated, medicinal core of a substance</note> from the body, and the thin from the thick.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Nature of Things," focusing on the specific chemical and alchemical processes used to separate and purify substances.</meta>

# Of the Nature of Things. Book VIII.

<margin>Calcination:</margin>
By <term>Calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to remove volatile components or to oxidize it into a powder.</note> is separated watery moisture, fat, natural heat, odor, and whatever else is combustible.

<margin>Reverberation.</margin>
By <term>Reverberation</term> <note>A process where a substance is heated in a furnace that reflects the flame and heat back down onto the material.</note> is separated color, odor, what is combustible, all humidity, wateriness <note>original: aquosity</note>, fat, and whatever is inconstant or fluid <note>original: fluxil</note> in anything, etc.

<margin>Sublimation.</margin>
By <term>Sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a solid until it turns into a gas and then cools back into a solid, used to purify substances.</note> is separated the fixed from the volatile, the spiritual from the corporeal, the pure from the impure, <term>Sulphur</term> from <term>Salt</term>, <term>Mercury</term> from <term>Salt</term>, etc. <note>Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt were considered the "Three Principles" or Tria Prima that made up all matter in Paracelsian alchemy.</note>

<margin>Reduction.</margin>
By <term>Reduction</term> <note>The process of restoring a metal to its metallic state from an ore or oxide.</note> is separated what is fluid <note>original: fluxil</note> from what is solid, a metal from its mineral, and one metal from another, a metal from its dross, and fat from what is not fat.

<margin>Coagulation.</margin>
By <term>Coagulation</term> <note>The process of thickening or solidifying a liquid.</note> is separated wateriness from humidity, and water from Earth.

<margin>Powdering.</margin>
By <term>Powdering</term> are separated powder and sand, ashes, and <term>Calx</term> <note>The dry, powdery residue (usually a metallic oxide) left after a substance has been heated or "calcined."</note>. Mineral, vegetable, and animal are separated one from the other, and all powders which are of an unequal weight are separated, as by winnowing chaff from grain <note>original: corne</note>.

<margin>Washing.</margin>
By washing are separated ashes and sand, a mineral from its metal, that which is heavy from what is light, a vegetable and animal from what is mineral, <term>Sulphur</term> from <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Salt</term>, and <term>Salt</term> from <term>Mercury</term>.

<margin>The preparation of metal is multifaceted.</margin>
But moving past the theory, we will now proceed to the practice and come to particulars.

You must therefore note that the separation of metals is the first by right, and we shall therefore treat it according to this method and manner.

<summary>This page defines seven primary alchemical processes—calcination, reverberation, sublimation, reduction, coagulation, powdering, and washing—and explains how each method is used to separate and purify different elements of matter.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, metallurgy, calcination, sublimation, Tria Prima, chemical separation, Paracelsian chemistry, reduction, coagulation</keywords>

# Of the Separation of Metals
## *from their Ores.*

<margin>By fluxing powders.</margin>
**T**he separation of metals from their ores <note>original: Mines; in this period, the term "mine" often referred to the mineral ore itself</note> is done in various ways, namely by boiling and melting with fluxing powders—such as alkali salt, litharge <note>original: Litharge; lead monoxide used in the refining process</note>, fluxing salt, the dross of glass, salt gem <note>original: Salt Gemme; rock salt</note>, saltpeter, etc. Let them be put in a crucible and melted in a furnace; in this way, the metal or *regulus* <note>original: regulus; the metallic mass that settles at the bottom of a crucible during smelting</note> will fall to the bottom of the crucible, but the other matter will float on top and become dross <note>original: droffe; waste material or slag</note>. You shall heat this metallic *regulus* in a reverberatory furnace <note>original: reverberating furnace; a furnace that reflects or "reverberates" heat onto the material from the roof</note> until all the metal becomes pure and freed from all its dross. By this means, the metal is well digested and, as I might say, refined from all its impurities.

Often, one ore contains more than one metal, such as copper and silver, copper and gold, lead and silver, or tin and silver. You will know it by this: if the metallic *regulus*, after being processed in a melting pot in the proper manner, is sufficiently dissolved. For within it, all base <note>original: imperfect</note> metals are separated—such as iron, copper, tin, and lead—and by adding a double quantity of lead to the *regulus*, they all evaporate into smoke <note>original: goe into a fume; a description of cupellation, where base metals oxidize and vanish or are absorbed</note>, and only fine silver or gold is left behind in the pot.

<margin>By Aqua fortis.</margin>
Also, two or three metals mixed together may be separated in *aqua fortis* <note>original: Aqua fortis; literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid</note>, and extracted from each other...

<sig>Ll 3</sig>
<meta>Catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This page describes metallurgical techniques for extracting and refining metals from mineral ores using fluxing powders and reverberatory furnaces. It explains how to separate precious metals like gold and silver from base metals through cupellation with lead and the use of nitric acid.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, smelting, flux, regulus, dross, silver, gold, aqua fortis, cupellation, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues a discussion on the chemical and alchemical methods used to separate mixed metals.</meta>

# Of the Nature of Things, Book 8

...the other. If two metals, or even one, are dissolved <note>original: "resolved"</note>, the other will fall to the bottom like sand and be precipitated; they can then be separated in this way. Metals may also be separated by melting <note>original: "flowing"</note> using the following process: melt the metals, and while they are in flux, cast <margin>Using sulfur.</margin> into them the best fine sulfur powder <note>original: "flower of Sulphur"; a fine powder produced by heating sulfur into a vapor and cooling it</note> you can get—specifically, one ounce for every pound of metal. Let it burn, and by that means it will draw the lightest metal to the top while the heavy metal falls to the bottom. Then, let them sit together until they are cold. In this single metallic mass <term>Regulus: the metallic heart or mass that settles at the bottom of a crucible during smelting</term>, two metals are found. They are not <margin>A wonderful power of sulfur in separation.</margin> mixed together as they were before, but are separated from one another by the sulfur, acting as a partition just as oil divides two waters so that they cannot be joined or mixed. Sulfur, therefore, is a remarkable *secret of nature* <note>original: "Arcanum"; a deep mystery or powerful alchemical secret</note> worthy of great praise.

<margin>Using mercury.</margin> Fixed metals, such as gold and silver—because they cannot easily be extracted with fire or nitric acid <note>original: "Aqua fortis"; literally "strong water," a corrosive acid used to dissolve metals</note>—must be amalgamated with mercury <note>original: "Quicksilver"</note> and so separated and extracted. The mercury is afterwards removed and separated from the oxide <term>Calx: the powdery residue or metal oxide left after a metal has been roasted or burned</term> of the metals (the gold and silver) through a certain degree of distillation.

In this same way, other metals besides gold and silver—such as copper, iron, tin, lead, and so on—may be treated. This also applies to whatever is prepared from them, such as red electrum <note>an alloy of gold and silver</note>, white magnesia, orichalcum <note>original: "Aurichalcum"; a gold-colored brass alloy used in the classical world</note>, calcined lead, latten <note>original: "Laton"; a brass-like alloy</note>, cauldron brass, and whatever metals of this kind are transmuted. These can be processed with mercury, but they must first be powdered, extracted, and separated from any foreign <note>original: "heterogeneous"</note> materials. For the nature and condition of mercury is this: it will unite and amalgamate with metals, but it will do so with one sooner than another, depending on whether the metal has a greater or lesser affinity for it.

<summary>This page describes alchemical techniques for separating mixed metals using sulfur to create layers in a melt or using mercury (quicksilver) to form amalgams. These processes are used to isolate precious metals like gold and silver from base metals or impurities.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, alchemy, sulfur, mercury, amalgamation, distillation, gold, silver, regulus, arcanum</keywords>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>BOOK VIII. Of the Nature of things.</header>

In this consideration, fine **gold** is the foremost, followed by fine **silver**, then **lead**, then **tin**, then **copper**, and finally **iron**.

So among transmuted metals, the first is "part with part" <note>likely referring to a 50/50 alloy</note>, then **ash**-colored **lead**, then **latten** <note>original: "Laton"; a brass-like alloy of copper and zinc</note>, then **cauldron brass**, then red, and finally the newest white. Although for the first stage, **mercury** should be amalgamated with no more than one metal at a time, that **amalgam** must be strained firmly through **leather** or **cotton cloth**. By this means, nothing but the **quicksilver** (mercury) will pass through the **leather** or **cloth**. The metal it attracted remains in the leather or cloth like a <term>calx</term> <note>original: "Calx"; here referring to a fine powder or residue left after filtration</note>, which you may afterwards reduce into a metallic body by melting it with **alkali salt** <note>original: "salt Alcali"</note> or some other salt.

Now, by this **art** (skill), **quicksilver** is separated from all kinds of metals much faster than by **distillation**, etc.

By this process using **mercury**, all metals—after <term>calcination</term> <note>original: "calcination"; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical state</note> and grinding into powder—can be extracted and separated from one another by a skillful and industrious **alchemist**.

In the same manner, and quite easily, **tin** and **lead** may be separated from **copper** (or **copper** vessels), and from **iron** and **steel** coated with **tin**, without using fire or water, but by using the **amalgam** of **quicksilver** alone.

Likewise, **beaten gold**, **leaf gold**, or **silver**—as well as any other metal that has been beaten or ground and then written or applied with a <term>pencil</term> <note>original: "pencill"; in this context, a small, fine brush used for painting or gilding</note> or quill onto cloth, parchment, paper, leather, wood, stone, or any other surface—can be dissolved by **quicksilver**. This is done so that the **quicksilver** may afterward be separated from those metals again.

It highlights the efficiency of filtration through leather or cloth over traditional distillation methods.</summary>

<margin>By corrosive waters.</margin>
Now, the separation of metals using *nitric acid* <note>original: *Aqua fortis*</note>, *nitro-hydrochloric acid* <note>original: *Aqua Regis*, literally "Royal Water," the only acid that can dissolve gold</note>, and similar corrosive waters, is done in this manner.

Let a metal that is mixed and joined with another be taken and beaten into thin plates or ground into a powder. Put it into a separating vessel and pour enough common nitric acid over it. Let them sit and soak <note>original: macerated</note> until all the metal is dissolved into a clear liquid. If it is silver and contains any gold, all the silver will be dissolved into the liquid, and the gold will also be calcined <note>reduced to a powder through the action of the acid</note> and settle at the bottom like black sand. In this way, gold and silver are separated. If you then wish to separate the silver from the nitric acid without distillation, put a plate of copper into the liquid. The silver will immediately settle at the bottom of the liquid like snow, and the copper plate will gradually be consumed.

The separation of silver and copper by common nitric acid is done in this manner. Let the copper which contains silver, or the silver which contains copper, be reduced to thin plates or powder and put into a glass vessel. Pour enough common nitric acid over it; by this means, the silver will be calcined and settle to the bottom like white chalk, but the copper will be dissolved and turned into a clear liquid. If this liquid, together with the dissolved copper, is separated from the silver residue <note>original: calx</note> using a glass funnel and poured into another glass, then the copper dissolved in the water may be precipitated <note>OCR note: likely "precipitated"</note> with common water, rainwater, or any other water, so that it will settle to the bottom of the glass like sand.

Now, for the separation of hidden gold from any metal,

is by the degree of extraction in *Aqua Regis* <note>original: "Aqua Regis," literally "royal water"; a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids so named because it can dissolve "noble" metals like gold</note>. For this kind of water will attempt to dissolve no metal, but only pure, fine gold, and so forth.

The same *Aqua Regis* also separates fine gold from gilded plate. For if that plate is washed over with it, the gold will be separated from it, etc.

<margin>By the degree of reverberation <note>A process of intense heating in a furnace where the flames are directed or "reverberated" back onto the material</note>,</margin>
Moreover, two metals mixed together may also be separated from one another with cement <note>A technical term in early metallurgy for a powder or paste used in a heating process to refine or part metals</note> by the degree of reverberation, especially if they are not at the same degree of fixation <note>In alchemy, "fixation" refers to the property of a substance to resist change or evaporation when exposed to heat</note>, such as iron and copper. For those metals which are only slightly fixed, such as tin and lead, are entirely consumed by the degree of reverberation in cement. For the more a metal is fixed, the less it is consumed by cement.

You must know, therefore, that fine gold is the most fixed and perfect metal, which can be destroyed or consumed by no cement. Next to this is fine silver. If, then, gold and silver are mixed together in one body (which is often called "part with part"), or if silver contains gold, or gold contains silver: I say that if these mixtures are reverberated in cement, then the gold remains entire and not at all injured, but the silver is consumed by the cement and thus extracted from the fine gold. In the same way, copper is extracted from silver, iron and tin from copper, and iron or lead from tin, and so forth.

## *Of the Separation of Minerals.*

**A**fter we have explained (as we have done so far) the separation of metals from their earth and matter, as well as the separation of one metal from another, and how it is done—having covered this as briefly as possible—it will next be necessary to treat those things from which metals grow and are generated. These are the three principles: Mercury, Sulphur, and Salt, as well as all minerals in which the "first being" <note>original: "first being"; in alchemy, the *prima ens* or the primordial, spiritual essence of a substance before it has fully materialized</note> of metals (that is, the spirit of metals) is found. 

This is evident in **marcasites** <note>iron pyrites or similar metallic-looking sulfides</note>, **garnets**, **cachimia** <note>original: "Cachymiies"; an alchemical term for an ore that is metallic but not yet a true metal, often considered an "imperfect" state of matter</note>, **red talc**, the **azure stone** <note>lapis lazuli</note>, and the like, in which the first being of **gold** is found through the degree of sublimation. 

In the same way, the first being of **silver** is found in **white marcasite**, **white talc**, **orpiment** <note>original: "Auripigmentum"; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, **arsenic**, **litharge** <note>lead monoxide</note>, etc. 

In **cobalt**, **zinc** <note>original: "Zinetus"</note>, etc., the first being of **iron** is found. 

In **zinc**, **vitriol** <note>sulfate minerals</note>, **verdigris**, etc., the first being of **copper** is found. 

In **zinc**, **bismuth** <note>original: "Biſemutus"</note>, etc., the first being of **tin** is found. 

In **antimony**, **minium** <note>red lead</note>, etc., the first being of **lead** is found. 

In **cinnabar**, the first being of **quicksilver** <note>mercury</note> is found.

Concerning this "first beginning," you must know that it is a volatile spirit, still existing in a state of volatility, like an infant lying in the womb of its **mother**, which

For all minerals follow one and the same process through all degrees, as the <term>art of alchemy</term> teaches, etc.

# Of the Nature of Things. BOOK 8.

## Of the Separation of Vegetables.

<margin>How plants are separated.</margin>
The separation of those things which grow out of the Earth and are combustible—such as fruits, herbs, flowers, leaves, grass, roots, wood, etc.—is performed in many ways.

For first, by distillation, the phlegm <note>original: "flegm"; the watery or tasteless component of a substance</note> is separated from them, then the Mercury, then the Oil, then the resin <note>original: "Re-fine"; likely a transcription error for "Resine"</note>, then the Sulfur, and lastly the Salt.

All these separations being made according to the Spagyric <note>original: "Spagiricall"; alchemy focused on medicine, specifically the separation and recombination of natural elements</note> art, many notable and excellent medicines come from there, which are to be used both within and without the body.

But now, seeing that idleness is so much in demand among physicians, and all labor and study is turned only to arrogance, truly I do not wonder that all such preparations are everywhere neglected. Coals are sold at so low a price that if blacksmiths could be as easily without coals in forging and working their metals as physicians are in preparing their medicines, certainly charcoal burners <note>original: "Colliers"</note> would long since have been brought to extreme want.

<margin>A rebuke of physicians.</margin>
In the meantime, I will give to Spagyric physicians their due praise. For they are not given to idleness and sloth, nor do they go about in proud clothing or plush and velvet garments—often showing off the rings upon their fingers, or wearing swords with silver hilts by their sides, or fine and showy <note>original: "gay"</note> gloves upon their hands—but they diligently follow their labors, sweating through whole nights and days by their furnaces.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process of separating plants into their core components (phlegm, mercury, oil, resin, sulfur, and salt) to create medicine. The author also sharply contrasts hardworking alchemical physicians with the lazy, fashion-obsessed mainstream doctors of the era.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, distillation, Spagyric art, plant medicine, Paracelsus, medical ethics, charcoal burners, chemical components</keywords>

### BOOK 8: Of the Nature of Things.

<margin>The commendation of chemists, and how they differ from other physicians.</margin>
These do not spend their time outdoors for recreation but take delight in their laboratory. They wear leather garments with a pouch and an apron with which they wipe their hands. They put their fingers into coals, clay, and dung, rather than wearing gold rings. They are sooty and black, like smiths or colliers <note>original: "Colliers"; workers who made or sold charcoal, or coal miners</note>, and do not pride themselves on clean and beautiful faces. They are not talkative when they visit the sick, nor do they praise their medicines; for they well know that the craftsman <note>original: "Artificer"</note> must not praise his work, but the work must praise the craftsman, and that the sick cannot be cured with fine words.

<margin>How many degrees of alchemy there are.</margin>
Therefore, setting aside all these kinds of vanities, they delight in being busy with the fire and learning the degrees of the science of alchemy <note>original: "Alchymie"</note>. Within this order are *distillation, resolution, putrefaction, extraction, calcination, reverberation, sublimation, fixation, separation, reduction, coagulation, tincture, etc.*

But how these separations may be performed through the help of distinct stages according to the art of alchemy has been discussed in general already. Therefore, it is unnecessary to repeat it here.

But to proceed to specifics and briefly explain the practice, you must know that *water, spirit, liquor, oil, etc.* cannot be separated by the same process of distillation from flowers, herbs, seeds, leaves, roots, trees, fruits, and woods.

For herbs require one process, flowers another, seeds another, leaves another, roots another, and trees another—

...another, the stalks another, the fruit another, and woods another.

<margin>The degrees of fire in distillations</margin>
And in this stage of distillation, there are also four distinct degrees of fire to be considered.

The first degree of fire in distillation is *Balneum Mariae* <note>original: *Balneum Mariæ*; literally "Mary’s Bath," now known as a bain-marie or water bath</note>; this distillation is performed in water.

The second degree of fire is distillation performed in ashes <note>The use of hot ashes provided a gentler, more even heat than a direct flame</note>.

The third is in sand <note>A sand bath allowed for higher, more sustained temperatures than water or ashes</note>.

The fourth is in an open flame <note>original: "naked Fire"</note>. Distillation may also be achieved using *aqua fortis* <note>Nitric acid; used here as a chemical solvent</note> and other "sharp" <note>referring to corrosive or acidic liquids</note> waters.

<margin>Which degree of fire to use for each vegetable substance.</margin>
Herbs, flowers, seeds, and similar items belong to the first degree of fire.

Leaves, fruits, etc., belong to the second.

Roots and the boughs of trees belong to the third.

Wood and similar substances belong to the fourth.

Note that each of these must be ground small and bruised before they are put into the still <term>An apparatus used for distilling liquids by heating and cooling</term>.

This concludes the discussion concerning the distillation of waters from vegetable substances.

Regarding the separation and distillation of oils, the process is the same as for water, except that some must be distilled *per descensum* <note>Latin: "by descent"; a method where the vapor is forced downward into a receiver, often used for heavy oils that do not easily rise</note>. Since these cannot ascend like water, the process must be adjusted in those cases.

However, liquors are not separated through distillation like waters or oils, but are squeezed <note>original: "expressed"</note> from their physical <note>original: "corporeall"</note> substances using a press.

<header>BOOK 8. Of the Nature of Things.</header>

And here we must know that there are some oils that are pressed out and separated with a press in the same way as liquids are. This is done so that they do not acquire a bad smell from the fire, as they otherwise would.

Included in this category are the oils of almonds, nuts, hard-boiled eggs, and the like.

We must also note that all oils, if they are prepared and coagulated according to the <term>Spagyric Art</term> <note>original: "Spagiricall Art"; the alchemical practice of separating a substance into its components and then recombining them</note>, yield a kind of varnish, gum, amber, or resin, which may also be called sulfur. Whatever remains in the bottom of the still may be calcined <note>burned to a fine powder at high heat</note> and reduced to ashes. From these ashes, the <term>alkali</term> <note>original: "Alcali"; water-soluble salts, such as potash, extracted from wood or plant ashes</note> can be extracted and separated using only warm water.

The ashes left behind are called the "dead earth," from which nothing else can ever be extracted.

## Of the Separation of Animals

It is necessary that <term>anatomy</term> precedes the separation of animals, so that the blood may be set apart, the flesh apart, the bones apart, the skin apart, the bowels apart, the tendons apart, and so on. After this, each of these must be separated individually with the help of the <term>Spagyric Art</term>.

<margin>Four stages of the separation of animal substances: Mummy, etc.</margin>
Therefore, the separations discussed here are primarily of four types. The first draws out a watery and phlegmatic moisture from the blood. For when blood is prepared in this way—according to the process shown in the *Book of Conservations*—it produces a most excellent mummy <note>original: "Mummie"; in early modern medicine, this referred to a substance derived from human remains, believed to have potent healing properties.</note>. It is so excellent a *specific* <note>original: "Specificum"; a medicine intended to treat a particular condition or body part.</note> that any fresh wound may be cured and healed <note>original: "consolidated"</note> within twenty-four hours with only a single dressing.

<margin>Balsam, etc.</margin>
The second is the separation of fat from flesh. Once separated from human flesh, this fat becomes a most excellent balsam for relieving the pains of gout, cramps, and similar ailments, provided the affected part is anointed with it while warm. It also helps the tendons of the hands or feet if they are contracted <note>original: "drawn together"</note>, provided they are anointed with it daily. It also cures scabies <note>original: "the scab"</note> and all kinds of leprosy.

Therefore, it is the most important surgical <note>original: "Chirurgical"</note> specific, and it is most beneficial in all cases such as wounds and the like.

The third is the separation of watery and phlegmatic moisture, along with the fat extracted from bones. If these two are carefully separated from human bones through the art of alchemy by means of distillation, and the bones are then reduced or burned into very white ashes through calcination <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to remove volatile substances and reduce it to powder.</note>, and then these three are joined together again in the correct manner so that they resemble butter, they become a most wonderful, specific *arcanum* <note>original: "Arcanum"; a secret or powerful medicinal remedy in alchemy.</note>. With this, you may thoroughly cure any bone fracture without any pain after only three dressings, provided that you handle and set the fracture according to the rules of surgery <note>original: "Chirurgery"</note> and then apply that specific as a plaster.

# Of the Nature of Things: Book VIII

...of thorns, and nails thrust through his hands and feet, and the spear with which his side was pierced, and the reed and sponge <note>These are the "Instruments of the Passion," the items used during the crucifixion of Jesus.</note> in which they gave him vinegar to drink, and the whips with which he was scourged and beaten. <margin>Matthew 25</margin>
For the voice shall be heard: *Arise you dead, and come to judgment.* Then shall the twelve Apostles sit down, their seats being prepared in the clouds, and shall judge the twelve Tribes of Israel. In that place, the holy angels shall separate the bad from the good, the cursed from the blessed, the goats from the sheep. Then the cursed shall be thrown downward like stones and lead, but the blessed shall fly on high like eagles. Then from the tribunal of God shall go forth this voice to those who stand on his left hand: *Go, you cursed, into everlasting fire prepared for the Devil and his angels from all eternity: For I was hungry and you fed me not; thirsty and you gave me no drink; sick, in prison, and naked, and you visited me not, freed me not, clothed me not, and you showed no pity toward me; therefore shall you expect no pity from me.* On the contrary, he shall speak to those on his right hand: *Come, you blessed and chosen, into my Father's Kingdom, which has been prepared for you and his angels from the foundation of the world: For I was hungry and you gave me food <note>original: "meat"</note>; thirsty and you gave me drink; I was a stranger and you took me in; naked...*

->*Of the Signature of Natural Things.*<-

In this book it is appropriate <note>original: "convenient"</note> for us, while speaking of the Signature of things, to first declare by whom things are signed, who the <term>Signator</term> <note>The "Signator" is the agent or force—whether human, divine, or natural—that imprints a specific mark or "signature" onto an object, revealing its inner properties or purpose.</note> is, and how many types of things are signed. You must therefore know that the things that are signed are of three sorts. The first sort of them is signed by Man; the second by the <term>Archeius</term> <note>In the philosophy of Paracelsus, the Archeus (or Archeius) is the "internal chemist" or vital force that governs the growth, function, and healing of living organisms.</note>; the

### Book IX: Of the Nature of Things

...the third, the stars of supernatural things. Therefore, upon this account, there are three Signators <term>Signators</term> <note>original: "Signators"; those who apply a "signature" or mark to a thing to reveal its purpose or character.</note>: **Man**, the **Archeus** <term>Archeus</term> <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, the Archeus is the "internal chemist" or vital force that directs the functions of life and nature.</note>, and the **Stars**.

Moreover, you must note that impressions signed by men bring with them a perfect knowledge and judgment of hidden things, and impart the knowledge of their hidden virtues and faculties.

The marks of the Stars cause prophecies and omens <note>original: "Presages"</note>, and declare the supernatural virtues of things. They allow for true judgments and signs in *Geomancy, Chiromancy, Physiognomy, Hydromancy, Pyromancy, Necromancy, Astronomy, the Beryllistic art* <note>original: "Berillisticall art"; the practice of crystal-gazing or scrying using a beryl stone.</note>, and other Astral sciences.

But so that we may explain all the signs or marks briefly and truly, it will be necessary in the first place that we speak of those signs of which Man is the signer. Once those are understood, you may more rightly understand the rest, whether natural or supernatural. It is therefore known that the Jews carry a yellow sign upon their cloak or coat <note>This refers to the "Jewish badge," a historical requirement in many parts of Europe during the Middle Ages and Early Modern period for Jewish people to wear identifying marks.</note>. And this is nothing else but that mark by which they would have you know, when you meet them, that they are Jews. Similarly, a Serjeant <note>original: "Serjeant"; here referring to a minor official or law officer rather than a modern military rank.</note> is known by his multi-colored coat or sleeve. Likewise, every magistrate dresses his servants in his own colors and liveries.

Every craftsman <note>original: "Mechanick"</note> marks his work with a certain sign, so that everyone may know whose work it is.

For which end carriers wear the livery of their master or city, so that it may be known whom they serve and from where they travel, so that they may thereby travel more safely.

So every soldier wears a mark or badge, such as colors—black, white, yellow, green, blue, or red, and so on—so that he may be distinguished from the enemy. From this, it may be known that this is Caesar’s soldier, that is the King's, this is an Italian, or this is a French soldier, etc. These are signs which belong to an order or office, of which many more could be listed. But because we have intended to describe the signs of natural and supernatural things, we shall not fill up this book with these other types of signs.

As concerning those signs which man makes—which do not only respect order, office, or name, but lead to the understanding of his knowledge, age, dignity, degree, etc.—you must know this concerning money: every kind of currency has its own peculiar trial and mark. By this, it is known how much it is valued, who is the master of it <note>This likely refers to the "Master of the Mint," the official responsible for the integrity of the coinage.</note>, and in what place it is usually current. From this comes that German Proverb: "Money is nowhere more in demand than where it was coined."

The same is also to be understood of things that are inspected and examined by sworn men appointed for that purpose before they are signed—such as cloth, which is marked with specific marks. By these, it may be known that in the examination, the goods were found to be of high quality and proven. Why is a seal put upon letters, except that it represents a certain bond which no man is permitted to violate? For a seal is a confirmation of letters, and because of it, they are accounted by all men to be ratified. Without a seal, an acquittance <term>a written receipt or legal document releasing a person from a debt or obligation</term> is void and of no force.

After the same manner, many things are marked with just a few letters, names, or words—such as books, which being

Being written on the outside with only one word, they immediately show what is contained within.

The same rule also applies to the glasses and boxes in Apothecaries' <note>an early version of a pharmacist</note> shops, all of which are distinguished by specific names or labels put upon them. Unless that were done, who could distinguish so many waters, liquors, syrups, oils, powders, seeds, ointments, and all <term>simples</term> <note>original: "simples"; medicinal plants or medicines consisting of only one ingredient</note>? In the same way, the <term>Alchemist</term> in his <term>laboratory</term> <note>original: "Elabatorie"</note> marks with names and labels all waters, liquors, spirits, oils, <term>phlegms</term> <note>original: "Flegms"; in alchemy, the watery, tasteless substance resulting from distillation</note>, <term>crocus</term> <note>a powder, often a metallic oxide, used in chemical preparations</note>, <term>alkali</term> <note>original: "Alcali"</note>, and all medicinal materials, so that he may make use of and recognize any of them when the occasion arises. Without this help, his memory could never keep track of them.

In the same way, all houses and buildings may be marked with numbers or figures, so that the age of every one of them may be known immediately at first sight of the number.

I wanted to show you these and other things that are marked so that, once these are understood, I might be better understood by you in the rest of my writing, and so the meaning of everything might be more plain and clear.

# On the Monstrous Signs of Men.

<margin>Various monstrous signs or marks.</margin>
Many people are born deformed with monstrous marks or signs; for example, one person has an extra finger or toe, while another lacks one. The fingers of some grow together in the mother's womb. Another has a twisted <note>original: "wry"</note> foot, arm, or neck, and so on, and brings it with them out of the womb. Another has a hump <note>original: "bunch"</note> in his back. In the same way, intersex people <note>original: "Hermaphrodites"</note> are born—that is, those who are both male and female and have the organs of both man and woman, or else lack both. I have observed many of these monstrous signs in both males and females; all of these are to be considered monstrous signs of secret, evil astrological influences <note>original: "ascendents"</note>. This confirms the proverb: "The more crooked, the more wicked," or "Lame limbs, lame deeds." 

<margin>What monstrous signs reveal.</margin>
For these are signs of vices and rarely signify anything good.

Just as an executioner marks his sons with shameful <note>original: "infamous"</note> marks, so do evil astrological influences imprint supernatural, mischievous marks upon the young so that they may be better avoided. These marks appear on their foreheads, cheeks, ears, fingers, hands, eyes, tongues, and so on, appearing shortened or cut off. Every one of these shameful signs indicates a specific vice. For example, a brand burned onto the face of a woman, or the cutting off of her ears, usually signifies theft. The loss of fingers indicates cheating gamblers <note>original: "dicers"</note>, and the loss of a hand [indicates...]

So you may also recognize those who deny the Christian religion by a cross burned into the soles of their feet—specifically because they have denied their Redeemer.

But moving past these examples so that we may proceed to the "monstrous signs" of malignant influences rising, you must know that all monstrous signs do not arise from an <term>Ascendent</term> <note>original: "Ascendent"; in astrology, the sign of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment, believed to influence personality and physical form</note> alone. They also frequently arise from the "stars" of the human mind, which continually and every moment rise and set with one's fancy, judgment, or imagination, just as they do in the superior firmament. Therefore, due to the fear or terror of pregnant <note>original: "breeding"</note> women, many "monsters"—or children marked with unusual signs in the womb—are born. 

The primary cause of these is fear, terror, or intense desire <note>original: "appetite"</note>, from which the imagination is stirred. If a pregnant woman begins to imagine intensely, then her internal "heaven" is set in motion, just like the superior firmament is moved every moment with its rising and setting stars. Following the example of the greater firmament (the universe), the stars of the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>original: "Microcosme"; the "little world" or the human being, viewed as a miniature reflection of the universe</note> are also moved by the imagination. This continues until there is an "assault" <note>a sudden, forceful psychological event or impact</note>, whereby the stars of the imagination convey an influence and leave an impression upon the pregnant woman, just as if someone were to press a seal into wax or stamp a coin. 

For this reason, these signs and birthmarks <note>original: "geniall marks"</note> are called the <term>Impressions</term> of inferior stars. Many philosophers have written extensively about this, and men have tried hard to provide a full and rational account of it, though it has never yet been fully achieved.

# *Of the Astral Signs of Physiognomy*
## *in Man.*

<margin>The origin of physiognomy <note>original: "Phyſiognomie"</note>.</margin>
**T**he signs of physiognomy <term>physiognomy: the practice of judging a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face</term> receive their origin from the superior stars. This art of physiognomy was greatly esteemed by our ancestors, and especially by Heathens, Tartars, Turks, and other peoples among whom it was the custom to sell men as slaves; it is not yet altogether laid aside among Christians. Yet, alongside it, many errors have crept in that have not yet been noticed by anyone, while every blockish, ignorant fellow would take it upon himself, without any degree of judgment, to judge anyone. It is a wonder that those errors were never noticed by observing the works, deeds, and abilities of the men themselves.

## LIB. IX. Of the Nature of Things.

...one or stir him up; he indeed does not speak wrongly: but yet there is some difference in it which must be taken notice of, because the stars compel some, and others they do not compel.

For here we must know who can rule or constrain the stars, and who can be governed by them. Therefore, for this you must note that a wise man can rule the stars and not be subject to them. The stars are subject to a wise man and are forced to obey him, and not he the stars. 

But the stars compel an "animal man" <term>animal man: an unspiritual person driven by basic instincts, ignorance, and physical nature rather than reason or divine wisdom</term>, so that wherever they lead him, he must follow—just as a thief follows the gallows, a highway robber the wheel <note>original: "the wheel"; a reference to the breaking wheel, a form of capital punishment used for highwaymen</note>, the fisherman the fish, the fowler the birds, and the hunter the wild beasts. And what is the cause of this, but that such a man knows neither himself nor his own strength? He never considers or thinks that he is the "lesser world" <term>lesser world: the microcosm; the philosophical concept that a human being is a miniature representation of the entire universe</term>, and that he has the universal firmament <term>firmament: the sphere of the heavens and the stars</term> with its powers hidden within him. 

Wherefore he is called an "animal," an ignorant man, and a slave to any base service and all earthly matters. Yet, he received that privilege from God in Paradise that he should rule and reign over all other creatures of the universe, and should not be obedient to them. 

<margin>Man is the Lord of all other creatures.</margin>

Therefore, God created him last of all, all the rest being made before him. Man later lost this privilege through his Fall <note>The Fall: the biblical event in which humanity lost its original state of innocence and dominion</note>; yet the wisdom of man was not made servile, nor did he let that liberty go out of his hands. Because of this, it is necessary that the stars should follow him and obey him, and not he the stars. And although he may indeed be the "son of Saturn" and have Saturn as his ascendant <term>ascendant: the astrological sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth, thought to determine character</term>, he can still remove himself from its influence and so overcome it that he can be the...

# Of the Nature of Things. Book IX.

<margin>How a person may withdraw themselves from one star and place themselves under another.</margin>
...offspring of the Sun, and bring himself under any other planet, making himself its son. It is the same situation as with a miner <note>original: "Digger"</note> who for a time has spent his efforts working for the Master of the Mines. Having performed his service faithfully at the risk of his life, he finally reasons and argues with himself in this way:

"What will eventually become of you if you spend your entire life underground, and through your constant labor also put your body and life in danger? I will get a release from my master and I will serve another master, where my life may be made sweeter, where I may have plenty of food and drink, where I may wear better clothes, have little work and high wages, and where there shall be no danger of the mountain hanging over me and being ready to fall on me," etc. In this way, he would seek his liberty, whereas otherwise he must remain a hired servant <note>original: "Mercenary servant"</note> and slave, wasting away with hard labor and poor food, etc.

Now you see how a wise man commands the stars and can remove himself from any harmful <note>original: "malignant"</note> planet and place himself under another better one. He can bring himself out of slavery into liberty and can free himself from the prison of an ill-omened planet.

So also a natural man <note>original: "Animall man"; in this context, it refers to a person living according to their basic physical instincts rather than spiritual wisdom</note>, who is the son of the Sun, of Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury, may remove himself from a favorable <note>original: "benigne"</note> planet and subject himself to Saturn or Mars. Such a person is like a man who runs away from a religious college; being impatient with an easy life, he becomes a soldier or else a man of no reputation, who afterwards spends his entire life in sorrow and misery.

### LIB. IX. Of the Nature of Things. 111

Such a person is also a rich man who, being prone to frivolity <note>original: levity</note>, spends and wastes all his goods on gambling <note>original: dicing</note>, feasting, and visiting prostitutes <note>original: whoring</note>. He gives himself to these things until everything is spent. Then he comes to want; being miserably afflicted with shameful poverty, he deservedly becomes an object of laughter and scorn to everyone—even to the very boys in the streets. You may hear them saying: "Look at that beggarly man who is worth nothing. When he was a master, he disdained his status <note>original: Mastership</note> and preferred to be a slave and a beggar—a slave even to servants—since he can never regain his former privilege."

A malignant star or an ill-omened ascendant <note>In astrology, the "ascendant" is the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of one's birth, thought to shape character and destiny.</note> drives him to this end. If he hadn't been a fool and dishonest, he would not have abandoned the certain dominion <note>This reflects the Hermetic and Paracelsian idea that a wise person can exercise "dominion" or authority over the influence of the stars through the power of will and reason.</note> that he held over the stars, but would have struggled against their influence. Even if he did not know how to resist the stars on his own, he might have turned his mind to the examples of others, thinking to himself: "Look how rich that man was, but he foolishly and shamefully brought himself to poverty. He also lived elegantly without much labor, and had such good food and such income <note>original: wages</note> that he could not have lived better. Now he lives sparingly and filthily <note>original: sordidly</note>; instead of wine, he must drink water. His daily labors increase, while his income decreases."

Now, how often does such a man talk to himself in this way: "What have I done? Where am I running so headlong, spending the goods I earned so basely? Who will repair my estate? If I ever recover what has been spent, I will take a completely different course of life. I will learn to be wise from my hardships, and..."

# Of the Nature of Things. Book XI.

...will make amends for my evil deeds.

<margin>No man is wise by his own harm, but by another’s.</margin>
But it is appropriate to know that no man can be wise by his own harm. For it is a foolish and senseless thing to be wise by one's own harm <note>The author argues that learning only through one's own suffering is a sign of folly; true wisdom is learning from the mistakes of others to avoid suffering altogether.</note>. Let him who wishes to be wise be wise by another man's example, not by his own. For he who has once spent his estate would spend it again if he should have it, and he who once perishes, perishes forever. He who has once cast a dice, casts it again. He who once stole and escaped the gallows will endeavor to steal a second time also. For he thinks thus to himself: "My enterprises have succeeded once and again, and why not a third or fourth time? If God should once restore what I have lost, he would restore it a second and third time," etc. "If he did not forsake me in my first misery, he will not in the second or third," etc.

An *animal* <note>original: "Animall man"; in this context, it refers to a person driven by physical instincts and worldly desires rather than spiritual reason or divine wisdom.</note> man does all these things; he is the servant and slave of the stars, who is turned every which way and moved by the stars like a *reed* in water.

And this is the reason why his life is spent in misery and he dies in infamy.

Who, therefore, will endure such servitude and not deliver himself from such a nasty prison? For anyone may, by his own wisdom together with the help of his star, deliver and free himself from there. Consider the matter thus:

A *fowler* <note>a person who hunts or catches wild birds</note> by his prudence and the help of his star—overcoming another star—needs not go after birds, for they will come flying after him...
