<meta>The text continues from the previous page, completing a word: "folding" (possibly "unfolding" or "following") to unusual places, contrary to their nature.</meta>

...folding to unusual places, contrary to their nature.

So a fisherman can, by making use of the wisdom which God has given him, make fishes swim to him of their own accord, so that he may take them up with his hands.

A hunter, employing his wisdom, does by his "star" <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, the "star" refers to an internal spiritual force or intellectual power within a person</note> so compel wild beasts that he does not need to follow them; instead, they will follow him without any natural impulse. The same is true for all other living creatures.

<margin>Stars are twofold.</margin>
Now, for the better understanding of these things, you must know that stars are twofold: terrestrial <note>earthly</note> and celestial <note>heavenly</note>. These latter are of wisdom, while the others are of folly.

And as there are two worlds—the greater <term>Macrocosm: the universe as a whole</term> and the lesser <term>Microcosm: the human being as a miniature reflection of the universe</term>—and the greater usually governs the lesser, so also the stars of the Microcosm <note>man's inner spirit and intellect</note> rule over and overcome the celestial stars.

<margin>The purpose of the stars is to serve, not command, man.</margin>
Neither did God create the planets and other stars of the heavens to rule over man, but rather that they—like all other creatures—should obey and serve him. And although the inferior stars <note>the physical planets in the sky</note> do influence men and mark them (as they do all other earthly bodies) with natural signs according to the manner of their generation, that is not a form of power or sovereignty. It is only a predestined command and office, through which nothing may remain hidden or concealed; rather, the inward force and power may be brought out and revealed by exterior signs.

<margin>Signs are twofold.</margin>
But to return to our purpose regarding the physiognomical <term>Physiognomy: the practice of judging character or predicting the future from physical features, especially the face</term> signs of men: you must know that they are twofold—

<summary>This page argues that human wisdom and the "inner stars" of the Microcosm have the power to command nature and overcome the influence of the celestial planets. It establishes that outward physical signs (physiognomy) exist to reveal the hidden internal powers and character of a person.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Microcosm, Macrocosm, Physiognomy, Astrology, internal stars, dominion over nature, natural signs</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on signs and signatures found in nature, specifically focusing on those found in the human body. The previous page likely introduced the concept of how various forces "sign" or mark physical forms.</meta>

# Of the Nature of Things. BOOK IX.

<page-num>114</page-num>

...fold, in external shape indeed alike, but in power and effect unlike.

Some are from the supernatural stars of the heaven; the others are from the inferior stars, namely, those of the Microcosm <term>Microcosm: the human being viewed as a "little world" that mirrors the patterns of the entire universe (the Macrocosm)</term>.

Whatever the superior star signs <note>original: "signes"; refers to the astrological "signature" or mark left on a person</note> during the process of generation, lasting even to middle age, that signed thing is predestined and possesses peculiar powers. For it testifies to the nature and condition of Man. Therefore, whatever the inferior star of the Microcosm signs during generation has its origin from the Father and Mother. This occurs as often as the Mother, through her imagination, appetite, fear, or terror, has by contact affected her infant in her womb with supernatural signs. These are called "Mother’s marks" or "Womb marks" <note>This refers to the historical belief in "maternal impressions," where a mother's intense emotions or experiences were thought to physically mark the developing fetus</note>. Since we have spoken of these already, we shall now save the labor of repeating ourselves, as our purpose is only to treat physiognomical signs. 

<margin>What Physiognomical signs are.</margin>
In this context, we shall speak only of the predestined signs of the stars. By these, we mean those signs in men that neither their Father nor Mother had any likeness of in their own bodies.

Of this sort are: black, gray, small, or large eyes; a long, crooked, or sharp nose; pits in the cheeks; raised cheekbones; a flat or broad nose; small or large ears; a long neck; a long face; a wide or small mouth; and thick or thin, many or few, black, yellow, and red hairs, etc.

If one or more of these signs appear in a man, you must know that they do not lack their own significance.

<summary>The author distinguishes between inherited birthmarks (maternal impressions) and "physiognomical signs" caused by celestial influence, which reveal a person's predestined nature.</summary>
<keywords>Microcosm, astrology, physiognomy, maternal impressions, birthmarks, predestination, signatures of nature</keywords>

<page-num>115</page-num>
->**BOOK IX: Of the Nature of Things**<-

But it is necessary that you consider them according to the <term>physiognomical art</term> <note>The practice of judging a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face.</note>, and that you have certain knowledge of the <term>Art of Signatures</term> <note>The belief that the external appearance of a thing indicates its internal nature or medicinal qualities.</note>, by which you may know the internal man through his external signs.

<margin>What black eyes signify.</margin>
But to proceed to the practice of what we intended, and to list some of the signs of men and their meanings in part:

You must know that black eyes, besides indicating a healthy constitution, also many times signify a constant mind—not wavering or fearful, but vigorous <note>original: "lusty"</note>, hearty, true, and loving virtue.

<margin>What gray eyes signify.</margin>
Gray eyes are a sign of a deceitful and changeable man.

<margin>Weak eyes.</margin>
Weak eyes signify good judgment, a witty nature, and profound deliberation.

<margin>Purblind eyes.</margin>
<term>Purblind</term> <note>original: "Purblind"; strictly meaning partially blind, but here referring to eyes that are dim-sighted or unsteady.</note> eyes, and such as turn upward, downward, and to both sides, signify a false and crafty man who cannot easily be deceived. Such a person is treacherous, hates labor, and is slothful, earning his living lazily through dice <note>gambling</note>, usury <note>lending money at high interest</note>, sex work, robbery, and so on.

<margin>Small and deep-set eyes.</margin>
Small and deep-set eyes for the most part signify weak and feeble vision, with blindness following in old age. They also indicate men who are strong, warlike, bold, deceitful, nimble, and factious <note>inclined to join small, often troublesome groups</note>—patiently enduring their condition, yet the end of their lives is for the most part tragic.

<margin>Great eyes.</margin>
Great eyes signify a covetous and ravenous man, especially if they bulge out of the head.

<margin>Always blinking.</margin>
Eyes that are always blinking indicate weak sight and a man who is fearful and anxious <note>original: "solicitous"</note>.

<header>Of the Nature of Things. Book IX.</header>

<margin>Rolling eyes</margin>
Rolling eyes show a romantic disposition <note>original: "amorous affection"</note>, as well as a wise man with a quick intellect <note>original: "quick intention"</note>.

<margin>Continually cast down</margin>
Eyes that are continually looking downward <note>original: "dejected"</note> show a bashful, modest man, etc.

<margin>Red eyes</margin>
Eyes that are red signify a bold and strong man, etc.

<margin>Clear eyes</margin>
Clear eyes that do not move easily show a man who is heroic, noble <note>original: "magnanimous"</note>, strong, cheerful, and formidable to his enemies, etc.

<margin>Large ears</margin>
Large ears show good hearing, a good memory, attentiveness, diligence, and a sound brain and head, etc.

Low-set ears are a bad omen. For the most part, they signify a man who is malicious, deceitful, and unjust; they also indicate poor hearing, a bad memory, and a man who is reckless, easily exposing himself to danger.

<margin>A long nose</margin>
A long nose that hooks downward is a good sign; it signifies a man who is valiant, wise <note>original: "prudent"</note>, secretive <note>original: "close"</note>, rigorous, and yet just, etc.

<margin>A flat nose</margin>
A flat nose signifies a man who is malicious, lustful, prone to lying, inconsistent, etc.

<margin>A sharp nose</margin>
A sharp nose indicates a man who is a turncoat <note>someone who frequently changes their loyalties or principles</note>, a mocker, etc.

<margin>A long nose</margin>
A long nose indicates a man who is slow in all his actions, but has a very keen sense of smell.

<margin>Hollow cheeks</margin>
Cheeks with pits or hollows in them signify a man who is talkative, scornful, argumentative <note>original: "contentious"</note>, etc.

<margin>A long chin</margin>
A long chin and a long face signify a man who is prone to anger and slow to work, etc.

### LIB. VIII. *Of the Nature of things.* <note>Book 8. *Of the Nature of Things.*</note>

<margin>*A cleft chin.*</margin>
A cleft chin <note>original: "divided chin"</note> signifies that a man is faithful, dutiful <note>original: "officious"</note>, subtle, and inconsistent in his words—speaking one thing while meaning another. He is prone to anger yet regretful of it, and is clever and inventive.

<margin>*A large mouth.*</margin>
A great and large mouth signifies that a man is a glutton <note>original: "great devourer"</note>, simple, foolish, unwise, and reckless. A small mouth signifies the opposite.

<margin>*Lips drawn in.*</margin>
Lips that are drawn in, where the upper lip is larger than the lower, signify a man who is angry, warlike, and courageous, yet for the most part of rude and uncivil behavior, with manners like a pig.

<margin>*Large lower lips.*</margin>
Lips that are large on the bottom signify a man who is dull, foolish, and stupid <note>original: "blockish"</note>.

<margin>*Hair signifies nothing by Art.*</margin>
Judgment based on the hair of the head or beard is not very reliable, because experience shows that they can be changed in many ways—made black, yellow, red, white, or grey <note>original: "hoary"</note>, or curled, soft, or hard, according to how a person desires to have them.

For this reason, it happens that many who are otherwise skilled enough in the art of physiognomy <note>term: the practice of assessing a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face</note> would be most shamefully deceived if they were to judge rashly by the hair, attributing to the influence of the stars what should instead be credited to the people themselves.

Yet it cannot be denied that hair firmly rooted on the head signifies the good health of the head, as well as of the whole body.

## Of the Astral Signs of Chiromancy.

**C**oncerning the signs of chiromancy <term>the art of divination through the study of lines, originally in the hand but here applied to all of nature</term>, we must maintain that they arise from the superior stars of the seven planets; and that we must know and judge them by those seven planets.

<margin>What chiromancy is.</margin>
Now, chiromancy is an art which does not only look into the hands of men and make judgments by their lines and wrinkles, but also considers all herbs, woods, hard stones, earths, and rivers—indeed, whatever has lines, veins, or wrinkles.

Neither is this art without its errors, for which those astronomers <note>original: "Astronomers"; here referring to practitioners of judicial astrology who assigned celestial influences to physical traits</note> were guilty.

For they assigned the fingers of both hands to the planets and the most significant stars, whereas there are only five fingers on one hand and ten on both, yet the planets are reckoned as only seven.

How, then, can these things agree with each other?

Now, if there were seven fingers on each hand, it might then be granted that every finger could be ascribed to several planets.

<margin>Whether the fingers are assigned to the planets.</margin>
Yet it many times happens that some men may have only seven fingers on both hands, the rest being accidentally <note>original: "casually"</note> cut off. But seeing as they were cut off, and

<summary>This page defines chiromancy as a universal science of interpreting lines and patterns across all natural objects, while critiquing the traditional astrological practice of mapping the seven planets onto the human fingers.</summary>

*Mercury:* The superfluous parts shall be taken away, and you shall burn the residue with fire. Put it into water and boil it; after it is boiled, you shall give it to the sick as medicine <note>original: "by way of physick"</note>.

*Alchemist:* What devil reprimands me—so great a man and <term>Philosopher</term>?

I know all true <term>Philosophers</term> and wise men, and I love them because they love me and do everything for me as I wish. Where I cannot go, they help me. But you <term>Alchemists</term>—of whose group you are also one—do everything contrary to me without my knowledge or consent. This is why everything turns out contrary to your expectations. You think that you deal with my sons <note>Here, "sons" refers to the metallic principles or chemical elements.</note> rationally, but you perfect nothing. If you consider it rightly, you do not handle them; they handle you. For you can make nothing from them, nor do you know how to do it; but when they please, they make fools of you.

## A TREATISE
-> OF <-
# SULPHUR:
### *The Preface.*

**COURTEOUS READER,**

Since I might not write more clearly than other ancient philosophers <note>original: "Philosophers"; in this context, the term specifically refers to alchemists, often called "philosophers by fire."</note> have written, perhaps you may not be satisfied with my writings—especially since you have so many other books of philosophers already in your hands. But believe me, I have no need to write books, because I seek neither profit nor vainglory by them; therefore, I do not publish who I am. 

Those things which I have now published for your profit and advantage seem to me to be more than enough. I intend that other things which remain shall be referred to the *Book of Harmony*, where I have treated extensively of natural things. Yet, by the persuasion of some friends, I must also write this *Treatise of Sulphur* <note>In alchemy, Sulphur is one of the "Three Primes" (with Mercury and Salt), representing the active, masculine, and combustible principle of matter.</note>; in which, whether it be necessary to add any...

<sig>L 2</sig>

<vocab>Alchemy, Sulphur, Philosophers, Book of Harmony, Treatise of Sulphur</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, the author explains that he writes not for profit or fame, but for the benefit of the reader, introducing a specific treatise on the alchemical principle of Sulphur.</summary>
<keywords>Sulphur, alchemy, Michael Sendivogius, Book of Harmony, alchemical principles, philosophical authorship</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>The text on this page continues from a discussion about the insufficiency of philosophical writings compared to the direct observation of Nature. The catchword from the previous page likely completed a thought regarding the clarity of the author's instructions.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>

->A New Light of Alchemy<-

...thing to what was written before, I do not know. Truly, even this will not satisfy you if the writings of so many philosophers cannot. And especially, if the daily operations of Nature are not a sufficient example, no other examples will do you any good. For if you would consider with mature judgment how Nature works, you would have no need for so many volumes by philosophers; in my opinion, it is better to learn from Nature, the mistress, than from her scholars. 

You have enough in the Preface of the book *The Twelve Treatises*, as well as in the First Treatise itself. In this Art <note>referring to the "Art" of Alchemy</note>, there are so many large books to be found that they hinder rather than help those who study it. Indeed, it seems that from that small *Schedule of Hermes* <note>original: "Schedule of Hermes," likely referring to the *Emerald Tablet*, the brief foundational text of Hermeticism</note>, the writings of philosophers have grown into such a vast and misleading labyrinth, becoming more obscure every day. I believe this is done only by envious philosophers, while the ignorant do not know what should be added or omitted if, by chance, the author’s handwriting cannot be easily read. 

If in any science or art the addition or removal of a single word can help or hurt significantly, it is even more so in this one. For example, it is written in one place: *Then mix these waters together*; another writer adds the word *Not*. He added only a little, yet by doing so, he made the whole chapter mean the exact opposite. Nevertheless, let the diligent student know that a bee gathers honey even from poisonous herbs. But if he judges what he reads according to the possibilities of Nature, he will easily get beyond all the sophistry <note>original: *Sophistry*, meaning deceptive or fallacious reasoning</note> of philosophers. Yet, he should not give up reading, because one book ex—

And by this means, I understood that the books of **Geber** the **Philosopher** <note>original: "Geber"; likely referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy</note> (and who could know it, except those who read other authors?) are so wonderfully enchanted that they cannot possibly be understood unless they are read over a thousand times—and even then, only by a clever reader. There are indeed many who undertake to interpret him as they do other authors, but I see their explanation is more difficult than the text itself. My advice is that you persist in the text, and apply whatever you read to the potential of Nature; and in the first place, inquire diligently into what Nature is. 

All indeed write that she is a thing of little value, easy, and common; and indeed, it is true, but it should have been added that she is only so to wise men. The wise man knows her to be found among dung, while the ignorant man does not believe her to be in gold. I will not praise my own writings; they shall be judged by whoever applies them to the potential and course of Nature. And if, through my writings, advice, and examples, he does not come to know the operation of Nature and her serving **vital spirits** that compress the air, as also the subject of the <term>first matter</term><note>original: "first matter"; the *materia prima*, or the fundamental, formless base of all substance</note>, he will scarcely understand them through the works of **Ramon Llull** <note>original: "Raimandus Lullius"; a 13th-century Majorcan philosopher and alchemist</note>. It is a difficult thing to believe that spirits have such power and force in the "belly of the wind." I, too, am forced to travel through this forest...

# OF SULPHUR:
## *The Second Principle.*

Sulphur is not the least <note>original: "last," here meaning least in importance</note> among the principles, because it is a part of metal—indeed, the principal part of the Philosophers' Stone—and many wise men have left in writing various and very true things regarding sulphur. Even Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), known in the West as Geber, was a foundational figure in alchemy; his works emphasize the "mercury-sulphur theory" of metals.</note> himself, in his first book, *The Height of Perfection* <note>original: *Summa perfectionis magisterii*</note>, chapter 28, says: "By the grace of the most High God, it illuminates every body, because it is light from light, and a tincture" <term>tincture</term> <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" is a spiritual or material essence that can color, refine, or transform other substances.</note>. 

But before we treat of it, it seems good to us to first describe the origin of the principles, especially since in ancient times, sulphur was considered the foremost of the principles. Now, very few have until now shown where the principles arise, and it is a difficult thing to judge any of the principles, or anything else, whose origin and generation are unknown; what can a blind man judge of color? What our predecessors have omitted, we have intended to supply in this treatise.

<summary>This page introduces Sulphur as the second and most vital principle of alchemy, referencing the authority of Geber and stating the author's intent to explain the origins of these principles which previous writers have neglected.</summary>
<keywords>Sulphur, Principles of Alchemy, Philosophers' Stone, Geber, Tincture, Alchemical Theory, Origin of Principles</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the foundational principles of alchemy, shifting from general theory to the specific origins of matter.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>A New Light of Alchemy.</header>

Now, the principles of things—especially of metals—according to the ancient philosophers are two: Sulphur and Mercury. However, according to more recent philosophers, there are three: Salt <note>original: Sal</note>, Sulphur, and Mercury. Now, the origin of these principles is the four elements, and we will begin first with the origin of these elements.

Therefore, let those who are students of this sacred science know that there are four elements, and that every one of these four has another element in its center, by which its elemental nature is formed <note>original: elementated</note>. These are the four pillars <note>original: statues</note> of the world, separated from Chaos during the creation of the world by divine wisdom. These uphold the fabric of the world by their contrary actions, maintained in equality and proportion; furthermore, by the influence <note>original: inclination</note> of celestial powers <note>original: vertues</note>, they bring forth all things that are within and upon the earth. We will discuss these in their proper places, but here we will return to our main purpose, starting first with the element that is nearest to us: the Earth.

### OF
# THE ELEMENT OF THE
# EARTH.

The Earth is of great worth in its quality and dignity; in this element, the other three, especially fire, rest. It is the most excellent element for concealing and revealing those things which are entrusted to it. It is dense and porous, heavy in respect to its smallness <note>meaning its high density despite its size</note>, but light in respect to its nature. It is also the center of the world, as well as the center of the other elements; through its center passes the axle-tree <note>the axis</note> of the world and of both poles. 

It is porous, as we said, like a <term>sponge</term>, and brings forth nothing of itself; rather, it receives everything that the other three distill and project into it. It preserves all things that are to be kept and brings to light all things that are to be revealed. It brings forth (as we said before) nothing of itself, but it is the receptacle for other things; it is that in which everything that is brought forth remains, and is decomposed <note>original: "putrefied," a stage in alchemical transformation where matter breaks down to be reborn</note> by the heat of motion, and is multiplied by the same, with the pure being separated from the impure. That which is heavy within it is hidden, and heat drives that which is light toward its <term>surface</term> <note>original: "superficies"</note>. It is the nurse and <term>womb</term> <note>original: "matrix"</note> of all seed and <term>mixture</term> <note>original: "commixtion"</note>. It can indeed...

# A New Light of Alchemy

...does nothing else than preserve the seed and what is made of it until it is ripe. It is cold, dry, and tempered with water; visible on the outside and fixed, but within it is invisible and volatile. It is a virgin and the *dead head* <note>original: *Caput Mortuum*; in alchemy, the worthless residue or "dross" remaining after a chemical process such as distillation or sublimation</note> left after the creation of the world. At the divine pleasure, it shall hereafter be calcined <note>reduced to powder through heat</note> after the extraction of its moisture, so that from it a new crystalline earth may be created. 

Also, this element is divided into a pure part and an impure part. The water uses the pure part to bring forth things, but the impure part remains in its globe. This element is the hiding place and mansion of all treasure. In its center is the fire of hell, preserving the fabric of the world in its existence, and this is done by the pressing out of water into the air. That fire is caused and kindled by the *First Mover* <note>original: *primum Mobile*; in the traditional Ptolemaic model of the universe, this was the outermost sphere that moved all the inner celestial bodies</note> and the influences of the stars. The heat of the sun, tempered with the air, meets with this internal heat for the ripening and drawing up of those things which are already conceived in its center. 

Moreover, the Earth partakes of fire, which is its intrinsic <note>internal or essential</note> part; it is only purified within fire. In this way, every element is purified by its own intrinsic part. Now the intrinsic part—the inside or center of the Earth—is the highest purity mixed with fire, where nothing can rest. It is, as it were, an empty place into which all the other elements project their virtues <note>inherent powers or essential qualities</note>, as has been discussed in the book of the *Twelve Treatises* <note>Referring to the first part of Sendivogius's work, "The Twelve Treatises on the Philosopher's Stone"</note>. And this concludes our discussion of the element of Earth, which we have called a sponge and the receptacle of other things, which serves our purpose.

### OF
## THE ELEMENT OF
# WATER.

Water is the heaviest element, full of oily phlegm <term>unctuous phlegm</term><note>In alchemy, this refers to a greasy, fatty, or "sulfurous" moisture believed to be a fundamental component of the elements</note>. It is an element more worthy in its quality than the Earth. On the outside, it is volatile, but on the inside, it is fixed. It is cold and moist, and tempered with the air. It is the sperm <term>sperm</term><note>In this context, "sperm" refers to the carrying medium or "vessel" of life, whereas the "seed" is the actual formative or generative principle within it</note> of the world, in which the seed of all things is kept; it is the keeper of the seed of everything.

Yet we must know that the Seed is one thing, and the Sperm another. The Earth is the receptacle of the Sperm, but Water is the receptacle of the Seed. Whatever the Air distills into the Water by means of fire, the water conveys that same thing to the Earth. Sometimes the sperm lacks a sufficient amount of seed for want of the heat which should digest <term>digest</term><note>In alchemical terms, "digestion" refers to the process of applying slow, steady heat to a substance to mature it or bring about a chemical change</note> it; for there is always plenty of sperm awaiting the seed, which by the...

# A New Light of Alchemy

Seed is already imagined, or conceived, in a certain proportion. This is because Nature makes and produces pure things through the first <term>putrefaction</term> <note>In alchemy, putrefaction is the process of decay or fermentation that allows a substance to be reborn into a higher form.</note>, but produces far more pure, worthy, and noble things through the second. 

You have an example of this in wood, which is <term>vegetable</term> <note>belonging to the plant kingdom</note>. In its first composition, Nature makes wood; but when that wood is corrupted after reaching maturity, it putrefies. From this decay, worms and similar vermin are bred, which possess both life and sight. It is manifest that a <term>sensible thing</term> <note>a "sensible" thing is a living creature capable of sensation and perception, as opposed to a stationary plant.</note> is always more worthy than a vegetable thing, for the organs of sensible creatures require much more subtle and purer matter. But let us return to our purpose.

This element is the <term>Menstruum</term> <note>original: "Menstruum"; in alchemy, this refers to a universal solvent or a life-giving fluid capable of dissolving or nourishing substances.</note> of the world, and it is divided into three sorts: namely pure, purer, and most pure. The heavens are created from its most pure substance; the purer substance is resolved into Air; but the pure, plain, and coarse substance remains in its own sphere. By divine appointment and the operation of Nature, this element preserves and keeps everything that is subtle. 

It forms one globe together with the Earth. It also has its center in the heart of the sea. It shares one axle-tree and pole with the earth, through which all courses and fountains of water issue forth, which afterward increase and grow into great rivers. By this flowing forth of waters, the Earth is preserved from burning; and through this moistening, the <term>universal Seed</term> is carried through the pores of the whole Earth, a process caused by heat and motion.

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the natural origins of water and the balance of the four elements (Fire, Air, Water, Earth), refuting the idea that stars are the primary source of water generation.</meta>

...which flow into the sea, proceed from the stars. Those who know no other reason why the sea does not overflow because of them claim that these waters are consumed in the heart of the sea. But Nature will not allow this, as we showed when we spoke of rain. The stars indeed cause water [to move or act], but they do not generate water, seeing that nothing is generated except from its own kind within the same species. Now, since the stars consist of *<term>Fire</term>*, and *<term>Air</term>*, how then could they generate water? 

And if it were so—that some stars generated water—then necessarily others must also generate Earth, and others the remaining elements. This is because the fabric of the world is supported by the four <term>Elements</term> in such a way that one may not exceed another by even the smallest particle; instead, they contend with one another in an equal balance. Otherwise, if one were to exceed the others, destruction would follow. 

Yet, let everyone hold whatever opinion they please; it is shown to us by the light of Nature that the fabric of the world is preserved by these four elements. Their equality is proportioned by the great God, and one does not exceed the other in its operation. But the waters upon the foundation of the Earth are contained, as if in a vessel, by the motion of the Air; toward the Arctic pole, they are compressed <note>original: "constringed"</note> by it, because there is no *<term>vacuum</term>*, or empty space, in the world. For this reason, the fire of hell exists in the center of the Earth, which the <term>Archeus</term> <note>The Archeus is an alchemical term for the "master craftsman" or vital force that directs the processes of nature and digestion.</note> of Nature governs.

<page-num>90</page-num>

# A New Light of Alchemical Science

The rest of the waters are, by the compression of the air, rolled around the Earth; together with the Earth, they make up the center of the world. This operation is performed daily, and so this world is also fortified daily and shall forever be naturally preserved from corruption—unless it is the good pleasure of the most High Creator (whose will is absolute) that it should be otherwise. Because that <term>Central Fire</term> <note>original: "Centrall Fire"; in alchemical theory, this was a heat source at the Earth's core that drove the growth of minerals and the movement of elements</note> never ceases to be kindled by the universal motion and influence of the heavenly virtues, it continues to warm the waters. Neither shall the waters cease to be resolved into air, nor shall the air cease to compress and keep down the residue of the waters with the Earth, containing them in the center so that they may not be moved out of their place. Thus, even in a natural manner, this world is made and continued through the mighty wisdom of God; and according to this example, it is necessary that all things in the world be naturally made.

We are willing to discover further to you this creation of the fabric of the world, so that you may know that the four <term>Elements</term> <note>Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note> have a natural sympathy with the superior realms because they were all made out of one and the same <term>Chaos</term> <note>the formless, primordial matter from which the universe was organized</note>. However, they are governed by the superior realms as the more worthy, and from that relationship comes the obedience found in this <term>sublunary</term> <note>referring to the Earthly realm "under the moon," which was believed to be subject to change and decay</note> place. 

But know that all those things were naturally discovered by the <term>Philosopher</term> <note>a term often used for a master alchemist or "Natural Philosopher"</note>, as shall be shown in its own place. Now to our purpose concerning the courses of waters and the ebbing and flowing of the sea: how they are carried from one pole to another by the <term>polar axle-tree</term> <note>the axis upon which the Earth or the celestial spheres were thought to rotate</note>. There are two poles: one is the <term>Arctic</term> <note>original: "Artick"</note>, which is in the superior and northern part, but the

Nothing would grow in the world if there were not a power of the *air* penetrating and altering, bringing with it nourishment that multiplies. In this element, by virtue of the *fire*, exists that imagined seed which binds the menstruum of the world by its occult <note>original: "occult" – hidden or secret natural powers</note> power. # OF
## The ELEMENT of
### FIRE.

Fire is the purest and most worthy element of all, full of oily corrosiveness <note>original: "unctuous corrosiveness"</note> adhering to it. It is penetrating, digesting, corroding, and wonderfully tenacious <note>original: "adhering"</note>; on the outside it is visible, but on the inside it is invisible and most fixed. It is hot, dry, and tempered with air. 

Its substance is the purest of all, and its essence was first of all elevated during the Creation to the throne of divine Majesty, when the Waters of the Heavens were established (as we have said in the section on the Element of Water). Out of the less pure part of its substance, the angels were created. Out of that which was less pure than that, being mixed with the purest air, the Sun, Moon, and stars were created. That which is less pure than that is raised up to bound <note>original: "terminate"</note> and hold up the heavens. But the impure and oily part of it

Therefore, God abhors all impurity; nothing that is filthy, compounded, or blemished may come near him. Thus, no mortal man can see God or reach him by natural means. For the fire that surrounds the Divinity—which carries the seal and majesty of the Most High—is so intense that no eye can penetrate it. We have said that it is the most quiet subject (and indeed it is); otherwise, it would follow—which is absurd even to think—that God could not rest. For it is a state of the most peaceful silence...

By the will of the supreme Maker of all things, a most vehement and awesome <note>original: "terrible"; in this period, the word meant "inspiring great awe or terror" rather than simply "bad."</note> motion is created.

You have an example of this when any monarch of this world sits in his pomp: what a quietness there is about him! What silence! And although some member of his court might move, that motion is only of one particular man or another, which is not noticed. But when the Lord himself moves, there is a universal stir and motion; then all who attend to Him move with Him. What then occurs when that supreme Monarch, the King of Kings and Maker of all things (after whose example the princes of the world are established on the earth), moves in the majesty of His own person? What a stir! What trembling, when the whole guard of His heavenly army moves about Him!

But someone may ask, "How do we know these things, since heavenly things are hidden from man’s understanding?" To whom we answer that they are manifest to all philosophers <note>The "philosophers" mentioned here are the "natural philosophers" or alchemists who believe that by studying the physical world, they can understand divine truths.</note>; indeed, the incomprehensible wisdom of God has inspired them to know that all things are created after the pattern of Nature, and that Nature has its boundaries derived from those secret things and works accordingly. # A New Light of Alchemy

...the various offices of *angels*. So also, nothing is brought forth or generated except by natural means. All human inventions, even the Arts, which either exist now or shall exist in the future, proceed only from *natural* principles. The most high *Creator* was willing to reveal all *natural* things to humanity; therefore, He showed us that *celestial* things themselves were naturally made, so that His absolute and incomprehensible power and wisdom might be better known. The *philosophers* have a clear sight of all these things in the light of *Nature*, as if in a *mirror* <note>original: "Looking-glasse"</note>. 

For this reason, they highly esteemed this *Art* <note>Alchemy</note>, not so much out of greed for *gold* or *silver*, but for the sake of knowledge—not only of all *natural* things, but also of the power of the *Creator*. They were willing to speak of these things sparingly and only figuratively, lest divine mysteries by which *Nature* is illustrated should be revealed to the unworthy. 

You may easily understand these things if you know how to know yourself and are not stubborn <note>original: "of a stiff neck"</note>; you, who are created in the likeness of the great world—even in the image of *God*. You have in your body the *anatomy* of the whole world. Instead of the *firmament* <note>The sphere of the heavens or the stars.</note>, you have the quintessence of the four *elements*, extracted out of the *chaos of seeds* <note>original: "Chaos of Sperms"; in alchemical theory, this refers to the primordial, undifferentiated reproductive matter or "first matter" from which all life originates.</note> into a womb <note>original: "matrix"</note> and into a skin which surrounds it. You have most pure blood instead of *fire*, in the vital spirit of which is placed the seat of the soul (which acts in place of the *King*). **Arctic** pole; and your belly instead of the **Antarctic**, and all your limbs correspond to certain **celestial bodies**. Because the **ancients** omitted this, we are willing to show it to whoever is a diligent student of this secret. We do this so that the incomprehensible power of the Most High God may reach their heart more clearly, and so they may love and adore Him more zealously. 

Therefore, let the **seeker** of this sacred science know that the soul in a man—the lesser world or **Microcosm** <term>Microcosm: The philosophical idea that the human being is a small-scale representation of the entire universe (the macrocosm).</term>—takes the place of its **center**. It is the **King** and is located in the vital spirit, in the purest blood. The soul governs the mind, and the mind governs the body. When the soul conceives anything, the mind knows all things; all the limbs understand the mind, obey it, and desire to fulfill its will. 

For the body knows nothing; whatever strength or motion exists in the body is caused by the mind. The body is to the mind as instruments are to the **artisan** <note>original: "Artificer"</note>. Now the soul, by which man differs from other **animals**, operates within the body, but it has an even greater operation outside the body. This is because outside the body it reigns absolutely. In these things, man differs from other **animals**, who possess only a mind but not the soul of the **Deity**. So also God, the Maker of all...

->### A New Light of Alchemy.<-

...things, our Lord and our God works in this world those things which are necessary for the world; and in these he is included in the world, from which we believe that God is everywhere. But he is excluded from the body of the world by his infinite wisdom, by which he works outside of the world and imagines much higher things than the body of the world is able to conceive; and those things are beyond Nature, being the secrets of God alone.

You have the soul as an example, which outside of the body imagines many most profound things; and in this, it is like God, who outside of the world works beyond Nature—although the soul, compared to God, is like a candle lit against the light of the midday sun. For the soul imagines but does not execute anything except in the mind, but God effects all things at the same moment he imagines them. Just as the soul imagines something being done in Rome or elsewhere in the twinkling of an eye, but only in the mind, God does all such things essentially <note>original: "essentially," meaning in actual essence or physical reality rather than just conceptually</note>, for he is omnipotent. 

God, therefore, is not contained within the world except in the way the soul is in the body; he has his absolute power separate from the world. Likewise, the soul of any body has its absolute power separate from the body to do other things than the body can conceive. It has a very great power over the body if it so pleases, or otherwise our philosophy would be in vain. By these means, therefore, learn to know God, and you shall know how the Creator differs from the Creature <note>original: "Creature," referring here to "the created," meaning the physical universe and all things within it</note>. You yourself shall be able to conceive of greater things, now that we have opened the gate for you. But lest this treatise should grow too large, let us return to our purpose.

We said before that the element of fire is the most quiet of all, and that it is stirred up by motion—a fact which wise men knew. It is necessary that a <term>philosopher</term> <note>In this context, a philosopher refers to an alchemist or a student of natural science.</note> know the generation and corruption of all things. To such a person, not only is the creation of the heavens manifest, but also the composition and mixture of all things. However, although they know all things, yet they cannot *do* all things. 

We know indeed the composition of man in all respects, yet we cannot infuse the soul; because this mystery belongs only unto God, and He exceeds all things by these kinds of infinite mysteries. Since these are outside the course of Nature, they are not yet within the disposition of Nature. Nature does not work before there is matter given unto her to work upon. The first matter is given to her by God, and the second by the philosopher. 

Now, in the operation of the philosophers, Nature has a power to stir up the fire which is secretly included by the Creator in the center of every thing. This stirring up of the fire is done by the will of Nature, and sometimes by the will of the skillful <term>artificer</term> <note>A craftsman, chemist, or alchemist who directs natural processes.</note> disposing of Nature. For naturally all impurities and pollutions of things are purged by fire. All things that are compounded are dissolved by fire. As water washes and purges all imperfect things which are not <term>fixed</term> <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that is stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat.</note>, so the fire purges all things that are fixed, and by fire they are perfected. 

As water conjoins all things that are dissolved, so fire separates all things that are conjoined; and what is natural and has an affinity with it, the fire very well purges and augments—not in quantity, but in <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "vertue"; here meaning the essential power, potency, or medicinal property of a substance.</note>. This element does in several ways secretly work upon other elements and all things else. For as the <term>animal soul</term> <note>The "animal soul" was historically believed to be the source of sensation and voluntary movement in living creatures.</note>

is of the purest of this Element, so the vegetable <note>original: "Vegetable"; here referring to the "vegetative soul" or the life-force found in plants</note> is of the elementary part of it, which is governed by Nature. That which produces one thing consumes another, and another thing is naturally produced from this—a thing which is more noble than the former. By this means, an equality of the Elements is preserved, and so also of the *compositum* <note>original: "compositum"; a compound body made of the four elements</note>.

Separation is the natural death of all things, especially of living things. But this separation of man's composition must have been established on the day of judgment—that is, the first judgment—when the sentence of a natural death was passed upon him, for in Paradise, man was immortal. All divines <note>theologians</note> as well as Sacred Writ <note>the Bible</note> testify to this; but no philosopher has hitherto <note>original: "hitherto", based on the catchword "therto"</note> given a sufficient reason for this immortality.

...has shown until now, which it is convenient for the searcher of this sacred science to know, so that he may see how all these things are done naturally and be most easily understood. 

But it is most true that every compound thing in this world is subject to corruption and separation; this separation in the animal kingdom is called death. And since man is created and compounded of four elements, how can he be immortal? It is hard to believe that this is done naturally, but rather that there is something above nature in it. Yet many ages ago, God inspired philosophers—who were good men—with the knowledge that this is so naturally. 

Understand it to be thus: Paradise was, and is, a place created by the great Maker of all things from true elements—not <term>elementated</term> <note>original: "elementated"; the author uses this to describe substances that are mixed and degraded in the physical world, as opposed to the pure, simple "true elements" of Paradise.</note>, but most pure, temperate, and equally proportioned in the highest perfection. All things that were in Paradise were created of those same elements and were incorruptible. There, man was also created and framed from those same incorruptible elements, proportioned in such equality that he could in no way be corrupted; therefore, he was consecrated to immortality. For without any doubt, God created this Paradise for humans only; we have treated largely of what and where it is in our *Book of Harmony*. 

But afterward, when man had transgressed the commandment of the most high God through the sin of disobedience, he was driven out among the beasts into the corruptible, elementated world which God created only for animals. Because man cannot live without nourishment, he was forced of necessity to receive sustenance from corrupt, elementated elements. By this nourishment, those pure elements from which he was created were infected, and so by little...

...and declined little into corruption, until one quality exceeded another, and destruction, infirmity, and finally the separation and death of the whole compound followed. Consequently, those who are born in corrupt elements and from corrupt seed—rather than in Paradise—are now near to corruption and death. Seed produced from corrupt nutrients cannot be durable; and the longer it has been since humanity was driven out of Paradise, the closer people are to corruption. As a result, their lives are much shorter, and it will eventually reach a point where even reproduction <note>original: "generation"</note> itself will cease because of the shortness of life. 

Yet, there are some places where the air is more favorable and the stars more auspicious <note>original: "propitious"</note>; in those places, people's natures are not corrupted as quickly because they also live more temperately. Our own countrymen, by contrast, hasten toward corruption through gluttony and excessive <note>original: "inordinate"</note> living. Experience teaches that those born from the seed of infirm parents do not live long. But if humanity had remained in Paradise—a place suited to our nature, where all the elements were as incorrupt and pure as a virgin—we would have been immortal forever. 

It is certain that when pure elements are joined together with equal virtues, the resulting substance <note>original: "subject"</note> must be incorruptible; and this is exactly what the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>A legendary alchemical substance believed to be capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the Elixir of Life.</note> must be. Ancient philosophers compared this Stone to the creation of Man, but modern philosophers, taking everything literally, apply it instead to the corrupt reproduction of this current age.

This immortality was the primary reason that philosophers used their intelligence <note>original: "exercised their wits"</note> to find out this Stone.

### A New Light of Alchemy

The fire, together with the earth and air in which it potentially exists, work together. ### OF THE
# THREE PRINCIPLES
## *Of all things.*

These four elements being described, we will now descend to the principles of things. But how they are produced from the four elements, understand it thus: After Nature had received from the most high Creator of all things the privilege of being Princess over this monarchy of the world, she began to distribute places and provinces to everything according to its dignity. In the first place, she made the elements the princes of the world; and so that the will of the Most High (in whose will Nature was placed) might be fulfilled, she ordained that one should continually act upon the other. 

The Fire, therefore, began to act upon the Air and produced Sulphur; the Air also began to act upon the Water and brought forth Mercury; and the Water also began to act upon the Earth and brought forth Salt. <note>These three—Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt—are known in alchemy as the *Tria Prima*, the three prime substances that make up all material things.</note> But the Earth, since it had nothing to work upon, produced nothing; instead, that which was produced by the others continued and remained within it. Therefore, there became...

<summary>This page explains the alchemical origin of the Three Principles (Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt) through the interaction of the four elements under the direction of Nature and the Creator.</summary>

...became only three Principles, and the Earth was made the nurse and mother of the rest. As we said, three Principles were brought forth. The ancient philosophers did not consider these so strictly, but instead described only two actions of the elements. (Or if they chose to remain silent about the third, who are we to judge them, since they dedicated their writing only to the "Sons of Art" <note>The "Sons of Art" refers to initiated alchemists or dedicated students of the hermetic sciences.</note>?) They named these two Sulphur and Mercury, which may also suffice for us as the matter of metals and also of the Philosopher’s Stone.

Therefore, whoever wishes to be a true seeker of this sacred science must necessarily understand "accidents" <term>In Aristotelian philosophy, an accident is a property that can change without changing the essential nature of a substance (e.g., its color or temperature).</term> and the nature of an accident itself. This knowledge allows the seeker to learn which subject or element they intend to reach, so they may approach it through mediums if they desire to complete the full number of four <note>Referring to the four classical elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth.</note>. 

For just as these three Principles were produced from the four elements, they must also be reduced by the same logic: these three must produce two (Male and Female), and those two must produce one incorruptible thing. In this one thing, those four elements—being equally perfect—shall be purified and "digested" <term>In alchemy, digestion refers to the slow, steady heating of a substance to bring it to maturity or chemical perfection.</term> to the utmost. Thus, a quadrangle shall answer a quadrangle. This is that "quintessence" <term>Literally the "fifth essence," the purest or most concentrated essence of a substance, believed to be beyond the four physical elements.</term> which is essential for every craftsman <note>original: "Artificer"</note>, once it has been separated from many conflicting parts. 

In this way, you have in these three Principles—within any natural composition—a body, a spirit, and a hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> soul. If you separate these three, purify them well, and rejoin them as we described before, they will undoubtedly yield the purest fruit by imitating the ways of Nature. For although the soul is taken from a most noble place, it cannot reach its destination except through a spirit, which is the dwelling place and mansion of the soul. If you wish to reduce...

to its proper place, it is necessary that it be washed from all its impurities <note>original: "faults"</note>; and that the place be purified, so that the soul may be glorified in it and never again be separated from it. Now, therefore, you have the origin of the three Principles. From these, it is your role—by imitating Nature—to produce the "Mercury of the Philosophers" and their "First Matter," and to apply those principles of things (especially of metals) to your purpose. Without these principles, it is impossible for you to perfect anything through Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the human practice of alchemy and chemical transformation.</note>, just as Nature herself can neither do nor produce anything without them. These three are in all things, and without them, nothing exists in the world, or ever shall exist naturally.

But because we said before that the ancient Philosophers named only two Principles, the Seeker of the Art <note>original: "Searcher of the Art"; a student of alchemy</note> must know—lest they fall into error—that although the ancients only described Sulphur and Mercury, they could never have achieved this work without Salt. This is because Salt is the key and the beginning of this sacred science. It is that which opens the "gates of Justice"; it is that which holds the keys to the "infernal prisons" where Sulphur lies bound, as will be more fully shown later in the third treatise, *The Principles of Salt*.

Now to our purpose, which is that these three principles are absolutely necessary because they are the "near matter" <note>Proximate material, as opposed to the fundamental elements</note>. For the matter of metals is twofold: near and remote. The "near matter" is Sulphur and Mercury. The "remote matter" consists of the four Elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note>, from which God alone is able to create things. Therefore, leave the Elements alone, because you will achieve nothing with them; nor can you produce anything from them except these three Principles, since Nature herself...

Is it not better to go three miles than four? Let it suffice you, then, to have three Principles, out of which Nature produces all things in the earth and upon the earth; which three we find to be entirely in everything. 

By the proper separation and conjunction of these, Nature produces both metals and stones in the Mineral Kingdom; trees, herbs, and all such things in the Vegetable Kingdom; and also, in the Animal Kingdom, the Body, Spirit, and Soul, which especially resembles the work of the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to alchemists, and their "work" is the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. The Body is Earth, the Spirit is Water, and the Soul is Fire, or the "Sulphur of Gold." The Spirit augments the quantity of the Body, but the Fire augments its virtue <note>original: "vertue"; here meaning its power, potency, or essential quality.</note>. 

But because there is more of the Spirit in weight than there is of the Fire, the Spirit is raised and oppresses the Fire, drawing it to itself; and so each one of them increases in virtue, and the Earth, which is the middle between them, increases in weight. Therefore, let every searcher of this Art <note>The "Art" refers to the practice of Alchemy.</note> decide in his mind what he seeks for out of these three, and let him assist it in overcoming its contrary; and then let him add his own weight to the weight of Nature, so that the defect of Nature may be made up by Art, so that she may overcome what is contrary to her.

# A New Light of Alchemy

Air acts as a mediator; if water is the dominant element, it produces things that are corruptible and last only a short time. But if fire prevails, it produces things that are lasting and incorruptible. Consider, therefore, what is necessary for your work. Furthermore, know that fire and water exist in everything. However, neither fire nor water alone creates anything, because they only struggle and compete with one another regarding their speed and power <note>original: "vertue"</note>. This does not happen by their own power, but through the stimulation of an internal <note>original: "intrinsecall"</note> heat, which is kindled in the center of the earth by the movement of heavenly influences <note>original: "vertues"</note>. Without this heat, those two elements could never accomplish anything; they would both remain still within their own boundaries and weight.

But Nature afterward joins the two in proper proportions and stirs them up with internal heat. They then begin to contend with one another, each calling upon its own kind for help. Thus they ascend and increase until the earth can rise no higher with them. In the meantime, these two are refined <note>original: "subtilized"; the process of making a substance more refined, volatile, or spiritual</note> because they are held back by the earth. (For within the earth, fire and water rise incessantly and act through the reserved pores that the air prepares.) From this refinement come flowers and fruit, in which the elements become friends, as you can see in trees. For the more they are refined and purified as they ascend, the better the fruit they produce—especially if they conclude with their powers <note>original: "vertues"</note> equally joined together.

Now then, once these things have been purged and cleansed, ensure that the fire and water are made friends. This will be easily accomplished within their own earth, which

All elements consist in combination. But many practitioners sow straw instead of wheat, and some sow both. Many cast away that which the Philosophers value. Some begin and then stop because of their inconstancy; they seek a difficult Art but want easy labor. They cast away the best things and sow the worst. But just as this Art is concealed in the Preface, so also is the raw material <note>original: "matter"</note> cast away at the very beginning. 

Now we say that this Art is nothing else but the powers <note>original: "vertues"</note> of the elements equally mixed. It is a natural balance of hot, dry, cold, and moist—a joining together of Male and Female, which the same Female produced <note>original: "begot"</note>; that is <note>original: "i. e."</note>, a conjunction of Fire and the radical moisture of metals. By considering that the <term>Mercury of Philosophers</term> has within itself its own good <term>Sulphur</term>, which is more or less purified <note>original: "depurated"</note> and refined <note>original: "decocted"</note> by Nature, you may perfect all things out of <term>Mercury</term>. 

But if you know how to add your own weights to the weights of Nature—to double the <term>Mercury</term> and triple the <term>Sulphur</term>—it will quickly result in something good, then better, until it becomes the best of all. This is true even though there is only one visible <term>Sulphur</term> and two <term>Mercuries</term>, both coming from one root. They should be neither raw <note>original: "crude"</note> nor over-boiled, yet purged and dissolved, if you have understood me.

It is not necessary to describe the substance of the <term>Mercury of Philosophers</term> or the substance of their <term>Sulphur</term>. No mortal man ever could, nor ever hereafter shall be able to describe it more openly and clearly than it has already been described and named by the Ancient Philosophers—unless he wishes to be an outcast <note>original: "Anathema"</note> from the Art. Indeed, it is named so commonly that it is not truly valued; therefore, students of this Art focus their minds instead on other subtleties...

It does not emerge into the light, for it is naked, yet it is covered by Nature in a wonderful manner.

To conclude, we repeat that Sulphur and Mercury are the source <note>original: "Mine"</note> of our Quicksilver (when joined together). That Quicksilver can dissolve metals, kill them, and bring them to life; it receives this power from that sharp Sulphur which is of its own nature. 

But so that you may understand it even better, listen to me while I tell you the difference between our Quicksilver and common quicksilver, or mercury. Common mercury does not dissolve gold or silver in a way that prevents it from being separated from them again. However, our *Argent vive* <note>original: "Argent vive"; Latin for "living silver," a term for quicksilver or the essential spirit of mercury.</note> dissolves gold and silver and is never separated from them, but is like water mixed with water. Common mercury has a combustible, "evil" sulphur which turns it black; but our *Argent vive* contains incombustible sulphur that is fixed, good, white, and red. Common mercury is cold and moist; our Mercury is hot and moist. Common mercury makes bodies black and stains them; our *Argent vive* makes bodies as white as crystal. Common mercury is turned by precipitation <note>A chemical process where a solid settles out of a liquid solution.</note> into a citrine <note>Yellow or lemon-colored.</note> powder and an evil sulphur; our *Argent vive*, by the power of heat, is turned into a most white sulphur that is good, fixed, and fluxible <note>Capable of being melted or flowing easily.</note>. The more common mercury is decocted <note>Heated or boiled to extract an essence or achieve a state of maturity.</note>, the more...

It is fluid: our <term>Argent vive</term> <note>original: "Argent vive" — literally "living silver," the archaic term for quicksilver or mercury.</note> is unique because the more it is refined <note>original: "decocted" — a process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence.</note>, the more it thickens. By these circumstances, you may consider how <term>Common Mercury</term> differs from the <term>Mercury of the Philosophers</term>. If you do not yet understand, do not expect to, for no mortal man will ever speak more plainly and clearly than we have done. 

Now, let us speak of its virtues. Our <term>Argent vive</term> is of such power that it is sufficient for you and for itself without the addition of any external thing; it is dissolved and solidified by a natural refining process alone. However, for the sake of brevity, philosophers add its own well-digested and ripened <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle that acts upon the passive Mercury.</note> to it, and that is how they work.

We could cite the writings of other philosophers to confirm what we have said, but because we have written more clearly than what is found in their writings, they do not need any confirmation; he who looks into other men's writings shall understand this. Therefore, if you will follow our advice, we counsel you that before you apply yourself to this <term>Art</term> <note>The "Art" refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>, you first learn to hold your tongue. You must also inquire into the nature of mines and metals, as well as vegetables, for you shall find our <term>Mercury</term> in every subject. The <term>Mercury of the Philosophers</term> may be extracted from all things, although it is nearer at hand in one subject than in another. 

Know also for certain that this Art is not based on luck or accidental invention, but in a real science. There is but this one matter in the world by which, and of which, the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> is made. It is indeed in all things, but in extracting it, a man's life would fail and not be sufficient. Yet without the knowledge of natural things,

Truly, such a person seeks after the Art only by chance; and although it often happens that a man may by accident come across the matter of our *quicksilver* <note>original: *Argent vive*</note>, he then ends the work when he should begin. Thus, just as he found it by chance, he loses it by chance, because he does not know the foundation upon which he should base his intentions. 

Therefore, this Art is the gift of the most high God; unless God reveals it by means of a sharp mind or a friend, it is hardly known. For we cannot all be like *Geber* <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–813), a prolific Islamic alchemist and chemist.</note> or *Lully* <note>original: *Lullius*; referring to Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and major figure in medieval alchemy.</note>. And although *Lully* was a man of keen intelligence, if he had not received the Art from *Arnold* <note>original: *Arnoldus*; Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), an influential physician and alchemist.</note>, he certainly would have been like those who find the Art with great difficulty. So also *Arnold* confesses that he received it from a friend. 

For it is easy to write what Nature dictates. There is a proverb: *It is easy to add to things already discovered.* Every art and science is easy for the master, but not for a young scholar. To find this Art, a long time is required, along with many vessels, great expense, and continual labor joined with much meditation; although to the person who knows it already, all things are easy and light. 

To conclude, therefore, we say that this Art is the gift of God alone. Once it is known, God must also be prayed to so that He would give His blessing to the Art; for without this divine blessing, it would be of no use and unprofitable. We ourselves have experienced this, having undergone great dangers because of this Art; indeed, we have suffered more mischief and misfortune from it than advantage. But there is a time when men become wise too late.

The judgments of the Lord are a great depth. Yet I marveled at divine providence in these, my misfortunes; for I always had the protection of our great Creator at hand, so that no enemy could be too hard for me or repress me. The Angel of the Lord, the guardian of this Treasury <term>The "Treasury" likely refers to the secret knowledge of the Philosopher's Stone or the hidden mysteries of Nature</term>, has always been my keeper. Into this Treasury, the most High Creator has safely locked this treasure alone, which He will always defend and secure.

For I have heard that my enemies have fallen into the very snare they laid for me. Those who would have taken away my life have lost their own; and some of those who would have taken away my goods have lost kingdoms. Moreover, I know many who sought to slander <note>original: "detracted from"</note> my good name who have perished in disgrace. I have always been so greatly preserved by the great Creator of all things, who took me from my mother's womb, placed me under the shadow of His wings, and infused me with the Spirit of understanding all natural things—to whom be praise and glory forever and ever. 

I have received such great blessings from the most High God, our Creator, that it is impossible not only for my pen, but even for my mind to comprehend them. God has hardly ever conferred greater things upon any mortal man—indeed, perhaps nothing so great. I wish I had such devotion, spirit, eloquence, and wisdom that I might give Him fitting <note>original: "meet"</note> thanks; for I know we have not deserved such great things. I only believe myself to be this: one who has always trusted in Him alone, does trust Him now, and shall trust Him always. For I know that no mortal man is able to help me, only this God and our Creator; for it is a futile thing to trust in princes, because they...

...are men (as the *Psalmist* says) and that all these receive their breath of life from God, and when this is taken away, they return to dust. However, it is a safe and secure thing to trust in the Lord God, from whom—as from the fountain of goodness—all good things flow most abundantly. 

Therefore, you who desire to attain to this Art, in the first place put your whole trust in God your Creator and urge Him with your prayers; then, believe assuredly that He will not forsake you. For if God knows that your heart is sincere and your whole trust is placed in Him, He will—by one means or another—show you a way and assist you in it, so that you shall obtain your desire. The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom. Pray, but also work: God indeed gives understanding, but you must know how and when to use it. For just as a good understanding and a good opportunity are the gifts of God, so too is the punishment for sin when a good opportunity is ignored.

But to return to our purpose: we say that <term>Argent vive</term> <note>original: "Argent vive"; literally "living silver," referring to mercury or quicksilver</note> is the first matter of that work, and truly nothing else; whatever is added to it arises from it. We have often said that all things in the world are made and generated from the <term>three Principles</term> <note>The primary alchemical elements: Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt</note>; however, we purge certain things of their <term>accidents</term> <note>In historical philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential properties—like color, smell, or surface impurities—that do not change the core essence of a substance</note> and, once purged, join them together again. By adding what needs to be added, we supply what is lacking; and by imitating Nature, we boil them to the highest degree of perfection—something Nature could never achieve because of these accidents. Thus, Nature ends where Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy</note> must begin. Moreover, if you desire to imitate Nature, imitate her in the ways she works. And do not let it trouble you that our writings seem to contradict one another in some [places]...

# A New Light of Alchemy

places; for so it must be, lest the Art be too plainly disclosed. But you must make a choice of those things which agree with Nature; take the roses and leave the prickles. If you intend to make a metal, let a metal be your groundwork, because from a dog nothing is generated but a dog, and from metal, nothing but a metal. For know for certain, if you do not take the radical moisture <term>the essential fluid or "life-spirit" believed to be contained within matter</term> out of the metal, well-separated, you shall never do anything. Without grains of wheat, you shall till your ground in vain; there is but one thing, but one Art, but one operation. If, therefore, you wish to produce a metal, you shall ferment with a metal; and if you wish to produce a tree, let the seed of the tree be your ferment. There is, as we said, but one operation, and besides it, there is no other that is true. 

All those, therefore, are mistaken who say that any particular thing <term>a partial alchemical success or a specific shortcut</term>, besides this one way and natural matter, is true; for a bough is not to be had except from the trunk of a tree. It is an impossible and senseless thing to go about trying to produce a bough; it is easier to make the Elixir itself <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or universal medicine</note> than any "particular" thing—even the most simple—that will be advantageous and withstand a natural examination and trial. 

Yet there are many who boast they can fix <term>to make a volatile substance solid or resistant to the heat of fire</term> silver, but it would be better for them if they could fix lead or tin. In my judgment, it is all the same labor, because lead and tin do not resist the trial of the fire while they remain in their own nature; but silver is, in its nature, fixed enough already and does not need any sophistical <note>original: "Sophisticall"—meaning deceptive, artificial, or chemically "faked"</note> fixation. But seeing there are as many opinions as there are men, we will leave everyone to his own opinion. Let him who will not follow our counsel and imitate Nature continue in his error.

# A New Light of Alchemy

Indeed, particulars <note>original: "particulars" — in alchemy, these refer to limited or "minor" transmutations that produce specific results, rather than the universal "Great Work" or Philosopher's Stone.</note> may easily be made if you have a tree whose young twigs may be grafted into various trees. For example, if you have one pot of water, different kinds of meat may be boiled in it; depending on the variety of the meat, the broth will have its own taste, yet all come from the same foundation. We conclude, therefore, that there is but one *Nature*, both in <term>metals</term> <note>original: "Metalls"</note> and in other things, though her operations are diverse. There is also one universal matter according to *Hermes*. *So, from this one thing, all things proceed.* <note>A reference to the *Emerald Tablet* of Hermes Trismegistus, a foundational text for alchemists.</note>

Yet there are many *Artificers* <note>original: "Artificers" — here meaning practicing alchemists or craftsmen.</note> who each follow their own fancy. They seek a new *Nature* and a new matter; in the end, they find a "new nothing" because they interpret the *writings* of *philosophers* according to the literal word rather than the possibilities of *Nature*. All these belong to that *Assembly* mentioned in the *Dialogue of <term>Mercury</term> with the <term>Alchemist</term>* <note>A satirical and instructive alchemical text commonly attributed to Michael Sendivogius.</note>, who return home without any *conclusion*. They look for the end result without a middle process or even a beginning. The reason for this is that they do not try to master the <term>art</term> <note>original: "Art" — the "Sacred Art" of alchemy.</note> through the *principles* and *foundations* of the *philosophers*, nor by reading their books.

Instead, they rely on reports and "recipes" from *mountebanks* <note>original: "Mountebankes" — charlatans or frauds who sold false secrets and fake medicines.</note>. (Though indeed, the books of the *philosophers* have perhaps been corrupted by the envious, who added material in some places and removed it in others.) Later, when things do not succeed, they turn to <term>sophistications</term> <note>original: "Sophistications" — deceptive chemical techniques used to create imitations of gold or silver.</note> and attempt various useless operations: making "whites," making "reds," "fixing" <term>silver</term>, and trying to extract the "soul" of <term>gold</term>. These methods were sufficiently refuted in the *Preface* to the *Twelve Treatises* <note>Referring to *The Twelve Treatises on the Philosophers' Stone*, the main body of this work.</note>. We do not deny—indeed, we say—that it is al-
