## OF

# SULPHUR:

**THE** philosophers have not undeservedly placed Sulphur in the first place amongst the three principles, as being the most worthy principle, in the knowledge of which the whole Art consists. Now there is a threefold Sulphur, and that which is to be chosen above the rest is: a Sulphur tingeing <note>original: "tinging"; meaning to dye or color permanently</note> or coloring; a Sulphur congealing Mercury; and the third is essential and ripening. 

Of these we ought to treat seriously; but because we have set forth one of the principles by way of a dialogue, so also we shall conclude the rest, lest we should seem to be partial and detract from either of them. Sulphur is more mature than any of the other principles, and Mercury is not coagulated <note>solidified or thickened</note> except by Sulphur. Therefore, our whole operation in this Art is nothing else but to know how to draw forth that Sulphur out of metals, by which our <term>Argent vive</term> <note>original: "Argent vive"; literally "living silver," the archaic term for Quicksilver or Mercury</note> in the bowels of the Earth is congealed into gold and silver.

<summary>This page introduces Sulphur as the most important of the three alchemical principles, identifying its three forms and its essential role in solidifying mercury into precious metals.</summary>

<keywords>Sulphur, Alchemy, Three Principles, Mercury, Argent vive, Gold, Silver, Transmutation, Coagulation</keywords>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the fundamental components of alchemy, specifically the roles of Sulphur and Mercury.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>A New Light of Alchemy.</header>

This <term>Sulphur</term> is indeed the male in this work, while <term>Mercury</term> takes the place of the female. From the combination and interaction of these two, the "mercuries of the <term>philosophers</term>" <note>original: Mercuries of Philosophers; this refers to refined alchemical substances rather than common quicksilver.</note> are produced.

We told you in the *Dialogue of <term>Mercury</term> with the Alchemist* about an assembly of alchemists who met together to discuss what substance the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> should be made from and how to make it. We also mentioned how, by the misfortune of a storm, they were scattered across almost the entire world without reaching any conclusion. For a powerful storm and a very great wind arose, which scattered them all. It blew through the heads of some so fiercely that even now they have not recovered; as a result, various types of "worms" <note>In early modern medicine and metaphor, "worms in the brain" referred to delusions, obsessions, or madness.</note> have bred in their minds. 

Among them were men of various opinions and conditions. Among the rest was the Alchemist I will discuss in this treatise. He was otherwise a good man, but he was undecided and lacked a clear conclusion. He was one of those who set out to find the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> by chance. He was also a companion to the philosopher who argued with <term>Mercury</term>. 

Now, this man said, "If I had been lucky enough to speak with <term>Mercury</term>, I would have drained him of all his secrets <note>original: fished him dry</note> in just a few words. That other man," he said, "was a fool; he did not know how to handle him. <term>Mercury</term> never pleased me, nor do I think there is any value in it. But I approve of <term>Sulphur</term>, because we discussed it excellently at that meeting. If that storm had not disturbed us, we would have concluded that it was the 'first matter' <note>original: first matter; the primary substance from which all things are believed to originate in alchemical theory.</note>, for I am not accustomed to troubling myself with..."

He began therefore to distill it, to sublime, to calcine <term>calcine: to reduce a substance to powder or ash by heating</term>, and to fix it; he even tried to make oil *by the bell* <note>original: *per Campanam*; a method of distillation using a bell-shaped glass cover to collect vapors</note> from it, sometimes by itself and sometimes with crystals and eggshells. He tried various other operations with it, and when he had spent much time and money and could find nothing to suit his purpose, he was sad. In his miserable state of perplexity, he passed many nights without sleep. He also often went out of the city to observe nature, so that he might more easily devise a certain method for his work.

It happened once, as he was walking about, that he fell into an ecstasy while observing things and came to a certain green **wood** full of all kinds of things. In it were **mines** of all **minerals** and **metals**, and all kinds of beasts and birds, and an abundance of **trees**, **herbs**, and fruit. There were also various water conduits, for in those places no water was to be had except what was brought there by various instruments and pipes, crafted by different artisans from various places. The most important water, clearer than the rest, was drawn by the beams of the moon; this was obtained only for the Nymph of the Wood. **Bulls** and **rams** also fed there, and the shepherds were two young men. The alchemist asked them, "**Whose wood is this?**" They answered him, saying, "**This is the wood and garden of our nymph,** ***Venus***." The alchemist walked up and down in it, and the place pleased him well; yet he still thought of his sulfur. Eventually, becoming weary from walking, and

In a sad condition, he sat by the side of a canal <note>original: "channel," likely a waterway or stream</note> under a certain tree. He began to lament most miserably, bewailing his time and the expenses <note>original: "charges"</note> which he had spent in vain on his work. Otherwise, he could not have helped but deceive others while only harming <note>original: "damnified"</note> himself. He said, "What is this? All men say it is a common thing, of small esteem, and easy to find; yet I am a learned man and I cannot find this wretched Stone."

And so, in his lamentation, he began to curse <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur was considered one of the three primary principles of matter in alchemy, representing the soul or the active, combustible principle</note>, because he had spent so much money and labor in vain upon it. <term>Sulphur</term> was also in that wood, but this was unknown to the Alchemist. While he was lamenting in this way, he heard a voice that sounded like an old man’s: "Friend, why do you curse <term>Sulphur</term>?" 

The Alchemist looked every way round about him, and seeing nobody, he was afraid. But that voice spoke to him again: "Friend, why are you so sad?" 

The Alchemist, taking courage, said, "Sir, just as the hungry man is always thinking about bread, so do I always think about the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term>." 

*Voice* <note>original: "Vox"</note>: "And why do you curse <term>Sulphur</term>?"

*Alchemist* <note>original: "Alch."</note>: "Sir, I believed that it was the first matter of the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term>, and therefore, after working upon it for many years, I have spent much but could not find that Stone."

*Voice:* "Friend, truly I know that <term>Sulphur</term> is the true and principal subject of the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term>, but I do not know you, nor anything of your labor and intention. You curse <term>Sulphur</term> without cause, because he is in a cruel prison and cannot be at hand for everybody. He is kept bound in a most dark dungeon and does not go out except where his keepers carry him."

*Alchemist:* "And why is he imprisoned?" 

*Voice:* "Because he would be obedient to every Alchemist and do what they would have him do, even if it were contrary to his..."

It was his mother's will, who forbade him to obey anyone except those who knew her; therefore, she put him into prison and commanded that his feet should be bound. She set keepers over him so that, without their knowledge and permission, he should go nowhere. 

***Alch.*** O wretch! For this reason, he could not come to me. Truly, his mother does him a great wrong. And when shall he be let out of those prisons? 

***Vox.*** O friend! The <term>Sulphur</term> of the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>Sulphur here refers not to common brimstone, but to the active, "combustible," or masculine principle in alchemy.</note> cannot go forth from here except after a long time and with a great deal of labor. 

***Alch.*** Sir! And who are the keepers who guard him? 

***Vox.*** Friend! His keepers are of the same stock, but they are tyrants. 

***Alch.*** And who are you, and what is your name? 

***Vox.*** I am the judge and governor of the prisons, and my name is *Saturn* <note>In alchemy, Saturn represents the metal lead and is associated with containment, heaviness, and the "nigredo" or black stage of the work.</note>. 

***Alch.*** Then *Sulphur* is kept in your prisons. 

***Vox.*** *Sulphur* indeed is kept in my prisons, but he has other keepers. 

***Alch.*** And what does he do in the prisons? 

***Vox.*** He does whatever his keepers want him to do. 

***Alch.*** And what can he do? 

***Vox.*** He is the maker of a thousand things and is the heart of all things. He knows how to improve <term>metals</term> and corrects <term>minerals</term>. He gives understanding to animals and knows how to create all kinds of flowers in herbs and trees, and he rules over them. He corrupts the air, which he then fixes again. He is the creator of all scents and the painter of all colors. 

***Alch.*** From what matter does he make flowers? 

***Vox.*** His keepers provide the matter and the vessels, but *Sulphur* <term>digests</term> <note>In alchemy, digestion refers to the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to mature or transform it.</note> the matter; and according to the variety of his digestion and weight, various flowers and scents are produced. 

***Alch.*** Is he old?

*Alch.* Sir, how is he known? 

*Vox*, In various ways, but best by the state of the vital organs <note>original: "Vitalls"</note> in animals, by the color in metals, and by the scent in vegetables. Without him, his mother accomplishes nothing. 

*Alch.* Is he the sole heir, or does he have brothers? 

*Vox*, His mother has only one son like him; his other brothers are associated with evil things. He has a sister whom he loves and who loves him in return, for she is like a mother to him. 

*Alch.* Sir, is he uniform everywhere? 

*Vox*, According to his nature, yes, but he is changed within his prisons; yet his heart is always pure, even if his garments are stained. 

*Alch.* Sir, was he ever at liberty? 

*Vox*, Yes, especially in those times when there were wise men with whom his mother shared great familiarity and friendship. 

*Alch.* And who were they? 

*Vox*, There were very many. There was *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical texts are attributed</note>, who was almost at one with his mother. After him were many kings and princes, as well as many other wise men in later ages, such as *Aristotle* and *Avicenna* <note>original: "Avicen"; the Persian polymath and physician Ibn Sina</note>, who set him at liberty. These men knew how to loosen his bonds. 

*Alch.* Sir, what did he give them for setting him free? 

*Vox*, He gave them three kingdoms. For when anyone unbinds and releases him, he then overcomes his keepers (who previously governed his kingdom). He delivers these keepers, now bound, to the person who released him to be their subjects, and gives that person their kingdoms to possess. But what is more, in his kingdom there is a *looking glass* <note>mirror</note> in which the whole world is seen. Whoever looks into this glass may see and learn in it three parts of the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how ancient philosophers attained wisdom by studying the "mirror of Nature" rather than relying solely on books. It likely follows a passage ending with the word "there-", completing the word "therefore."</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>

Therefore, they shall possess the wisdom of the whole world and will become very wise in these three kingdoms <note>The three kingdoms of nature: animal, vegetable, and mineral.</note>. Such were Aristotle, Avicenna, and many others, who—just like those before them—saw in this mirror how the world was made. By this, they learned the influences of the celestial powers upon earthly bodies and how Nature compounds things by the "weight of Fire" <note>An alchemical concept referring to the specific proportion of heat or "vital fire" required to create or transform matter.</note>. They also learned the motion of the Sun and Moon, and especially that universal motion by which Nature is governed. Through this, they understood the degrees of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness, as well as the properties of herbs and indeed of all things, which made them the most excellent physicians. 

And truly, unless a physician is someone who knows why a specific herb is hot, dry, or moist in a certain degree—not just from the books of Galen or Avicenna, but from the original source of Nature, where those authors also found their understanding—he cannot be a well-grounded physician. They diligently considered all these things and bequeathed their writings to their successors so that people might be inspired toward studies of a higher nature and learn how to set "Sulphur" <note>original: "Sulphur" — in alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, masculine, and transformative principle within matter.</note> at liberty and release it from its bonds. 

But the people of this age take those writings as their final authority and seek no further; it is enough for them if they can simply say, "So says Aristotle," or, "Thus says Galen."

***Alchemist:*** And what do you say, sir? Can an herb be known without a herbal <note>original: "Herball" — a book containing the names and descriptions of plants and their medicinal properties.</note>?

***Voice:*** Those ancient philosophers wrote their prescriptions from the very fountain of Nature.

***Alchemist:*** How, sir?

***Voice:*** Know that all things in the earth and upon the earth are generated and produced from three principles <note>original: "three Principles" — likely the *Tria Prima* of Paracelsus: Salt (body), Sulphur (soul), and Mercury (spirit).</note>; sometimes from two, to which the third is joined. ...chemist, being weary, there appeared to him this vision: he saw in that wood a fountain full of water, around which walked <term>Sal</term> <note>original: Sal; Salt, which in alchemy represents the principle of body, fixity, and earthiness</note> and <term>Sulphur</term> <note>original: Sulphur; representing the principle of soul, inflammability, and the "active" seed</note>, contending with one another until at last they began to fight. The <term>Prince</term> said to them, "Why do you not help *Diana*, that Virgin?" They replied, "Sir, this river is indeed small, and as if dried up, but most dangerous. Once upon a time, we were willing to go into it without your knowledge, and we hardly escaped the danger of eternal death; we know also that others who were our predecessors perished in it."

She, being willing to save herself, also drew the <term>Prince</term> himself to her, and so both were drowned. ...been freed from those bodies which are polluted and stained.

***Alchemist:*** But do you ever return into those bodies again?

***Souls*** <note>original: *Animæ*</note>***:*** Not into such filthy bodies. We will return only when they are purified, when this river shall be thoroughly dried up by the heat of the sun, and when this province shall be frequently tested by the Air.

***Alchemist:*** What shall you do in the meantime?

***Souls:*** Here we shall fly upon the river until those clouds and tempests shall cease.

In the meantime, the Alchemist fell into a much-desired dream about his Sulphur. Behold, there appeared to him many other alchemists coming to that place to seek after Sulphur. When they found the carcass of that Sulphur which had been slain by Salt <note>original: *Sal*</note> near the fountain, they divided it among themselves. When the Alchemist saw this, he also took a portion with them; and so every one of them returned home and began to work with that Sulphur, and they do not give up even to this day.

But **Saturn** <note>In alchemical allegory, Saturn often represents the element lead or the stage of "blackness" and decay (putrefaction) necessary for transformation</note> meets this Alchemist and says to him, "Friend, how is it with you?"

***Alchemist:*** Oh Sir, I have seen many wonderful things; my wife would scarcely believe them! Now I have also found Sulphur. I beseech you, Sir, help me; let us make the Philosopher's Stone.

***Saturn:*** With all my heart, my friend. Prepare the <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: *Argent-vive*, literally "living silver"</note> and the Sulphur, and bring a glass vessel here.

***Alchemist:*** Sir, let us have nothing to do with Mercury <note>original: *Mercury* and *Argent-vive* both refer to Quicksilver</note>, for he is worthless; he has deluded my companion and many others.

***Saturn:*** Without Quicksilver—in whose kingdom Sulphur is the King—the philosophers have achieved nothing, nor do I know what to do without it.

***Alchemist:*** Sir, let us make it from Sulphur alone.

***Saturn:*** Very well then, my friend, but the results will follow accordingly <note>Saturn implies that the experiment will fail or produce poor results if the Alchemist ignores the proper ingredients</note>.

Then they took that Sulphur which the Alche—

[The Alche]mist <note>The OCR "mist" is the completion of the word "Alchemist" from the previous page.</note> found, and they did what the Alchemist would have done: they began to work in various ways and to make experiments with sulfur in the various strange kinds of furnaces which the Alchemist had. But at the conclusion of every operation, nothing was brought forth but matches <note>original: "Matches"; in this period, matches were sticks or cords dipped in sulfur used to transport fire</note>, such as old women usually sell to light candles with. 

Then they began a new work, and sublimed and calcined sulfur <term>To sublime is to heat a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again; to calcine is to heat a substance to high temperatures to remove impurities.</term> according to the Alchemist’s pleasure; but in whatever way they worked it, it turned out as before, for they still made nothing but matches. Then the Alchemist said to Saturn <note>In alchemy, Saturn is the planetary name for the metal Lead, often personified as an old man.</note>, "Truly Sir, I see it will not succeed according to my fancy; I beseech you, work alone on what you know." 

Then Saturn said, "See then, and learn." He then took two Mercuries <note>original: "two Mercuries of a differing substance"; likely referring to the dual nature of the 'philosophical mercury'</note> of different substances but of one origin, which Saturn washed in his own urine <note>In alchemical texts, 'urine' often symbolically referred to a specific acidic or alkaline solution, or 'the water of life'</note>, and called them "Sulfurs of Sulfurs." He mixed the fixed with the volatile <term>Fixed substances are those that remain stable under heat; volatile substances evaporate or transform easily when heated.</term>, and once the composition was made, he put it into its proper vessel. To prevent the sulfur from flying away, he set a keeper <note>A 'keeper' refers to a seal or a method of closing the vessel to ensure it remains airtight (hermetic).</note> over it and then put it into a bath of very gentle heat, according as the matter required, and he performed all things very well. 

Then they made the Philosopher's Stone <term>A legendary substance in alchemy capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</term>, because from the true matter, a true thing must necessarily be made. The Alchemist, being very glad, took the Stone within the glass, and beholding its color—which was like burnt blood—he was amazed. By reason of too much joy, he began to leap; in his leaping, the glass fell out of his hands upon the ground and was broken. And so Saturn vanished away. The Alchemist also, being awakened out of his dream, found nothing in his hands but matches.

...which he made of sulfur <note>original: "brimstone"</note>: but the Stone flew away, and still flies, which is why it is called <term>Volatile</term>. And so that miserable <term>Alchemist</term> learned nothing else by that vision but how to make matches. Afterward, laying aside the Stone, he became a physician; by searching after the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>original: "stone of the Philosophers"</note>, he instead got kidney stones <note>original: "stone of the Kidneys"</note>. Last of all, he led the kind of life that such alchemists are accustomed to, for they mostly become physicians or <term>Quacksalvers</term> <note>original: "Quacksalvers"; a term for uneducated or fraudulent doctors who sell fake remedies</note>. This will happen to all who take up the Art casually without any foundation, but only by hearsay or by means of recipes <note>original: "Receipts"</note>.

Some of them, when things do not succeed, say: "We are wise men; we can hear the grass grow. If the Art were true, we would have possessed it before other men." And so, being bold and shameless <note>original: "having brazen faces"</note>—lest they should be accounted as undeserving men (as indeed they are, and perverse besides)—they condemn and undervalue the Art. This science hates such men, and always shows them the beginning in the end <note>The author suggests they are stuck in a cycle of failure, never progressing beyond the start.</note>. 

Now we grant to the unworthy that this Art is nothing; but to the lovers of virtue, the true searchers, and the Sons of Wisdom, we do most highly commend it, and do affirm it not only to be true, but altogether the truest thing. We have sometimes actually proven this before men worthy of such a sight—I say, before men of both high and low condition. (However, this <term>Medicine</term> <note>In alchemical texts, "Medicine" often refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the Elixir of Life, believed to cure all diseases and transmute metals.</note> was not made by us, but received from a friend, and yet it is most true.) Regarding the searching out of this, we have sufficiently instructed the seekers; if our writings do not please them, let them read those of other authors, which...

...are easier, but with this caution: that whatever they read, they should always compare it with the possibility of Nature, lest they attempt <note>original: "assay"</note> anything contrary to Nature. Nor should they believe, even if it were written in the books of philosophers, that fire does not burn, because this is contrary to Nature. But if it is written that fire has a drying and heating property <note>original: "faculty"</note>, this is to be believed because it is according to Nature. For Nature always agrees with sound judgment, and in Nature nothing is difficult; all truth is plain.

Then also let them learn which things in Nature have an affinity with each other. We believe this can be done more easily through our writings than any other, seeing as we think we have written sufficiently—until someone else comes along who shall set down the whole recipe <note>original: "Receipt"</note> as plainly as making cheese from milk, which it is not lawful for us to do <note>Alchemical authors often claimed they were forbidden by divine or natural law from revealing the "Great Work" too plainly.</note>.

But so that I do not direct everything I say only to the new beginner, we shall also say something to those of you who have now moved past these painful labors. Have you seen that country where a man married a wife, whose nuptials were celebrated in the house of Nature? Have you understood how the common people <note>original: "vulgar"</note> among you have seen this <term>Sulphur</term>? <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle of matter.</note> If, therefore, you want even old women to practice your philosophy, show the <term>dealbation</term> <note>The process of whitening or purifying a substance.</note> of your <term>Sulphurs</term>. Say to the common people, "Come and see, for now the water is divided, and <term>Sulphur</term> has come out; it will return white and congeal the waters." Burn, therefore, <term>Sulphur</term> from incombustible <term>Sulphur</term>, then wash it, and make it white and red, until <term>Sulphur</term> be—

## THE
# CONCLUSION.

Every seeker of this Art <note>original: "searcher of this Art"; here referring to the practice of alchemy</note> must first, with mature judgment, examine the creation, operation, and virtues <note>original: "vertues"; the inherent powers or medicinal properties</note> of the four elements <note>The four elements are Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note> along with their actions. For if he is ignorant of the origin and nature of these, he shall not come to the knowledge of the principles, nor shall he know the true matter of the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life</note>, much less reach any successful conclusion. This is because every end is rooted in <note>original: "terminated upon"</note> its beginning. He who well knows what he begins shall well know what the end will be. For the origin of the elements is the Chaos <note>The formless, primordial state of matter from which the universe was created</note>, out of which God, the Maker of all things, created and separated the elements—a task that belongs to God alone. But from the elements, Nature produces the principles of things; and this is Nature's work, through the will

<meta>of</meta>

<summary>This concluding section emphasizes that an alchemist must understand the origins and operations of the four elements to succeed. It distinguishes between the divine act of creating elements from Chaos and Nature's role in producing the principles of things.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, four elements, Chaos, Philosopher's Stone, creation, principles of nature, Michael Sendivogius</keywords>

--- Page 268 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of how natural substances are formed, specifically focusing on the hierarchy from God to the Elements, then to the Three Principles, and finally to metals and minerals.</meta>

<lang>en</lang>
<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>A New Light of Alchemy</header>

from God alone: From these principles, <term>Nature</term> <note>In this context, Nature is viewed as an semi-intelligent force acting according to God's laws</note> afterward produces minerals and all things. From these materials, the <term>Artist</term> <note>original: "Artist"; the alchemist or practitioner of the art</note> can also do many wonderful things by imitating <term>Nature</term>. Because <term>Nature</term> produces minerals, metals, and all kinds of things out of these principles—which are <term>Salt</term>, <term>Sulphur</term>, and <term>Mercury</term>—it does not simply produce metals directly from the elements. Instead, it works through these principles, which are the middle state between the elements and the metals. Therefore, if <term>Nature</term> does not create those things directly from elements, much less shall Art.

And not only in this example, but in every natural process, a middle state or "middle disposition" must be observed. Therefore, in this treatise, we have sufficiently described the elements, their actions, and their operations, as well as the origin of the principles (because until now, no philosopher has discovered these things more clearly). We have done this so that the well-minded seeker might more easily consider to what degree the <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> differs from metals, and metals from elements. 

For there is a difference between gold and water, but less of a difference between water and mercury, and the least difference of all between mercury and gold. For the "house" <note>In alchemy, a "house" refers to a substance's natural dwelling place or its foundational matrix</note> of gold is mercury, and the house of mercury is water. But <term>Sulphur</term> is that which coagulates mercury; this <term>Sulphur</term> is indeed prepared with the greatest difficulty, but it is even more difficult to find. For this secret consists in the "Sulphur of the Philosophers," which is contained in the inner chambers of mercury. We shall discuss the preparation of this—without which it is useless—hereafter in the section on the third principle, <term>Salt</term>, since here we are treating the power and origin of <term>Sulphur</term>, not its <term>Praxis</term> <note>original: "Praxis"; the practical application or laboratory work</note>.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical process where Nature uses the three principles (Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury) as intermediaries to create metals from elements. It posits that mercury is the "house" of gold and that the true secret of alchemy lies in finding and preparing the "Sulphur of the Philosophers."</summary>
<keywords>Tria Prima, Salt, Sulphur, Mercury, Metallogenesis, Philosopher's Stone, Alchemical Theory, Nature vs Art</keywords>

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of alchemical theory, specifically addressing the distinctions between the mineral, vegetable, and animal kingdoms.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>*A New Light of Alchemy,*</header>
<sig>V</sig>

Therefore, we have not written this treatise to disprove any of the ancient philosophers, but rather to confirm their writings and provide the details they omitted. Since philosophers themselves were only men, they could not be perfectly accurate in all things, nor is one man capable of knowing everything. 

Miracles have also led some men astray from the true path of nature, as we read happened to <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a Dominican friar and polymath often associated with alchemical legends.</note> *Albertus Magnus*, a very clever <note>original: "witty"</note> philosopher. He wrote that in his time, grains of gold were found between the teeth of a dead man in his grave. He could not explain this miracle, but judged it to be caused by the "mineral virtue" within man. He was confirmed in this opinion by the saying of *Morien* <note>Morienus, a legendary hermit-alchemist said to have taught the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>: "*And this matter, O King, is extracted from you.*" 

However, this is a mistake, for *Morien* intended those things to be understood philosophically. The mineral virtue is placed in its own kingdom, just as the animal virtue is in its own kingdom. As we have distinguished in the book of the *Twelve Treatises*, we have divided these into three kingdoms; each of these stands by itself and is multiplied without the entry <note>original: "ingresse"</note> of anything else. 

It is true indeed that in the animal kingdom, <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid, passive, or material principle.</note> acts as the matter, and <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, fiery, or organizing principle.</note> acts as the virtue, but the animal is not mineral. If the virtue of the animal sulphur were not in man, it could not congeal the blood-mercury into flesh and bones. Likewise, if there were not a virtue of vegetable sulphur in the vegetable kingdom, it could not coagulate water (or the vegetable mercury) into herbs and trees. The same is to be understood regarding the mineral kingdom. These three mercuries do not, in fact, differ

<summary>The author argues that the three kingdoms of nature—animal, vegetable, and mineral—operate by the same principles of Mercury and Sulphur but remain distinct, correcting a literal misinterpretation of alchemical metaphors by Albertus Magnus.</summary>
<keywords>Albertus Magnus, Morienus, Three Kingdoms, Alchemy, Mercury, Sulphur, Mineral Virtue, Twelve Treatises</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between Mercury and Sulphur in the generation of metals, specifically addressing a case where gold was allegedly found in a human corpse.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>-> A New Light of Alchemy <-</header>

...in virtue, nor the three <term>Sulphurs</term> <note>original: "Sulphurs"; in alchemy, the active, combustible, and masculine principle of metals</note>, because every <term>Sulphur</term> has a power to coagulate its own <term>Mercury</term>; and every <term>Mercury</term> has a power to be coagulated by its own proper <term>Sulphur</term>, and by no other that is a stranger to it. Now the reason why <term>Gold</term> was found and generated between the teeth of the dead man is this: because in his lifetime, <term>Mercury</term> was conveyed into his infirm body by some <term>physician</term>, either by unction <note>The application of an oil or ointment, often containing mercury</note>, or by <term>Turbith</term> <note>original: "Turbith"; a purgative root used in early modern medicine</note>, or some other way, as the custom and manner was; and it was the nature of <term>Mercury</term> to go up to the mouth and, through the sores there, to be evacuated with the phlegm. 

If, therefore, during such a cure the sick man died, that <term>Mercury</term>, finding no exit, remained in his mouth between his teeth; and that carcass became the natural vessel of the <term>Mercury</term>. So, being shut up fast for a long time, it was congealed into <term>Gold</term> by its own proper <term>Sulphur</term>, being purified by the natural heat of <term>putrefaction</term> <note>Decay or rotting, which alchemists believed produced a specific kind of heat</note> caused by the corrosive phlegm of the man's body. But if mineral <term>Mercury</term> had not been brought in there, <term>Gold</term> could never have been produced. 

And this is a most true example that Nature, in the bowels of the earth, does from <term>Mercury</term> alone produce <term>Gold</term>, <term>Silver</term>, and other <term>metals</term>, according to the disposition of the place, or <term>matrix</term> <note>The "womb" or geological environment where minerals form</note>; for <term>Mercury</term> has within itself its own proper <term>Sulphur</term>, with which it is coagulated into <term>Gold</term>, unless it be hindered by some accident, or does not have the required heat or a confined space. The virtue, therefore, of animal <term>Sulphur</term> <note>original: "Snlphur"; likely a printer's error for "Sulphur".</note> does not congeal <term>Mercury</term> into <term>Gold</term>, but into flesh: for if there were such a virtue in man, it would happen to be so in all bodies; which it does

<sig>V</sig>
<meta>catchword: not.</meta>

<summary>The author explains that gold found in a corpse resulted from medicinal mercury reacting with the body's internal heat and its own innate sulfur during decay, rather than the body itself having the power to create gold. This serves as an analogy for how nature produces metals from mercury within the earth's "matrix."</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Mercury, Sulphur, Gold, medicine, putrefaction, matrix, coagulation, transmutation</keywords>

...not. Many such miracles and accidents occur, which, when not carefully considered by writers, cause readers to fall into errors. Yet the honest seeker must test everything against the possibilities of Nature; if they do not agree with Nature, they must be ignored and set aside.

It is enough for the diligent student that he has heard here what the origin of the Principles <note>In alchemy, the "Principles" usually refer to the tria prima: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note> is—since if the beginning is unknown, the end is always doubtful. In this treatise, we have spoken of these principles to the seeker, not in riddles <note>original: "Ænigmatically"</note> but as clearly as we could and as much as was lawful for us. Through these words, if God enlightens anyone’s mind, they shall know what a successor owes to their predecessors, seeing that this Art is always acquired by the same kind of intellects <note>original: "wits"</note> and temperaments <note>original: "dispositions"</note>. Following this clear manifestation of the Art, we entrust it to the care of God, the most high Creator and our Lord, and commend ourselves, along with all honest-hearted readers, to His grace and infinite mercy. To Him be praise and glory, forever and ever.

# FINIS.

<summary>This concluding page of the treatise advises seekers to test all alchemical claims against the laws of Nature and offers a final blessing, entrusting the "Art" of alchemy to God's care.</summary>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page concludes the treatise "A true Light of Alchemy," providing final warnings and a concluding prayer.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>A true Light of Alchemy <note>original: "Alchymy"</note>.</header>

# A true Light of Alchemy.

Many such mishaps and accidents occur which, because they were not well considered by the authors, cause readers to fall into error. Yet the honest practitioner must apply all things to the possibility of Nature; for if they do not agree with Nature, they must be left alone and set aside <note>original: "waved"</note>.

But for you, Christian reader, who see here briefly the whole counsel of the principles (since the beginning is known, but the end is always doubtful), we have spoken in this treatise—not superficially, but as clearly as we could and as it was lawful for us—to the seeker of this knowledge. If God enlightens your understanding through these means, you will know what it means to follow others. Since this art is always justified by its own "sons"—those who are wise and deep in the art which they know—dedicate this manifestation of it to the honor of God, the most high Creator and our Lord. Commend us, together with all the true and humble keepers of His commandments, to Him for His help and glory, forever and ever, AMEN.

### *Of the generations of Natural things.*

<margin>The generation of natural things is twofold.</margin>
<margin>All things proceed from putrefaction.</margin>
**T**he generation of all natural things is twofold: natural and without art, and artificial, namely <note>original: "viz."</note> by alchemy <note>original: "Alchymie"</note>. Although in general it may be said that all things are naturally generated from the Earth by means of putrefaction <term>the process of decay or decomposition, seen by the author as a necessary stage for new life</term>. For putrefaction is the chief stage and first step toward generation <term>the process of coming into being or being created</term>. Now, putrefaction is

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: occasioned</meta>

<summary>This introductory page defines the two types of generation (natural and artificial) and identifies putrefaction as the essential first step in the creation of all things from the Earth.</summary>
<keywords>generation, alchemy, putrefaction, natural philosophy, origins of life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the fundamental processes of nature, specifically focusing on the role of decay as a catalyst for new life. The previous page likely introduced the concept of natural transformations.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
### Of the Nature of Things. Book 1.

<margin>The cause of putrefaction is a moist heat,</margin> ...occasioned by a moist heat. For a continual moist heat causes putrefaction <note>In this context, putrefaction refers to the natural process of decay or fermentation that breaks down a substance to allow for its transformation.</note> and changes all natural things from their first form and essence—as well as their virtues and efficacy—into another thing. <margin>The power and nature of putrefaction.</margin> For just as putrefaction in the stomach changes and reduces all food <note>original: "meats"</note> into dung, so also putrefaction outside of the stomach in a glass container <note>original: "glasse"</note> changes all things from one form into another: from one essence into another, from one color into another, from one smell into another, from one virtue into another, from one power into another, from one property into another, and generally from one quality into another. 

For it is evident, and proven by daily experience, that many good things which are wholesome and medicinal <note>original: "medicinable"</note> become worthless <note>original: "naught"</note>, unwholesome, and mere poison after putrefaction. Conversely, there are many bad, unwholesome, poisonous, and harmful things which, after their putrefaction, become good; they lose all their unwholesomeness and become wonderfully medicinal. This is because putrefaction produces great results, of which we have a most famous example in the holy Gospel, where Christ says:

> *Unless a grain of wheat is cast into the earth and putrefied, it cannot bring forth fruit a hundredfold.*

From this we must also know that many things are multiplied in putrefaction so as to bring forth excellent fruit. <margin>What putrefaction is.</margin> For putrefaction is the change and death of all things, and the destruction of the first essence of all natural things; from this arises a regeneration and new generation a thousand times better, etc.

Since putrefaction is therefore the first degree and step toward generation <note>The process of creating or bringing something into existence.</note>, it is very necessary that we...

<meta>catchword: know</meta>

<summary>This page explains that putrefaction, caused by moist heat, is the essential first step in the transformation and regeneration of all natural substances. The author argues that this process of decay is necessary to destroy an object's original form so that it can be reborn into a more potent or useful state.</summary>
<keywords>putrefaction, regeneration, generation, transformation, alchemy, moist heat, medicinal properties, essence</keywords>

<header>BOOK I. Of the Nature of Things.</header>
<page-num>3</page-num>

<margin>Putrefaction is the first degree of life.</margin>
...know putrefaction well. Now, there are many kinds of putrefaction, and one produces life <note>original: "generation"</note> in a different manner than another does. One also happens sooner than another. We also said that moisture and 
<margin>Putrefaction takes many forms.</margin>
heat were the first degree and step toward putrefaction, which produces all things, just as a hen does her eggs. Therefore, through and within putrefaction, all mucilaginous phlegm <note>original: "flegme"; refers to a thick, viscous substance</note> and matter is made living, regardless of what it eventually becomes.

<margin>An artificial hatching of chickens.</margin>
You have an example of this in eggs, which contain a mucilaginous humor <note>original: "humour"; a fluid or liquid substance</note>. This humor is putrefied by any kind of moderate, continuous heat and turned into a living chick—not only by the heat of the hen, but by any similar kind of heat. For at such a degree of heat <note>original: "Fire"</note>, eggs may be brought to maturity in a glass vessel and ashes and become living birds. Indeed, any man may ripen an egg in his armpit and hatch a chick as well as a hen.

<margin>The raising of a dead bird to life.</margin>
And here we must take notice of something even greater than this: namely, if that living chick is placed in a glass vessel shaped like a gourd and sealed up, then burnt to powder or ashes in the "third degree" of fire <note>an alchemical term for a specific, intense level of heat</note>, and afterward—while still sealed—is putrefied with the most precise putrefaction of horse dung <note>horse dung was used in alchemy to provide a steady, gentle fermenting heat</note> until it becomes a mucilaginous phlegm, then that phlegm may be brought to maturity and become a renewed and newly-made chick. That is to say, if that phlegm is again enclosed in its former shell or container. This is to revive the dead through regeneration and purification <note>original: "clarification"</note>, which is indeed a great and profound miracle of Nature. According to this process, all birds may be killed and made alive again, being made new; and this is the highest and greatest miracle and mystery of God,

# Of the Nature of Things. Book 1.

...which he ever discovered to mortal man.

<margin>The artificial generation of man.</margin>
We must also know that in this way, humans may be generated without a natural father or mother—that is, not from a woman in the natural way. Instead, by the art and industry of a skillful <term>alchemist</term> <note>original: "Alchymist"; here referring to a practitioner of early chemistry and speculative philosophy</note>, a human may be born and grow, as will be shown later.

<margin>The generation of humans by animals.</margin>
<margin>As the seed is, so is the fruit.</margin>
It is also possible that humans may be born from beasts, according to natural causes; yet this cannot be done without much impiety and heresy. That is to say, if a man should couple with a beast, and that beast should—just as a woman does—receive the <term>semen</term> <note>original: "Sperm"</note> of the man with desire and lust into her <term>womb</term> <note>original: "matrix"</note> and conceive, then the semen necessarily putrefies <note>In alchemy, putrefaction was considered a necessary stage of decay that allowed for new life or transformation to begin.</note>. By the continual heat of the body, a human, and not a beast, is produced from it. For the fruit that is brought forth is always the same as the seed that is sown; unless it were so, it would be contrary to the light of Nature and to philosophy. Therefore, as the seed is, so is the herb that springs from it. So, from the seed of an onion, an onion is produced—not a rose, nor a nut, nor lettuce. Likewise, from wheat, wheat is produced; from barley, barley; from oats, oats; and so it is with all other fruits which have seed and are sown, etc.

<margin>The power of a woman's imagination.</margin>
In the same way, it is also possible, and not contrary to Nature, for an irrational beast <note>original: "bruit"</note> to be produced by a woman and a man. In this case, we are not to judge or condemn the woman as we would the man (as in the former case). She, therefore, is not to be accounted impious or heretical, as if she acted contrary to Nature; instead, it is to be attributed to her imagination. For her imagination is always the cause of it. And

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>BOOK 1. Of the Nature of things.</header>

The imagination of a pregnant woman is so powerful that, in conceiving the seed into her body, she may change her infant in various ways. This is because her "inward stars" <note>original: "starres"; in Paracelsian philosophy, these are internal spiritual forces that mirror the celestial bodies and influence health and formation.</note> are so strongly focused upon the infant that they create an impression and influence upon it. Therefore, while the infant is forming in the mother's womb, it is placed into the hand and will of its mother, like clay in the hand of a potter who shapes and makes what his will and pleasure dictates. Similarly, the pregnant woman forms the fruit in her body according to her imagination and her stars. Therefore, it often happens that from the seed of a man, cattle and other horrific <term>monsters</term> <note>In this historical context, "monsters" refers to any birth—human or animal—that deviates significantly from the natural form.</note> are conceived, depending on how strongly the mother’s imagination is directed upon the embryo, etc.

Now, as you have heard that many different things are generated and brought to life through <term>putrefaction</term> <note>original: "putrefaction"; the process of decay, which early modern science believed could spontaneously generate living organisms like insects or worms.</note>, you must also know that various living creatures are bred from many herbs through putrefaction, as those skilled in these matters are aware.

<margin>All animals that are bred purely from putrefaction are poisonous.</margin>
Here we must also know that all such animals that are bred and made from <term>putrefaction</term> contain some poison and are poisonous. However, some are far more potent than others, and they are poisonous in different ways. You can see this in serpents, vipers, toads, frogs, scorpions, basilisks, spiders, wood-bees, <term>pismires</term> <note>original: "Pismires"; an archaic word for ants.</note>, and many types of worms, such as <term>cankers</term> <note>original: "Cankers"; likely referring to caterpillars or larvae that destroy plants.</note>, maggots, locusts, and so on. All of these are bred in and through putrefaction. Also, various <term>monsters</term> are bred among animals. There are also monsters that are not bred from putrefaction on their own, but are made by "Art in a glass" <note>This refers to alchemical experiments—such as the creation of a homunculus—conducted in laboratory glassware.</note>, as has been said; because they often appear in a very wonderful

# Of the Nature of Things. BOOK I.

...shape and form, fearful to behold. Often, they have many feet, many tails, and many colors. Often they have many heads, like worms with the tails of fishes or feathers, and other unusual forms that have never been seen before.

<margin>What monsters are</margin>
Therefore, not only are all animals that do not have proper parents and are not born of things like themselves considered monsters, but also those which are bred from other things.

<margin>The secret poison of the Basilisk</margin>
So you see it is concerning a Basilisk, which is also a monster—and indeed, a monster above all monsters. None is more to be dreaded than he, because he can kill any man with his mere looks and sight. His poison is above all poisons, to which nothing in the world can be compared. He carries his poison in a most secret manner in his eyes. It is a "conceived" poison <note>original: "conceived poison"; this refers to a toxin generated internally, often through biological or spiritual corruption</note>, not much unlike a menstruating woman, who also carries a secret poison in her eyes. By her looks alone, a looking glass is fouled and tainted. 

Likewise, if she looks upon a wound or an ulcer, she infects it in the same manner and hinders its cure. So also with her breath, as well as her sight, she infects various things, corrupting and weakening them; she does the same with her touch. For you see if she meddles with wine during the time of her menstruation, it is suddenly changed and made thick. The vinegar she meddles with also becomes dead and useless. Similarly, "hot waters" <term>distilled spirits or strong medicinal liquors</term> lose their strength. In like manner, civet <note>a musky substance used in perfumes</note>, amber, "gryse" <term>ambergris</term>, musk, and similar perfumes lose their odor if such a woman carries or handles them. So also gold and corals lose their color, as do many gems and looking glasses...

# BOOK I. Of the Nature of Things.

...foiled therewith, etc. <note>This continues from the previous page's discussion of being overcome by poison.</note> But to return to what I proposed concerning the <term>basilisk</term> <note>original: "Basiliske"; a legendary reptile reputed to be king of serpents and said to have the power to cause death with a single glance</note>: by what reason and in what manner he carries poison in his looks and eyes, you must know that he has that property and poison from menstruating women, as mentioned before. For the <term>basilisk</term> is bred of, and proceeds from, the greatest impurity of a woman—namely, her <term>menstruation</term> <note>original: "Menstrues"</note>—and from the blood of the <term>semen</term> <note>original: "Sperm"</note>, if it is put into a gourd glass and putrefied in horse dung; in which putrefaction a basilisk is brought forth. But who is so courageous and bold as to create one, take him out, and kill him again, unless he first covers and fortifies himself well with mirrors <note>original: "glasses"; likely referring to the legend that a basilisk’s gaze is fatal to itself if reflected</note>? I would persuade no one to do it; indeed, I would advise everyone to be wary of it.

<margin>Monsters do not live long.</margin>
But to proceed in treating of <term>monsters</term>, know that monsters among animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> which are brought forth from other things and not from their own kind seldom live long—especially if they live near or among other animals. This is because, by an innate disposition and God’s will, all monsters are hateful to animals that are born naturally. Likewise, human monsters generated by man seldom live long. The more wonderful and remarkable they are, the sooner they die, so that none survive past the third day among men unless they are immediately carried to some secret place and kept apart from all people. Moreover, you must know that God abhors these kinds of monsters and that they are displeasing to Him. None of them can be saved, since they do not bear the image of God. From this, we can conclude nothing else but that they are so formed...

# Of the Nature of Things. Book I.

<margin>*Monsters come from the Devil.*</margin>
...by the Devil, and are made for the Devil's service, rather than God's, because no good work was ever done by any monster, but rather all manner of evil, wickedness, and devilish deceits. For as an executioner marks those under his power <note>original: "his sons," likely referring to those subject to his punishment</note> by cutting off their ears, putting out their eyes, burning their cheeks, fingers, or hands, and cutting off their heads—so does the Devil mark his "sons" through the imagination of their mothers, which they drew from evil desires, lusts, and thoughts <note>original: "cogitations"</note> during conception.

<margin>*Monsters are to be avoided.*</margin>
Also, all people are to be avoided who have extra limbs, lack any limb, or have a double limb. For that is an omen <note>original: "presage"</note> of the Devil's influence and a most certain sign of some hidden <term>occult</term> wickedness and deceit which follows from it. Therefore, they seldom die without facing the executioner, or at least bearing some mark made by him.

<margin>*The artificial generation of men:*</margin>
But we must by no means forget the generation of artificial men. For there is some truth in this matter, although it has been concealed for a long time, and many doubts and questions have been raised by some of the ancient philosophers: whether it was possible for Nature or Art to beget a man outside the body of a woman and a natural womb <note>original: "matrix"</note>? To this I answer that it is in no way contrary to the <term>Art of Alchemy</term> and Nature; indeed, it is very possible. But to achieve it, we must proceed as follows:

Let the sperm of a man by itself be putrefied in a <term>gourd glass</term> <note>a flask or cucurbit used in distillation</note>, sealed up, at the highest degree of putrefaction in horse dung <note>horse manure was frequently used by alchemists as a source of steady, gentle heat</note> for the space of forty days, or until it begins to come alive, move, and stir, which may easily be seen. After this time, it will be something like a human, yet transparent, and with—

<margin>Fairies, nymphs, giants, etc., are made from artificial men.</margin>

This is because through Art <note>meaning alchemy or technical skill</note> they receive their lives; through Art they receive their bodies, flesh, bones, and blood; through Art they are born. Therefore, Art is now incorporated with and inbred within them, and they do not need to learn from anyone, but others are compelled to learn from them. For through Art they have their origin and current existence, like a rose or flower in a garden, and they are called the children of <term>Fairies</term> and <term>Nymphs</term> because, in power and virtue, they...

# Of the Nature of Things. BOOK I.

<margin>Of the three principles: Mercury is the Spirit, Sulphur is the Soul, and Salt is the Body.</margin>
&emsp;&emsp;Here it is necessary that we speak something of the generation of <term>metals</term> <note>The creation or "birth" of metallic ores within the earth</note>; but because we have written sufficiently of that in our book on the generation of <term>metals</term>, we shall treat it very briefly here, adding only what was omitted in that book. Know that all seven <term>metals</term> are produced in this manner out of a threefold matter: namely <note>original: *viz.*</note> <term>Mercury</term>, <term>Sulphur</term>, and <term>Salt</term>, yet in distinct and peculiar colors. For this reason *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical texts are attributed</note> did not speak wrongly when he said that all seven <term>metals</term> are produced and compounded of three substances, as are also the <term>Tinctures</term> and the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term>. Those three substances he calls the <term>Spirit</term>, <term>Soul</term>, and <term>Body</term>, but he did not show how this is to be understood, or what he meant by this, although perhaps <note>original: haply</note> he might have known the three Principles but did not mention them. Therefore, we do not say that he was in error here, but only that he remained silent. Now, so that those three distinct substances may be rightly understood—namely, <term>Spirit</term>, <term>Soul</term>, and <term>Body</term>—we must know that they signify nothing other than the three Principles: that is, <term>Mercury</term>, <term>Sulphur</term>, and <term>Salt</term>, from which all seven <term>metals</term> are generated. For <term>Mercury</term> is the <term>Spirit</term>, <term>Sulphur</term> is the <term>Soul</term>, and <term>Salt</term> is the <term>Body</term>. But a <term>metal</term> is the <term>Soul</term> between the <term>Spirit</term> and the <term>Body</term> (as *Hermes* says), which <term>Soul</term> indeed is <term>Sulphur</term>; it unites these two contraries, the <term>Body</term> and <term>Spirit</term>, and changes them into one essence, and so forth.

<margin>Whether metals can be made artificially by fire.</margin>
&emsp;&emsp;Now, this is not to be understood as meaning that the seven <term>metals</term>, the <term>Tincture</term>, or the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> may be generated from just any <term>Mercury</term>, <term>Sulphur</term>, or <term>Salt</term> through the <term>Art of Alchemy</term> or human industry with the help of <term>fire</term>; but all the seven...

The author clarifies and expands upon the teachings of Hermes Trismegistus regarding how these principles unite to form a single essence.</summary>

<lang>en</lang>
<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>BOOK 1. Of the Nature of things.</header>
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the origins of natural substances; this page continues with the generation and transmutation of metals. The catchword "duced" indicates the text continues with "produced" on the next page.</meta>

Metals must be generated in the mountains by the <term>Archeus</term> <note>original: Archeius; in Paracelsian philosophy, the "inner chemist" or vital force that directs the growth and development of all things</note> of the Earth. For the alchemist will sooner transmute metals than generate or create them from scratch.

<margin>Living mercury is the mother of metals.</margin>
Nevertheless, living mercury <note>original: ☿; the chemical symbol for mercury or quicksilver, representing the fluid, volatile principle in alchemy</note> is the mother of all the seven metals, and it may deservedly be called the Mother of Metals. For it is an "open" metal, and just as it contains all colors which it reveals in the fire, it also secretly contains all metals within itself; however, without fire, it cannot show them, etc.

<margin>The regeneration of metals into tinctures.</margin>
But the generation and renewal of metals is achieved in this way: just as a man may return into the womb of his mother—that is, into the Earth, out of which he was first made—and shall be raised again at the Last Day, so also all metals may return into living mercury again. They become mercury once more and are regenerated and purified by fire if, for the space of forty weeks, they are kept in a continual heat, like an infant in its mother's womb. Thus, what are brought forth are not common metals, but <term>tinging metals</term> <note>metals transformed into "tinctures" or "elixirs" capable of changing the properties of other base metals</note>. For if silver is regenerated in the manner we have described, it will afterward "tinge" (transform) all other metals into silver; likewise, gold will transform them into gold, and the same is to be understood for all the other metals.

<margin>The soul is the medium by which the spirit is united to the body.</margin>
Now, since <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure to whom the foundational texts of alchemy are attributed</note> said that the soul alone is the medium which joins the spirit to the body, he did not say so without cause. For seeing that <term>Sulphur</term> <note>the alchemical principle of the "soul," representing flammability, oilyness, and the active power of ripening</note> is that soul and, like fire, ripens and digests all things, it can also bind the spirit with the body, incorporating and uniting them together so that from there may be pro-

<sig>Bb 2</sig>

<summary>This page explains that metals are naturally generated by the Earth's internal force (the Archeus) and can be alchemically "reborn" by returning them to a state of living mercury. Through a forty-week process of heat, metals are transformed into "tinging metals" or tinctures that possess the power to transmute other substances.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Archeus, living mercury, transmutation, tinging metals, Sulphur, Hermes Trismegistus, regeneration, metalwork</keywords>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Of the Nature of things. LIB. I.</header>

...produced a most excellent body. Now, common combustible sulfur is not to be mistaken for the soul <note>original: "foule"; likely a printing error or OCR misreading of "soule" (soul).</note> of metals, for the soul is a different kind of thing than a combustible and corruptible body.

<margin>What the soul of metals is.</margin>
Therefore, it can be destroyed by no fire, seeing that it is actually fire itself. Indeed, it is nothing else but the quintessence <note>The pure, concentrated essence of a substance.</note> of sulfur, which is extracted out of reverberated sulfur <note>Sulfur that has been heated in a specialized furnace to refine it.</note> by the spirit of wine <note>An early term for alcohol or ethanol.</note>. It has a red color and is as transparent as a ruby; it is indeed a great and excellent <term>Arcanum</term> <note>original: *Arcanum*; a profound secret or a powerful "inner" mystery of nature.</note> for the transmuting of white metals <note>Usually referring to silver or tin.</note>, and for coagulating living mercury <note>original: ☿ (the symbol for Mercury).</note> into fixed and true gold. Regard this as an enriching treasure, and you may be well contented with this single secret in the transmutation of metals.

<margin>The generation of metals and minerals.</margin>
Concerning the generation of minerals and semi-metals, nothing else needs to be known than what was first said concerning metals: namely <note>original: *viz.* (videlicet).</note> that they are produced in a similar way from the three principles—Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt—though not as perfect metals, but from more imperfect and baser forms of Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt, yet each with their own distinct colors.

<margin>The origin of gems.</margin>
The generation of gems comes from the subtlety of the Earth, produced from transparent and crystalline Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt, according to their distinct colors.

<margin>Also the origin of common stones.</margin>
But the generation of common stones comes from the subtlety of water, produced from mucilaginous <note>Thick, sticky, or glue-like.</note> Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt. For from the mucilaginous nature of water all stones are produced, as well as sand and gravel.

<summary>This page defines the "soul of metals" as an indestructible, fire-like quintessence of sulfur used in alchemy to create gold, and explains that all metals, gems, and stones are formed from varying qualities of the three principles: Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt.</summary>

<margin>An artificial way to create stones.</margin>
For any stone put in the water suddenly draws a slimy substance <note>original: mucilaginousness</note> to itself. Now, if that slimy matter is taken from this stone and solidified in a glass vessel, it becomes the same kind of stone that was in that water; however, it would require a long time before it would solidify on its own.

## THE SECOND BOOK,

### *Of the growth and increase of natural things.*

<margin>Heat and moisture are the causes of the growth of things.</margin>
**I**t is sufficiently manifest and known to everyone that all natural things grow and are ripened through heat and moisture, which is sufficiently demonstrated by rain and the heat of the sun. For no man can deny that rain makes the earth fruitful, and it is granted by all that all fruits are ripened by the sun.

--- Page 291 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the natural and artificial processes of growth, transitioning from the divine order of nature to the alchemist's ability to replicate these processes to ripen or generate life.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
## LIB. II. Of the Nature of Things.

<margin>An artificial ripening of things.</margin>
Since it is naturally possible by divine decree, who can deny or disbelieve that a person is able—through the wise and skillful art of <term>alchemy</term> <note>original: Alchymie; the early modern study of the transformation of matter</note>—to make that which is barren fruitful, to ripen that which is raw <note>original: crude</note>, and to cause all things to grow and be increased? 

<margin>God has subjected all things to man.</margin>
For the Scripture says that God subjected all creatures to man and put them into his hands as his own, so that he might use them for his necessity and rule over the fish of the sea, the birds <note>original: fowles</note> of the air, and all things upon the earth, nothing excepted. Therefore, man might well rejoice that God should so endow him with the excellence of nature, that all of God's creatures should be forced to obey and be subject to him—especially the whole earth and all things which are bred, live, and move in and upon it. Since we see with our eyes and are taught by daily experience that the more often and more abundantly the rain moistens the earth, and the sun then dries it again with its heat and warmth, the sooner the fruits of the earth come forth and are ripened; indeed, all fruits clearly grow and increase, no matter what time of year it may be. 

<margin>The artificial generation of fruit.</margin>
Let no man from now on wonder that the <term>alchemist</term> also, by repeated <term>imbibition</term> <note>the process of a solid absorbing or soaking up a liquid</note> and <term>distillation</term> <note>the process of purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor and then condensing it</note>, should do the same. For what else is rain but the <term>imbibition</term> of the earth? And what is the heat of the sun but the <term>distillation</term> of the <unclear>sun</unclear>, which draws up those liquids <note>original: humidities</note> again? Therefore, I say it is possible by this kind of art, even in the middle of winter, to bring forth green herbs, flowers, and other fruit from seed and root through earth and water. If this can be done with all herbs and flowers, it may also be done in many other similar things, such as in all mi- <note>Catchword: "neralls"; likely "minerals"</note>

<summary>This page argues that alchemy can replicate natural processes like rain and sunshine to artificially ripen plants or generate growth out of season, citing man's God-given dominion over nature.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, dominion over nature, imbibition, distillation, artificial ripening, agriculture, Paracelsian philosophy</keywords>

minerals, whose imperfect metals by virtue of a mineral water may be brought to maturity through the industry and art of a skillful alchemist.

<margin>The ripening of minerals.</margin>
In like manner, all *marcasites, garnets* <note>original: *Granati*</note>, *zinc* <note>original: *Zineta*</note>, *arsenic, talc, cachymie* <note>an alchemical term for metallic ores or the "dross" of metals</note>, *bismuth* <note>original: *Bisemuta*</note>, *antimony*, etc. (all of which carry with them crude gold and silver), may be so ripened that they may be equalized to the most rich veins of gold and silver, only by this art. So also the elixir and tinctures of metals are brought to maturity and perfected:

<margin>What the growing of the beard of a dead man signifies.</margin>
Seeing therefore, as it has been said, moisture and heat do ripen all things and make them grow, let no one wonder that the beard, hair, or nails of a malefactor <note>a criminal</note> hanging on a gibbet <note>a gallows-like structure used to display the bodies of executed criminals</note> or wheel do for a long time grow; neither let it be accounted for a sign of his innocence, as the ignorant believe, for this is natural and from natural causes. For while any moisture remains in him, his beard, hair, and nails grow, even until the second year, or until he be wholly putrefied, etc.

We must also know that there are many things that grow forever and are increased in size, weight, and virtue in the water and earth, in which they continue good and efficacious—such as metals, *marcasites, cachymie, talc, garnets, antimony, bismuth, gems, pearls, corals*, all stones, and clay. 

<margin>The augmentation of ☉. <note>The alchemical symbol for gold.</note></margin>
So also it may be ordered that gold may grow and be increased in weight and body, if only it be buried in the earth looking towards the East and be always soiled with the fresh urine of a man and pigeons' dung.

<margin>How gold may be generated in a glass.</margin>
It is possible also that gold, through the industry and skill of an expert alchemist, may be so far exalted <note>refined or raised to a higher state of purity</note> that

<meta>This page continues the instructions for a specific alchemical process, describing how a metallic solution can be made to "grow" into a decorative, tree-like structure within a glass vessel.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>BOOK II. Of the Nature of Things.</header>

...so that it may grow in a glass like a tree, with many wonderful boughs and leaves, which is indeed pleasant to behold and most wonderful.

<margin>How the Philosophical Tree is made.</margin>
The process is as follows: Let gold be calcined <term>calcined: reduced to a powder by heat or chemical action</term> with Royal Water <note>original: *Aqua Regis*, a potent mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids</note> until it becomes a kind of powder like chalk. Place this into a gourd-shaped glass vessel and pour fresh Royal Water over it until it is covered to a depth of four fingers' breadth. Then, draw the liquid off again using the third degree of heat <note>in alchemy, this refers to a strong, steady heat, often using a sand bath</note> until no more vapor rises. Pour the water that was distilled off back on, and then distill it off again. Do this until you see the gold rise in the glass and grow in the manner of a tree, having many boughs and leaves. In this way, a wonderful and pleasant shrub is made from gold, which the alchemists call their "Golden Herb" and the "Philosophical Tree." You may proceed in the same manner with silver and other metals, though their calcination must be performed differently using a different Strong Water <note>original: *Aqua fortis*, or nitric acid</note>, which I leave to your own experience. If you are skilled in alchemy, you shall not go wrong in these matters.

<margin>To make an artificial stone of any shape.</margin>
Know also that any flint stone taken from river water may be made very large in a few days by the art of alchemy—a task which the Archeus <note>original: *Archeius*; in Paracelsian philosophy, this is the vital force or "inner alchemist" that directs the growth of things in nature</note> of the waters could scarcely accomplish in many years. To do this, place the flint into a gourd-shaped glass vessel and fill the glass with river water. Distill the water off until not a single drop remains and the stone is dry. Fill the glass again with this water and distill it off once more. Repeat this process until the glass is filled with this stone.

<summary>This page provides practical alchemical instructions for growing a "Philosophical Tree" from gold or silver and a method for artificially enlarging river stones through repeated distillation.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Philosophical Tree, Gold, Silver, Aqua Regis, Aqua fortis, distillation, Archeus, mineral growth, calcination</keywords>

->**[Decorative Marbled Endpaper]**<-

This page features a classic example of hand-marbled paper, a decorative element commonly used for the endpapers and covers of high-quality bookbindings from the 17th through the 19th centuries. <note>Marbled paper was created by floating pigments on a liquid base, combing them into intricate patterns, and transferring the design to a sheet of paper.</note>

The pattern displayed here consists of complex, organic swirls using a palette of deep red and dark brown or black pigments. <note>Beyond their beauty, these patterns were practical; the busy designs helped to mask wear, stains, or structural reinforcements in the book's binding.</note>

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>This page contains no text; it is a decorative marbled paper used for book covers or endpapers.</meta>

->**Decorative Marbled Endpaper**<-

This page consists of a full-page decorative marbled pattern, a signature element of fine bookbinding from the early modern period through the 19th century. The pattern features intricate swirls of deep red and black pigments, highlighted by scattered flecks of metallic gold.

<note>Marbling is a technique of surface design where pigments are floated on a liquid solution (called "size") and manipulated into patterns before being transferred to paper. This particular style is often associated with "Turkish" or "swirl" marbling.</note>

In historical bookbinding, these papers served as endpapers—the sheets of paper pasted to the inside covers of a book. They were used to hide the structural elements of the binding, such as the leather turn-ins and glue, while providing a luxurious decorative finish to the volume.

<summary>This page features a decorative marbled endpaper with red and black swirls and gold flecks, representing a classic technique in historical luxury bookbinding.</summary>

# To the Reader

*Discerning Reader,*

**There is an abundance of know-**
**ledge, yet very little truth**
**is known. The majority of**
**our knowledge is like castles**
**in the air, or groundless**
**fancies.** I know of only
two ways that are ordained for the acquiring 
of wisdom: <term>namely</term> the Book of God <note>The Bible</note> and the Book 
of Nature <note>The physical world and its laws</note>; and these also can only be understood if they are read 
with reason. Many look upon the former 
as something beneath them, and upon the latter as 
a source of atheism, and therefore they neg-
lect both. It is my judgment that just as 
searching the Scriptures is most necessary, 
so without reason it is impossible. Faith with-

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that true wisdom is gained by studying both the Bible and the natural world through the lens of reason, warning against intellectual pride and the fear that science leads to atheism.</summary>
<keywords>wisdom, reason, Book of Nature, Scripture, theology, faith, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing a discussion on the relationship between faith and intellect. The word "out" is the conclusion of "without" from the preceding page.</meta>

<header>The Epistle</header>

...without reason is but implicit. *If I cannot understand by reason how everything is, yet I will see some reason that a thing is so, before I believe it to be so.* I will ground my belief in the <term>Scriptures</term> upon reason; I will improve my reason through <term>Philosophy</term> <note>In the 17th century, "philosophy" often referred to "Natural Philosophy," or what we now call science and logical inquiry</note>.
