<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "Art of Signatures" from page 139, transitioning from the botanical world to the physiological signs found in humans and animals that reveal health, age, and history.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>

->Of the Nature of things. BOOK IX.<-

<margin>The liver is the source of blood.</margin>
And it is no wonder that the liver can show that by natural signs. For the origin of the blood is in the liver, and from there, through the veins, it is spread into the whole body and is thickened <note>original: "coagulated"; here referring to the biological process of blood turning into solid tissue.</note> into flesh.

Therefore, from a diseased and poorly functioning liver, no healthy or fresh blood can be produced, just as from bad blood, no wholesome flesh can be formed.

Furthermore, even without examining the liver, the state of the flesh and blood may be known. For if both are healthy, they possess their true and natural color, which is red and clear, mixed with no other strange color such as yellow or blue. For those strange colors signify diseases and ailments.

<margin>What the knots in an infant's navel signify.</margin>
There are also other signs worthy of wonder—namely, when the <term>Archeius</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, the internal "alchemist" or vital force that governs the body's functions.</note> acts as the <term>Signator</term> <note>The "Signer"; the natural power that impresses physical marks or "signatures" upon living things to reveal their inner character.</note> and marks the navel of the infant with little knots. By these, it may be predicted how many children the mother of the infant has already birthed or shall birth in the future.

<margin>The branches of a deer's antlers signify its age.</margin>
The same <term>Signator</term> marks the horns of a <term>Hart</term> <note>A male red deer.</note> with branches, by which its age is known. For as many branches as the horn has, that is how many years old the deer is. And seeing as he grows a new horn every year, the age of the deer may be known up to twenty or thirty years.

<margin>What the circles on a cow's horns represent.</margin>
Similarly, the <term>Signator</term> of a cow marks her horns with circles, by which it may be known how many calves she has brought forth. For every circle signifies one calf.

<margin>The teeth of a horse.</margin>
The same <term>Signator</term> brings forth the first teeth of a horse so that for the first seven years, its age may be known by its teeth. For a horse is first born with fourteen teeth, of which it loses two every year, and so in the span of seven years, it loses them all.

<summary>This page describes how the "Art of Signatures" applies to the animal kingdom and human physiology, explaining how internal health is revealed by the color of blood and how physical markers on navels, antlers, and teeth reveal a creature's history or age.</summary>
<keywords>liver, blood, Archeus, Signator, signatures, deer, cow, horse, physiognomy, aging</keywords>

## THE FIRST TREATISE.

### *Of Nature: what she is, and what those who seek her ought to be.*

**M**any wise and very learned men from many ages ago—indeed, as Hermes <note>original: "Hermes," referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure and reputed author of the Hermetic Corpus, which forms the basis of alchemy.</note> testifies, even before the flood—wrote many things concerning the creation of the Philosopher's Stone <term>A legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and sometimes said to be an elixir of life.</term>. They have bequeathed so many writings to us that, unless Nature daily performed acts that are credible to us, hardly anyone would believe it to be true that there was such a thing as Nature at all. This is because in former ages, there were not so many inventors of various theories, nor did our ancestors regard anything besides Nature itself and the possibilities inherent in Nature. And although they were

<summary>This page introduces a treatise on alchemy, asserting that ancient wisdom, dating back to before the biblical flood, focused on the true potential of Nature and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Nature, Hermes Trismegistus, Philosopher's Stone, ancient wisdom, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the wisdom of ancient philosophers. The catchword "ent" on this page suggests the text continues with "ancient" on the following page.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>

<header>A New Light of Alchemy.</header>

...contented with the plain way of nature alone, yet they discovered those things which we—now preoccupied with various other matters—could not conceive with all our intelligence. This is because we consider nature and the generation <note>The creation or production of substances.</note> of things in the world to be common and simple. Therefore, we direct our minds not toward familiar and known things, but toward those things which can be done only with great difficulty, or not at all. Consequently, it happens that we are more skilled at devising strange complexities—things that the [ancient] philosophers themselves never even thought of—than we are at achieving the true process of nature and the correct meaning of the philosophers.

Such is the disposition of human nature: to neglect the things we know and to always be seeking after other things. This is even more true of human intellect and imagination, to which our physical nature is subject. For example: you see that any craftsman <note>original: "Artificer"</note>, when he has reached the highest perfection of his craft, either searches for other crafts, misuses the one he already has, or else abandons it entirely.

In the same way, noble nature is always active and working until its very **Iliad** <gloss>(i) utmost period <note>In the alchemical tradition of Paracelsus, the "Iliad" or "Iliadus" refers to the final limit or the ultimate completion of a natural process.</note></gloss>, and afterward it ceases. For nature was granted a certain permission from the beginning to continually achieve better and better things throughout her entire progress, and to come to her full rest. She moves toward this rest with all her might and rejoices in her end, just as an ant <note>original: "Pismire"</note> does in her old age, at which time nature gives her wings. <note>This refers to the winged stage of certain ants, which was historically interpreted as a sign of the insect reaching its final, "perfect" stage of life.</note>

Even so, our intellects have proceeded so far—especially in the philosophical art, or the practice <note>original: "praxis"</note> of the [Philosopher's] Stone—that we have now almost reached the **Iliad** itself. For the art of chemistry has now discovered such complexities that scarcely any greater could be invented, and they differ as much from the art of the ancients...

...different philosophers, as a clockmaker <note>original: "Clock-ſmith"</note> does from a simple blacksmith: and although both work upon iron, neither understands the other's labors, even though both are masters of their art. If *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus: The legendary Hellenistic figure considered the father of alchemy and author of the Hermetic Corpus.</note> himself, the father of philosophers, were alive now, along with the subtle-witted *Geber* <note>Geber: The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 813), a foundational figure in early chemistry and alchemy.</note> and the most profound *Raimundus Lullius* <note>Raimundus Lullius (Ramon Llull): A 13th-century philosopher and mystic; many alchemical works were pseudonymously attributed to him.</note>, they would not be considered philosophers by our modern chemists, but rather as students. There is only one thing lacking to us: to know that which they achieved—namely, the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> or <term>Physical Tincture</term> <note>Physical Tincture: Another name for the Philosopher's Stone, a substance believed to be capable of transmuting base metals into gold or healing all illnesses.</note>. While we seek that, we find other things; and unless the procreation of man were as common as it is, and Nature did not still observe her own laws and rules in that matter, we would surely err. 

But to return to what I intended: I promised in this first treatise to explain Nature, lest every idle fantasy should turn us aside from the true and plain way. Therefore, I say Nature is but one—true, plain, perfect, and entire in its own being—which God made from the beginning, placing His spirit in it. But know that the boundary of Nature is God Himself, who is also the origin of Nature. For it is certain that everything that begins ends nowhere but in that which it began. I say it is that alone by which God works all things—not that God cannot work without it (for truly He Himself made Nature and is omnipotent), but so it pleases Him to do. All things proceed from this very Nature alone; neither is there anything in the world without Nature. And although it happens...

### A New Light of Alchemy

<page-num>4</page-num>

Sometimes there are failures <note>original: "abortives"</note>; this is not Nature's fault, but the fault of the <term>Artist</term> <note>the alchemist or craftsman</note> or the environment. This Nature is divided into four realms <note>likely referring to the four elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note>, in which she works all these things which appear to us as shadows; for truly, things may be said to be cast as shadows to us rather than appearing as they really are. She changes into male and female and is compared to <term>Mercury</term>, because she joins herself to various places; and according to the goodness or the badness of the place, she brings forth things, although to us there seem to be no bad places at all in the earth. Now, as for qualities, there are only four, and these are in all things, but they do not agree, for one always exceeds another. Moreover, Nature is not visible, although she acts visibly; for she is a <term>volatile spirit</term> which performs its duty in bodies and is placed and seated in the will and mind of God. 

Nature, in this context, serves us for no other purpose than to understand her environments, which are more suitable and of nearer affinity to her; that is, to understand how to join one thing to another according to Nature, so that we do not mix wood and man together, or an ox or any other living creature and metals together. Instead, let everything act upon its own kind, and then certainly Nature shall perform her duty. The realm of Nature is nothing other than, as I said before, what is in the will of God.

The seekers of Nature <note>original: "searchers of nature"</note> ought to be just as Nature herself is: true, simple, patient, constant, etc. And what is most important of all, they should be religious, God-fearing, and not harmful to their neighbors. Then let them diligently consider whether their purpose is agreeable to Nature. Let them learn by clear examples whether it is possible—namely, from what substances anything may be made, how, and in what vessel Nature works. For if you wish to do any—

<summary>This page describes Nature as a divinely-guided "volatile spirit" that operates through four realms and qualities, emphasizing that the alchemist must work in harmony with natural laws and possess a moral, patient character.</summary>

...thing plainly, as nature herself does it, follow nature; but if you want to attempt to do a thing better than nature has done it, consider well in what, and by what it is improved, and let it always be done within its own kind. For example, if you desire to exalt a metal in virtue (which is our intention) further than nature has done, you must take a metalline nature, both male and female, or else you shall accomplish nothing. For if you intend to make a metal out of herbs, you shall labor in pain <note>original: "paine"; meaning to toil fruitlessly or with great difficulty</note>, just as you shall not produce wood out of a dog or any other beast.

# THE SECOND TREATISE.

->*Of the Operation of Nature in our Intention, and in Sperm.*<-

I said just now that nature was true, but only one, seen everywhere, constant, and is known by the things which are produced, such as woods, herbs, and the like. I also said that the researcher of nature must be true, simple-hearted, patient, and constant, giving his mind to only one thing alone, etc. Now we must begin to discuss the acting of Nature. As nature exists in the will of God, and God created her—or placed her upon every imagination—so nature made herself a seed <note>original: "( i )"; an abbreviation for the Latin "id est," meaning "that is"</note>, namely her will and pleasure within the Elements. She indeed is only one, and yet brings forth diverse things; but she works nothing without a <term>sperm</term> <note>original: "sperme"; in alchemical terms, this refers to a fundamental creative seed or life-force within matter, not limited to its modern biological definition</note>. Nature works whatever the...

The seed <note>original: "sperme"; in this context, it refers to the essential generative principle or "spirit" of a substance, not just biological sperm</note> pleases, for it is, as it were, an instrument of some craftsman. Therefore, the seed of every thing is better and more advantageous to the craftsman than nature herself. For by nature without seed, you shall do as much as a goldsmith shall without fire, gold, or silver, or a farmer without grain or seed. If you have the seed, nature is presently at hand, whether it be for bad or good. 

She works in the seed just as God does in the free will of man. That is a great mystery, because nature obeys the seed not by compulsion, but voluntarily—even as God allows all things which man wills, not by constraint, but out of His own free pleasure. Therefore, He gave man free will to choose either bad or good. 

The seed, therefore, is the Elixir of every thing, or the Quintessence <note>the "fifth essence," the purest and most concentrated form of a substance</note>, or the most perfect decoction or digestion of a thing. It is the Balsam of Sulphur, which is the same as the "radical moisture" <note>original: "Radicall moisture"; the fundamental life-fluid thought by alchemists to sustain the existence of metals and living beings</note> in metals. 

There might truly be a large discourse written about this seed, but we shall only keep to that which serves our purpose in the <term>Chemical Art</term> <note>Alchemy</note>. The four elements beget a seed through the will and pleasure of God and the "imagination" of nature. For just as the seed of man has its center or vessel in the kidneys, so the four elements—by their never-ceasing motion, each according to its quality—cast forth a seed into the center of the earth, where it is digested and sent abroad by motion. 

Now, the center of the earth is a certain empty place where nothing can rest. The four elements send forth their qualities into the outlying parts of the earth, or into the circumference of the center. This is like a man who sends forth his seed into the entrance of the woman's womb; the seed does not remain in that spot, but after the womb has received it...

So it also happens in the <term>center of the earth</term> that the <term>magnetic virtue</term> <note>original: "magnetick vertue"; refers to a natural power of attraction inherent in specific locations</note> of a particular place draws to itself anything that is suitable for its own development. The residue is cast out as stones and other waste materials <note>original: "excrements"</note>. For all things have their origin from this fountain; nothing in the world has any beginning except through this fountain. 

For example: let a vessel of water be set in the middle of a smooth, even table. All around it, let there be laid various things and various colors, and also salt, each one separate. Then, let the water be poured out into the middle; you shall see that water run out here and there. When one stream reaches the red color, it is made red by it; if it reaches the salt, it takes the taste of the salt from it, and so on for the rest. For the water does not change the place, but the diversity of the place changes the water. 

In the same way, the <term>seed</term> or <term>sperm</term> <note>original: "sperme"; in alchemical theory, this refers to the universal "first matter" or life-force, not just biological semen</note>, being cast forth from the center to the circumference by the <term>four elements</term>, passes through various places. According to the nature of the place, it creates things. If it comes to a pure place of earth and water, a pure thing is made. 

The seed and sperm of all things is but one, and yet it produces various things, as is evident by the following example. The seed of a man is a noble seed and was created and ordained for the generation of man only. Nevertheless, if a man abuses it—as he has the free will to do—an <term>abortive</term> <note>original: "abortive"; here meaning a malformed, unnatural, or monstrous birth</note> is born. For if a man, contrary to God’s most express command, should couple with a cow or any other beast, the beast would immediately conceive the seed of the man because nature is but one; and then there would not be born a man, but—

Now while the sperm is still in the center, a tree may as easily be brought forth from the sperm as a metal, and just as easily a herb as a stone—one more precious than another, according to the purity of the place. But how the Elements beget a sperm is to be treated of next, and it is done thus: 

The Elements are four: two are heavy and two are light, two are dry and two are moist. One which is most dry and another which is most moist are males and females, and so forth. Every one of these, by itself, is most apt to produce things like itself in its own sphere, as it pleased God it should be. These four are never at rest, but are always acting upon one another; and every one by itself sends forth its own essence <note>original: "thinness"</note> and subtlety, and they all meet in the center. Now, in the center is the <term>Archeus</term> <note>The Archeus is a Paracelsian term for the "master workman" or vital force in nature that directs the growth and development of all things.</note>, the servant of nature, which mixes those seeds and sends them forth. How that is done can be seen more fully in the Epilogue of the Twelve Treatises.

# THE THIRD TREATISE.
## *Of the true first matter of Metals.*

---

The first matter of metals is twofold, but one cannot create a metal without the other. The first and principal is the humidity of the air mixed with heat; this the Philosophers <note>original: "Philosophers" – In this context, the term refers to the ancient and medieval alchemists who sought the Philosopher's Stone.</note> called *Mercury*, which is governed by the rays of the *Sun* and *Moon* in the *Philosophical sea*. The second is the dry heat of the earth, which they called *Sulphur*. 

But because all true *Philosophers* mainly concealed this, we will explain it a bit more clearly—especially the weight or *poise* <term>poise: a precise balance or specific ratio of weights</term>; if this is unknown, everything is ruined. For this reason, many produce an *abortive* <note>original: "abortive" – a failed, premature, or malformed result of an experiment.</note> result from good materials. There are some who take the entire body for the matter, or *seed*, or *sperm*; others take only a piece. All of these stray from the right path. 

For example, if someone were to take the foot of a man and the hand of a woman and tried to create a human being by mixing them together, it would be impossible to do. For in every body there is a *Center*, a specific place or point for the *seed* or *sperm*, which is always the 1/8200th part—even in every *grain of wheat*—and this cannot be otherwise. The entire grain or body is not turned into *seed*; rather, only a *spark* or a certain small, essential part within the body is used, which is preserved by the rest of the body from all excessive heat and cold. If you have ears, or any

You will be removed from the company not only of those who are ignorant of the location of the sperm <note>original: "sperm"; in alchemy, this refers to the vital "seed" or life-force of a substance</note> and who endeavor to convert the whole grain into seed, but also from all those who are occupied in the fruitless dissolution of metals and desire to dissolve the entirety of metals so that, through their mutual mixing, they might create a new metal. 

But if these men considered the processes of Nature, they would see that the case is quite different; for there is no metal so pure that it does not have its impurities, though some have more or fewer than others. But you, friendly reader, shall observe the first point of nature, as stated above, and you will have enough. However, take this caution with you: do not seek for this point in the common <note>original: "vulgar"; referring to ordinary, commercially processed metals</note> metals, for it is not in them. For these metals, especially common gold, are dead, but ours are living and full of spirit, and these must be taken in their entirety. For know that the life of metals is fire while they are still in their mines, and their death is fire—namely, the fire of melting. 

Now, the first matter of metals is a certain humidity mixed with warm air, and it resembles fat water <note>original: "fat water"; likely referring to a viscous, oily, or nutrient-rich liquid</note>, sticking to everything, whether pure or impure. It is found in one place more abundantly than in another because the earth is more open and porous in some places than others, having also an attractive power. It sometimes emerges into the light by itself with a kind of covering, especially in such places where there was nothing it could easily cling to. It is known in this way because everything is compounded of three principles <note>The "Tria Prima" of Paracelsus: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note>; but in reference to the matter of metals, it is only one thing, without being joined to anything except its covering or shadow—namely, sulfur, etc.

# THE FOURTH TREATISE.

## *How Metals are generated in the bowels of*
## *the earth.*

Metals are produced in this manner. After the four elements have sent forth their virtues into the center of the earth, the Archeus <term>Archeus</term> <note>In alchemy, the "internal chemist" or vital force that directs the growth and formation of minerals and living things.</note> sends them up to the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth by means of the heat from its perpetual motion. For the earth is porous, and this "wind," by distilling through the pores of the earth, is resolved into water, out of which all things are made. 

Therefore, let the "sons of wisdom" <note>A common term for students of alchemy or those initiated into its secrets.</note> know that the seed <note>original: "sperm"</note> of metals does not differ from the seed of all things—namely, the moist vapor. Therefore, artists look in vain for a way to reduce metals back into their first matter <term>first matter</term> <note>The *materia prima*, or the original, formless substance from which all physical things were thought to originate.</note>, which is only a vapor. The Philosophers did not mean such a first matter, but only the "second matter," as Bernard Trevisan <note>original: "Bernardus Trevisanus"; a famous 15th-century alchemist known for his works on the transmutation of metals.</note> learnedly discusses—though not very clearly, because he speaks of the four elements. However, he still said as much, though he spoke only to the "sons of art" <note>Another term for practitioners of alchemy.</note>. 

But I, so that I might more clearly explain the theory, would warn everyone to take heed how they give way to so many solutions, so many circulations, so many calcinations <term>calcinations</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or oxide.</note>, and repetitions of the same processes. For it is useless to seek for something in a hard, finished substance when the essence itself is lost and can be found everywhere. Let only the second matter be sought after, not the first—namely, that which as soon as it is

But if you ask how a metal may be reduced into such a matter, I will keep strictly to the intention of the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>original: "Philosophers" in alchemy usually refers to the ancient masters of the art</note>. Above all else, I desire that the sons of Art <note>A term for practitioners of alchemy</note> would understand the underlying meaning and not just the literal words of these writings; and where nature ends—namely, in metallic bodies that seem perfect to our eyes—there Art must begin. 

But to return to my purpose (for I do not intend here to speak only of the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>), let us now discuss the matter of metals. I said a little earlier that all things were made of the liquid air, or the vapor, which the elements distill into the bowels of the earth through perpetual motion; and then the <term>Archeus</term> <note>The vital principle or formative power of nature</note> of Nature takes and sublimes <note>To transform a substance from solid to gas and back, or to refine through heat</note> it through the pores and distributes it to every place as it sees fit (as we have stated in the preceding treatises). Thus, the variety of things arises from the variety of places. 

There are some who suppose that <term>Saturn</term> <note>Alchemical name for lead</note> has one kind of seed, and <term>Gold</term> another, and so on for all the other metals. But these are foolish fancies. There is only one seed; the same seed is found in <term>Saturn</term> as is in <term>Gold</term>, and the same in <term>Silver</term> as in <term>Iron</term>. However, the location within the earth is different—if you understand me correctly—even though nature completes its work in <term>Silver</term> sooner than in <term>Gold</term>, and so on for the rest. For when that vapor is sublimed from the center of the earth, it passes through places that are either cold or hot. If it passes through places that are hot and pure, where the richness <note>original: "fatnesse," referring to the oily or combustible principle of sulfur</note> of <term>Sulphur</term> sticks to the walls, then that vapor—which the <term>Philosophers</term> have called the <term>Mercury of Philosophers</term>—applies itself to and joins with that richness, which it then sublimes along with itself.

This is the repetition and circulation of nature; it is sublimated for so long in producing new things until the place is very well purified. The more it is purified, the nobler the things it brings forth.

In the winter, when the air is cold and binds the earth fast, that oily vapour is congealed. For nature is not idle for a single moment. Metals are made in this way: the earth is purified by long distillation, then they

## THE FIFTH TREATISE.

### *Of the generation of all kinds of Stones.*

THE matter of stones is the same as that of other things; and depending on the purity of the locations where they are formed, they are created in this manner. When the four elements distill their vapor into the center of the earth, and the <term>Archeus</term> <note>Archeus: In Paracelsian alchemy, the internal "master-craftsman" or vital force that directs natural processes.</note> of nature sends that vapor forth and sublimes it; as this vapor passes through various places and the pores of the earth, it carries along with it all the impurity of the earth to the very surface <note>original: "superficies"</note>. Afterward, the air congeals it. (For what the pure air creates, the crude air solidifies, because air has entrance into air and they join together—for nature delights in nature.)

In this way, rocks and stony mountains are formed, according to the size of the earth's pores. The larger the pores of the earth, the better that place is purified. Since a greater heat and a larger quantity of water pass through such a "breathing place" or vent, the earth is <term>depurated</term> <note>depurated: purified or cleansed of impurities</note> more quickly. Therefore, metals are more easily produced in those places afterward.

<summary>This page introduces the Fifth Treatise, explaining that stones and mountains are formed when the Archeus of nature sublimes elemental vapors through the earth's pores to the surface, where they are solidified by the air.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Archeus, generation of stones, elements, depuration, Michael Sendivogius, geological formation</keywords>

Even so, experience itself testifies that gold cannot be obtained or found anywhere but in mountains, and seldom or never in plain and level ground. This is because most commonly such places are moist—not with the vapor <note>original: "vapour"; in alchemy, this refers to a subtle, rising gas or "spirit" of the earth</note> but with "elementary water" <term>elementary water: ordinary liquid water, as opposed to the philosophical "spirit of water"</term>. This water draws that vapor to itself, and they embrace one another so closely that they can hardly be separated. Afterward, the sun of the heavens "digests" <term>digesting: a slow heating process used in alchemy to mature or ripen a substance</term> them, creating that thick clay which *potters* use. 

But in places where there is coarse sand, and where the vapor does not carry with it that "fatness" or "sulphur" <term>sulphur: in this context, the active, oily, and combustible principle of matter</term>, it instead produces herbs and grass in meadows. There are other kinds of stones called precious stones, such as the *diamond*, *rubies*, *emerald*, and similar gems, all of which are generated in this manner: 

When the "vapor of nature" <note>the pure, primordial essence of the earth</note> is sublimated <term>sublimed/sublimated: the process of a substance turning from a solid or liquid into a gas and then back to a solid</term> by itself, without being joined to the fatness of sulphur, and reaches a place of pure salt water, *diamonds* are created. This occurs in cold places where that fatness cannot reach, because such fatness would actually hinder the formation of these stones. 

For we must know that the spirit of water is sublimated easily even with a small amount of heat. However, oil and fatness cannot be carried upward except by great heat, and even then only into hot places. When it comes from the center of the earth, if it encounters even a little cold, it congeals and comes to a standstill. But the vapor continues to ascend to its proper places, and in pure water, it is congealed into stones, grain by grain.

...salt, until it is colored with a digested <term>heat</term> <note>In alchemy, "digestion" refers to the slow, steady application of heat to a substance over time</note>, red or white. This color, which tends toward a further perfection, is carried up by that <term>spirit</term> because it is refined and made thin by so many repeated <term>distillations</term>. 

The spirit afterward has the power to enter into imperfect things and thus brings color to them. This color is later joined to that water—which is then partially congealed <note>solidified</note>—filling up its pores and becoming fixed to it with an inseparable <term>fixation</term> <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat</note>. 

For all water is congealed with heat if it lacks spirit, and congealed with cold if it possesses a spirit. But he who knows how to congeal water with heat and how to join a spirit with it shall certainly find a thing more precious than gold and everything else. 

Therefore, let him cause the spirit to be separated from the water so that it may <term>putrefy</term> <note>original: "putrifie"; the breakdown or fermentation of a substance, considered a vital stage in alchemical transformation</note> and become like a grain. Afterward, once the <term>feces</term> <note>The dross, sediment, or waste matter left after a process</note> are cast away, let him reduce and bring the spirit back again from the depths into the water, and cause them to be joined together once more. For that <term>conjunction</term> <note>The union of the alchemical "male" and "female" principles</note> will generate a branch of a shape unlike its parents.

# THE SIXTH TREATISE.

->*Of the second matter, and putrefaction of*<-
->*things.*<-

We have spoken of the first matter <note>original: *materia prima*; the primordial substance from which all things are made</note> of things, and how things are produced by Nature without seed—that is, how Nature receives matter from the elements, out of which she makes seed. But now we intend to treat of the seed itself, and things generated from seed. For everything that has seed is multiplied in it, but without the help of Nature, it is not accomplished. For the seed is nothing else but the air congealed <term>congealed: solidified or thickened</term> in some body, or it is a moist vapor; and unless this is resolved <term>resolved: dissolved or broken down</term> by a warm vapor, it is of no use. 

Therefore, let the searchers of the Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy</note> understand what seed is, lest they seek after a thing that does not exist; and let them know that what is brought forth by the four elements is threefold. The first is Mineral, and is that which we now speak of; the second is Vegetable; the third is Animal. Mineral seed is known by philosophers <note>Alchemists often referred to themselves as "philosophers" or "Philosophers by Fire"</note> alone; the Vegetable is common and ordinary, as we may see in fruits; the Animal is known by imagination <note>In this context, "imagination" likely refers to the internal formative power or vital faculty within living creatures</note>. 

The Vegetable seed shows us how Nature made it from the four elements. For we must know that winter is the cause of putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the decomposition of a substance, which alchemists viewed as a necessary stage for new growth or purification</term>, seeing it congeals the vital spirits <note>vital spirits: the life-force or "pneuma" believed to animate living organisms</note> in trees; and when those, by the heat of the sun (in which there is a magnetic...

Then the heat of nature, stirred up by motion, drives or forces the subtle vapor of the water to the circumference. This vapor opens the pores of the tree and makes drops distill, always separating the pure from the impure. 

Yet the pure sometimes precedes the impure; the pure stays and is congealed <note>original: "congealed"; here meaning solidified or fixed into a specific form</note> into flowers, while the impure goes into leaves, and the coarse and thick parts go into the bark. The bark of the tree remains fast and firm, but the leaves fall with cold or heat when their pores are stopped. The flowers, in solidifying, receive their color according to the heat by which the color is made, and they bring with them fruit and seed. 

Consider an apple, for example: it contains "sperm" <note>original: "sperm"; here referring to the fleshy fruit or pulp surrounding the seed</note> from which a tree cannot be grown. But inside that pulp is a seed or kernel, from which a tree is produced even without the pulp. This is because multiplication <note>original: "multiplication"; the alchemical and biological term for reproduction or increasing in number</note> is not in the pulp, but in the seed.

So we see with our eyes that Nature creates a seed out of the four Elements <note>The classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note>, so that we should not labor in vain over it; for what is already created does not need a Creator. Let this example be sufficient for the instruction <note>original: "advertisement"</note> of the reader; but now I return to my purpose concerning minerals. 

Nature creates the mineral seed, or the seed of metals, in the bowels of the earth. Therefore, it is not believed that there is any such seed in the nature of things <note>original: *in rerum naturâ*</note> because it is invisible. But it is no wonder if ignorant men doubt it, since they cannot perceive what is right before their eyes, much less what is hidden from them. Yet it is most true that what is superior is like what is inferior, and vice versa <note>A reference to the famous Hermetic axiom from the Emerald Tablet: "As above, so below."</note>. Also, what is produced above is produced [forth]

And what privilege should *plants* <note>original: "Vegetables"</note> have over *metals*, that *God* should put a seed into them and yet withhold it from metals without cause? Are not *metals* as highly valued by *God* as *trees*? Let this be accepted as a truth: that nothing grows without seed, for where there is no seed, the thing is dead. 

It is necessary, therefore, that the four *elements* should create the seed of *metals*, or else produce them without a seed. If they are produced without a seed, then they cannot be perfect; since everything without a seed is imperfect, according to the laws of composition. Anyone who does not believe this undoubted truth is not worthy to search into the secrets of nature, for nothing is created in the world that lacks a seed. 

The seed of *metals* is truly and really placed within them, and its generation happens in this way: in the first operation of *Nature*, the four *elements*—with the help of the *Archeus* <note>The "Archeus" in Paracelsian alchemy is the vital force or "master workman" within nature that directs the growth and transformation of matter.</note> of *Nature*—distill a ponderous or heavy *vapor of water* into the center of the earth. This is the seed of *metals*. It is called *Mercury* because of its fluidity <note>original: "fluxibillty"</note> and its ability to join with everything, rather than because of its actual essence. Because of its internal heat, it is likened to *Sulphur*, and after it congeals <note>original: "congealation"</note>, it becomes the *radical moisture* <note>The "radical moisture" was considered the fundamental, life-sustaining fluid inherent in all things.</note>.

Such people mistake the physical body for the seed, failing to realize that the *vulgar mercury* they speak of already has its own seed within itself. ...example. It is clear that men have seed, by which they are multiplied. Therefore, he who wishes to beget a man should not take <term>Mercury</term>, which is the body, but rather the seed, which is the congealed vapor of water.

Similarly, in the regeneration of <term>Metals</term>, common <term>Chemists</term> <note>original: "vulgar Chymists"</note> work in a backward manner. They dissolve <term>metallic</term> bodies—whether they be <term>Mercury</term>, <term>Gold</term>, <term>Lead</term>, or <term>Silver</term>—and corrode them with "sharp waters" <note>original: "sharp waters" (referring to corrosive acids like nitric or sulfuric acid)</note> and other foreign things not required for the true <term>Art</term>. Afterward, they join them together again, not knowing that a human is not generated from a human body cut into pieces; by this method, the body is ruined and the seed is destroyed beforehand.

Everything is multiplied through <term>Male</term> and <term>Female</term>, as I have already mentioned in the *Treatise of the Twofold <term>Matter</term>*. The separation of the sexes produces nothing, but a proper joining of them together brings forth a new form. Therefore, the seeds or <term>sperms</term> <note>original: "spermes" (the vital reproductive essence of a substance)</note>, not the bodies, are to be taken. Take, therefore, a living <term>Male</term> and a living <term>Female</term>; join these together so that a <term>sperm</term> may be conceived between them to bring forth fruit according to its kind.

No living man can believe that he can create the <term>first matter</term> <note>The primordial, unformed substance of the universe</note>. The <term>first matter</term> of Man is earth, and no man can make a human out of that; only God knows how to do this. However, from the <term>second matter</term> <note>Matter that has already been organized by nature into a specific potential, such as seed or sperm</note>, which is already made, a thing of that species or kind may easily be generated by the operation of <term>Nature</term> if it is put into its proper place. # THE SEVENTH TREATISE.

### *On the virtue of the second matter.*

**But** so that you may more easily understand what this "second matter" <term>In alchemy, the "second matter" refers to the specific "seed" or intermediate substance used to multiply or grow a particular thing, whereas the "first matter" is the raw, universal substance of all things.</term> is, I will describe its virtues <note>original: "vertues"; here meaning its inherent powers, properties, or medicinal potencies</note>, by which you may recognize it. 

First of all, know that Nature is divided into three Kingdoms. Two of them are such that either could exist on its own even if the other two did not; these are the Mineral, Vegetable, and Animal Kingdoms. The Mineral Kingdom can exist by itself, even if there were no humans in the world, nor any tree or herb. The Vegetable Kingdom likewise could stand by itself even if there were no metal or animal. These two are made from one substance by a single process. 

However, the third—the Animal Kingdom—derives its life from <margin>*</margin> the other two we have mentioned, and it could not exist without them. It is more noble and excellent than those two, just as it is the last of the three and rules over the others. This is because excellence always culminates in a third stage and is multiplied in the second. 

Do you see how this works in the Vegetable Kingdom? The "first matter" is an herb or a tree, which you do not know how to create—Nature alone makes it. In this kingdom, the "second matter" is the Seed, which you can see; it is through this seed that the herb or the tree is multiplied. In the Animal Kingdom, the "first matter" is a beast or a human, which you do not know how to create, but the "second matter" is the sperm, in which they are multi-

<summary>This treatise defines the three kingdoms of nature—Mineral, Vegetable, and Animal—and explains the concept of "second matter" as the seed or sperm necessary for multiplication.</summary>
<keywords>Three Kingdoms of Nature, Mineral Kingdom, Vegetable Kingdom, Animal Kingdom, second matter, seed, multiplication, alchemy</keywords>

<page-num>23</page-num>

applied, you know. Regarding minerals, you do not know how to make a metal; and if you brag that you can, you are a fool and a liar. Nature creates that. Even if you possessed the "first matter" <note>original: "first matter" — the fundamental substance from which all things originate in alchemical theory</note> described by the philosophers, it would still be impossible for you to multiply that central salt without gold. Now, the seed of metals is known only to the Sons of Art <note>initiated alchemists</note>. 

In vegetables, the seed appears outwardly; the kidneys <note>original: "reins" — the seat of internal power or vital heat</note> of its digestion is warm air. In animals, the seed appears both inwardly and outwardly; the kidneys of its digestion are the kidneys of a male. Water in minerals is the seed in the center of their heart and is their life; the kidneys of its digestion is fire. 

The receptacle of the vegetable seed is the earth. The receptacle of the animal seed is the womb of the female. The receptacle of water, which is the mineral seed, is air. And these are the receptacles of seeds, which are the solidifications <note>original: "congealations"</note> of their bodies; their digestion is their solution; their putrefaction is their destruction. 

The power <note>original: "vertue"</note> of every seed is to join itself to everything within its own kingdom because it is subtle and is nothing else but air, which is solidified in water by fatness <note>In alchemy, "fatness" often refers to a sulfurous or oily combustible principle</note>. This is known because it does not mix itself naturally to anything outside of its own kingdom. It is not dissolved, but solidified, because it does not need dissolution, but solidification. It is necessary, therefore, that the pores of the body be opened so that the sperm <note>The fluid vehicle for the "seed" or life force</note> may be sent forth. The seed, which is air, lies in the center of this sperm. When it enters into its proper matrix <note>The place where something is generated; a womb or mold</note>, it is solidified, and it solidifies whatever it finds—whether it be pure, or impure mixed with what is pure. As long as the seed is in the body, the body lives; when it is all consumed...

...consumed, the body dies. Likewise, all bodies are weakened after the emission of seed; experience also testifies that men who give themselves over too much to sexual desire <term>original: venery</term> become feeble, just as trees that bear too much fruit later become barren. Therefore, as has been often repeated, the seed is an invisible thing, but the sperm is visible and is almost a living soul. It is not found in things that are dead. It is drawn out in two ways: pleasantly, or by force. But because we are here only to discuss its virtue <note>original: "verte," meaning its inherent power or essence</note>, I say that nothing is made without seed. All things are made by the power of seed; and let the "Sons of Art" <note>A traditional term for practicing alchemists.</note> know that it is useless to seek seed in trees that are cut off or cut down, because it is found only in those that are green and living.

# THE EIGHTH TREATISE.

## *How by Art Nature works in Seed.*

ALL seed is worth nothing on its own if it is not placed into its proper matrix <term>In alchemy, a "matrix" refers to a womb or a specialized environment—like a flask or soil—that fosters growth.</term>, whether by Art or by Nature. And although seed is in itself more noble than any other creature, the matrix is its life. It causes the sperm or grain to putrefy <note>In the alchemical process, "putrefaction" is the necessary stage of decomposition that precedes transformation and rebirth.</note> and brings about a coagulation of the "pure point" <note>The essential, concentrated spark of life within the seed.</note>. Also, through the heat of its body, the matrix nourishes the seed and makes it grow. This occurs in all the aforementioned Kingdoms of Nature and is done naturally over months, years, and ages. But

<summary>The author explains that seed is the invisible essence of life found only in living things and requires a "matrix" to grow. The Eighth Treatise begins by detailing how this matrix facilitates growth through putrefaction and heat.</summary>

...that is a clever <note>original: "witty"</note> Art, which can shorten [the time needed to perfect] anything in the *Mineral* and *Vegetable Kingdom*, but not in the *Animal*. In the *Mineral Kingdom*, it perfects that which *Nature* could not, because of the crude <note>original: "crude" - raw, unrefined, or thick</note> air, which filled the pores of every body with its intensity; not only in the bowels of the earth, but also on its surface <note>original: "superficies"</note>. As I have already said in the previous chapters. 

But so that this may be more easily understood, I will add to this that the *Elements*, striving amongst themselves, send forth their seed into the *Center* of the earth, as if into their kidneys <note>original: "reins" - an archaic term for kidneys, often viewed as the source of generative power</note>; but the *Center*, helped by motion, sends it into its *Matrix* <note>original: "Matrix" - a womb or place of origin where something is formed</note>. Now, these *Matrices* are innumerable—there are as many *Matrices* as there are places, one purer than another, and so on almost to infinity <note>original: "in infinitum"</note>. 

Know, therefore, that a pure *Matrix* will produce a pure conception in its own likeness. For example, in *Animals*, there are the *Matrices* of women, cows, mares, dogs, and the like. So in the *Mineral* and *Vegetable Kingdoms*, there are *Metals*, *Stones*, and *Salts*; for the *Salts* in these two *Kingdoms* are to be considered, as well as their locations, which vary in their degree of purity.

# THE NINTH TREATISE.

### *Of the Mixing of Metals, or the drawing forth of their seed.*

We have spoken of Nature, of Art, of the Body, of Sperm, and of Seed; now let us descend to <term>practice</term> <note>original: Praxis</note>, namely, how metals ought to be mixed together, and what their correspondence or agreement is with one another. Know, therefore, that a woman does not differ from a man; both are bred of the same seed and in one <term>matrix</term> <note>original: Matrix; meaning the womb or the formative environment</note>. There was nothing different besides the level of digestion, and the fact that the matrix contained purer blood and salt. In the same way, silver is made of the same seed as gold and in the same matrix, but the matrix had more water in it than digested blood, according to the cycle <note>original: season</note> of the Moon in the heavens. 

But so that you might more easily imagine for yourself how metals couple together—so that their seed may be released and received—look at the heavens and the spheres of the planets. You see that Saturn is placed the highest; next to that is Jupiter, then Mars, then Sol (the Sun), then Venus, then Mercury, and last of all Luna (the Moon). Consider also that the virtues <note>the inherent powers or spiritual influences</note> of the planets do not ascend, but descend. Experience teaches us this: for example, that from Venus (copper) one cannot make Mars (iron), but

From *Mars* <note>Iron</note> is made *Venus* <note>Copper</note>, as it is an inferior sphere. So also *Jupiter* (or tin) is easily changed into *Mercury* (or quicksilver), because *Jupiter* is the second from the firmament, and *Mercury* the second from the earth. *Saturn* <note>Lead</note> is the first from the heavens, and *Luna* <note>Silver</note> the first from the earth. *Sol* <note>Gold</note> mixes itself with all, but is never improved by its inferiors. 

Now know that there is a great agreement between *Saturn* (or lead) and *Luna* (or silver), in the midst of which the Sun is placed; as also between *Jupiter* and *Mercury*, in the midst of which *Sol* is also placed; and in the same manner between *Mars* and *Venus*, which also have *Sol* placed in the midst of them. Chemists <note>original: Chymiſts; here referring to alchemists</note> know how to change iron into copper without gold; they also know how to make quicksilver out of tin; and there are some who make silver out of lead. But if they knew how to use these mutations to provide them with the nature of gold, they would certainly find out a thing more precious than any treasure. 

Therefore, I say we must not be ignorant of which metals are to be joined to each other, choosing those whose natures are compatible with one another. Moreover, there is granted to us one metal which has the power to consume the rest, for it is almost like their water and mother. Yet there is one thing, and that alone—the radical moisture <term>The essential, life-sustaining fluid or "seed" believed to exist within a substance</term> (namely, of the Sun and Moon)—that withstands it and is improved by it. To disclose it to you: it is called *Chalybs*, or steel. 

If gold couples eleven times with it, it sends forth its seed and is weakened almost to the point of death. The *Chalybs* conceives and bears a son more excellent than his father. Then, when the seed of that which is now brought forth is put into its own matrix <note>The vessel or environment where growth and transformation occur</note>, it purifies it and makes it a thousand times...

# THE TENTH TREATISE.

->Of the Supernatural Generation of the Son of the Sun.<-

We have treated of things that Nature makes and which God has made, so that the searchers of Art might more easily understand the possibilities of Nature. But to delay no longer, I will now enter upon the manner and Art of how to make the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>The legendary substance sought by alchemists to transform base metals into gold and achieve spiritual perfection.</note>. 

The <term>Philosophers' Stone</term>, or <term>tincture</term> <note>An alchemical essence capable of infusing its qualities into another body, often used interchangeably with the Stone.</note>, is nothing else but Gold digested to the highest degree. For vulgar gold <note>Common gold found in currency or jewelry, rather than the "philosophical" gold prepared by alchemists.</note> is like a herb without seed; yet when it is ripe, it brings forth seed. Likewise, when Gold is ripe, it yields seed or <term>tincture</term>. 

But some will ask: why does Gold, or any other <term>metal</term>, not bring forth seed? The reason given is this: because it cannot be fully ripened due to the "crudity" <note>the raw, unrefined, or cold state</note> of the air; it does not have sufficient heat. It does happen that in some places pure Gold is found, which Nature would have perfected, but was

<summary>In this section, the author begins the Tenth Treatise by defining the Philosophers' Stone as Gold refined to its highest state. He explains that while common gold is like a plant without seed, "philosophical" gold can be ripened to produce a seed or tincture if provided with the correct heat.</summary>

...hindered by the raw <note>original: "crude"</note> air. For example, we see that orange trees in *Poland* <note>original: "Polonia"</note> do indeed flourish like other trees; however, in *Italy* and elsewhere, where their natural soil is, they yield and bring forth fruit because they have sufficient heat. But in these cold places, they behave differently; for when they begin to ripen, they come to a standstill because they are oppressed by the cold. Thus, in these places, we never have their fruit naturally. But if at any time Nature is gently and skillfully <note>original: "sweetly, and wittily"</note> assisted, then Art may perfect that which Nature could not.

The same happens with metals: gold may yield fruit and seed, through which it multiplies itself by the industry of the skillful craftsman <note>original: "Artificer"</note> who knows how to elevate <note>original: "exalt"</note> Nature. But if he attempts to do it without Nature, he will be mistaken. For not only in this art, but also in everything else, we can do nothing but assist Nature, and this is done through no other medium than fire or heat. But since this cannot be achieved while there are no spirits visible in a solid <note>original: "congealed"</note> metallic body, it is necessary for the body to be loosened or dissolved and its pores opened, so that Nature may work.

But regarding what that dissolution should be, I want the reader to notice that there are two kinds of dissolution. Although there are many other types of dissolution (which are of little purpose), there is only one that is truly natural; the other is "violent," under which all the rest are included. Natural dissolution is this: that the pores of the body are opened in our water, so that the seed which has been ripened <note>original: "digested"</note> may be sent forth and placed into its proper <term>Matrix</term> <note>A "womb" or environment in which something is formed or produced.</note>. Now our water is heavenly—it does not wet the hands, and it is not common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> water, but rather something like rainwater. The "body" is gold, which yields the seed; our <term>Lune</term> <note>The alchemical name for silver, associated with the Moon.</note> or <term>Silver</term> (not common silver)...

<summary>The author explains that just as orange trees require artificial warmth to bear fruit in cold climates, metals require the craftsman's assistance through heat and a specific "natural dissolution" to release their seed. And thus the infant of the second generation is created. Now let us pass from theory to practice <note>original: "Praxis"</note>.

->*Regarding the Practice and Making of the*<-

Throughout all these previous chapters, our discussion of things has been scattered by way of examples so that the practice <note>original: "Praxis"</note> might be more easily understood. This must be done by imitating Nature in this manner—

Take eleven grains of our earth, through eleven degrees; take one grain of our Gold, and not the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> kind; and take two grains of our Moon <note>original: "Lune," the alchemical name for silver</note>, not the common kind. But be well advised that you do not take common gold and silver, for these are dead; take ours, which are living. Then put them into our fire, and let a dry liquor be made of them. First of all, the earth will be dissolved <note>original: "resolved"</note> into water, which is called the <term>Mercury of Philosophers</term>.

...fixes the matter. I tell you truly that I have revealed to you the governance, or rules of the fire, if you understand Nature.

The <term>vessel</term> <note>In alchemy, the vessel is the container (such as a flask or retort) where the chemical transformation takes place.</note> remains yet to be spoken of. It must be the <term>vessel of Nature</term>, and two are sufficient. The <term>vessel</term> of the first work <note>The "first work" usually refers to the initial stage of purification or the creation of the "mercury of the philosophers."</note> must be round; but in the second, a glass should be used, a little smaller and shaped like a vial or an egg. The <term>vessel</term> of Nature, therefore, is but one; but for the sake of brevity, we use a pair. The matter is one, but it is composed of two substances. If, therefore, you will apply your mind to making things, consider first those things that are already made. If you cannot reach or understand the things presented before your eyes, you will understand much less those things that are yet to be made and which you desire to create. 

For know that you can create nothing, for that power belongs to God alone. However, an intelligent <term>philosopher</term> <note>original: "Philosopher" – in this context, an alchemist or a seeker of natural wisdom.</note> is granted the power by God, through Nature, to make things appear that are not currently perceived but lie hidden in the shadows, and to take from them their veil. 

Consider, I beseech you, the simple water of a cloud: who would ever believe that it contains within itself all things in the world—hard stones, salts, air, earth, and fire—when, by itself, it seems to be so simple? What shall I say of the earth, which contains within it water, fire, salts, and air, yet by itself seems to be nothing but mere earth? 

O wonderful Nature, which knows how to produce wonderful fruits out of water in the earth, and from the air to give them life! All these things are done, and the eyes of the <term>vulgar</term> <note>original: "vulgar" – referring to the common people or those unlearned in the "hermetic arts," rather than a comment on their character.</note> do not see them; but the eyes of the <term>understanding</term> and <term>imagination</term> perceive them, and do so with a true sight. <meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the hidden workings of Nature, specifically focusing on the relationship between celestial and terrestrial heat and the role of the elements.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
# A new Light of Alchemy

The eyes of the wise look upon Nature differently than the eyes of common men. For example, the eyes of the common people <note>original: "vulgar"</note> see that the sun is hot; but the eyes of Philosophers, on the contrary, see it rather as cold, though its motion is hot. Its actions and effects are understood through the distance of places. The fire of Nature is one and the same with it: for just as the Sun is the Center among the spheres of the Planets, and from this Center of the heavens it scatters its heat downward by its motion, so in the Center of the earth is the "sun of the earth." By its perpetual motion, this central sun sends its heat or beams upward to the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth. 

That intrinsic heat is far more powerful <note>original: "efficacious"</note> than this Elementary fire, but it is tempered <note>original: "allayed"</note> by an Earthy water, which day by day penetrates the pores of the earth and cools it. Similarly, the Air tempers and mitigates the heavenly Sun and its heat, for this air flies around the world day after day. Unless this were so, all things would be consumed by such great heat, and nothing would be produced. For just as that invisible fire or Central heat would consume all things if the water coming between them did not prevent it, so the heat of the Sun would destroy all things if the Air did not intervene. 

I will now briefly explain how these Elements work with one another. In the Center of the earth is the Central Sun which, by its own motion or that of its firmament, produces a great heat that extends all the way to the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth. That heat creates air in this manner: the Matrix <note>In alchemy, a "matrix" refers to a place or substance in which something is developed or generated.</note> of air is water, which brings forth offspring <note>original: "sons"</note> of its own nature, yet unlike itself and far more subtle than itself.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical theory of the dual suns—the celestial Sun and the "sun of the earth"—and how the elements of air and water act as necessary buffers to prevent their extreme heat from destroying life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of the "central heat" within the earth and how it drives the movement of elements like water and air to create meteorological phenomena and minerals.</meta>

For where water is denied entrance, air enters. Therefore, when that central heat—which is perpetual—acts, it causes water to distill and be heated. Because of this heat, the water is turned into air; it then breaks forth to the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth because it will not allow itself to be trapped. Then, when it cools, it turns back into water. 

Meanwhile, it also happens that in opposite places, not only air but water exits; you see this when black clouds are violently carried up into the air. Take this as a familiar example: heat water in a pot, and you will see that a low fire causes gentle vapors and winds, but a strong fire causes thick clouds to appear. 

The central heat works in exactly the same way: it lifts the subtle <note>original: "subtill," meaning refined or rarefied</note> water into the air. That which is thick because of its salt or fatness <note>original: "fatnesse"; in alchemical theory, this refers to the oily, combustible principle of a substance</note> is distributed to the earth, through which various things are generated; what remains becomes stones and rocks. 

But some may object that if it were so, it would happen constantly, yet often no wind is perceived at all. I answer that if water is not poured violently into a distillation vessel <note>original: "distillatory vessell"</note>, no wind is produced, for a little water stirs up only a little wind. You see that thunder is not always produced, even though there is rain and wind; it only happens when the swelling water is carried by the force of the air to the sphere of fire <note>In traditional cosmology, the "sphere of fire" was thought to be the highest region of the atmosphere, just below the moon</note>, for fire will not tolerate water. You have an example before your eyes when you pour cold water into a hot furnace, which creates a thundering noise. But the reason why the water does not enter uniformly into those places and cavities is that these sorts of vessels and places are ma—

This results in great cracks or furrows being made in the earth.

Mark, and be sure that you do not take cold for hot, or hot for cold, but mix natures with natures; and if there is anything that is contrary to nature (for Nature alone is necessary for you), separate it, so that Nature may be like Nature. # THE TWELFTH TREATISE.

## Of the Stone and its Virtue.

<margin>Decorative initial I</margin> In the foregoing Treatises, it has been sufficiently spoken concerning the production of natural things, concerning the Elements, the <term>First matter</term> <note>original: "First matter"; refers to the Prima Materia, the formless base of all substance.</note>, and <term>Second matter</term> <note>The secondary stage of matter, where the universal substance has taken on a specific character but is not yet a finished body.</note>, Bodies, Seeds, and concerning the use and virtue of them. I wrote also the <term>practice</term> <note>original: "Praxis"</note> of making the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term>. Now I will reveal as much of its virtue as Nature has granted to me and experience has taught me. But to comprehend the argument of all these Treatises briefly and in few words, so that the reader who fears God may understand my mind and meaning, the thing is this: If any man doubts the truth of the Art, let him read the voluminous writings of ancient...

Ancient <note>The text likely completes the word "Ancient" from the previous page.</note> philosophers, verified by reason and experience, whom we should rightly trust in their own art; but if anyone will not trust them, then we do not know how to argue with them, as they deny first principles—for deaf and mute men cannot speak. What special privilege should all other things in this world have over metals? Why should these alone be denied "seed" without cause, and thus be excluded from God’s universal blessing of multiplication? Holy Scripture affirms that this blessing was placed in and bestowed upon all created things immediately after the world was created. Now, if they have seed, who is so foolish <note>original: "sottish"</note> as to think that they cannot be multiplied through their seed? 

The art of alchemy is true in its own right, and nature is also true, but the practitioner <note>original: "artificer"</note> is rarely true. There is one Nature and one Art, but many practitioners. Now, whatever things Nature makes out of the elements, she generates them by the will of God out of the **first matter** <note>In alchemy, the "materia prima," the formless base of all substance</note>, which God alone knows. Nature makes and multiplies those things out of the **second matter**, which the philosophers understand. 

Nothing is done in the world without the pleasure of God and Nature. Every element exists in its own sphere, but one cannot exist without the other; one lives by virtue of the other, and yet when joined together, they do not agree <note>they exist in a state of productive tension or opposition</note>. However, water is of more value than all the other elements because it is the mother of all things; upon this swims the spirit of fire. Because of fire, water becomes the first matter—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, through the struggle between fire and water. Thus, winds and vapors are generated, which are inclined and easy to be solidified <note>original: "congealed"</note> with the earth by the help of the raw air, which from the

...beginning was separated from it. And this is done without ceasing, by a perpetual motion; because fire, or heat, is stirred up only by motion. You can easily understand this by imagining a blacksmith filing iron, which grows hot through intense motion, just as if it had been heated in the fire. Motion, therefore, causes heat; heat moves the water; and the motion of the water creates air, the life of all living things. 

Things therefore grow in this way (as I said before), namely, out of water. Out of its subtle vapor, subtle and light things proceed; out of its oiliness, heavy things of greater value; and out of the salt, things far more excellent than the others. 

Now, because Nature is sometimes hindered from producing pure things—seeing as the vapor, fatness, and salt are fouled or stained, and mix themselves with the soil of the earth—experience teaches us to separate the pure from the impure. Therefore, if you wish for Nature to be improved or corrected in her actions, dissolve whatever body you like. Separate and cleanse that which was added or joined to Nature as foreign <note>original: "heterogeneous"</note> matter. Join pure things with pure, ripe with ripe, and raw <note>original: "crude"</note> with raw, according to the balance <note>original: "poise"</note> of Nature, rather than the bulk of the matter itself. 

And know that the central salt nitre <note>Nitre, or saltpeter, was considered by alchemists to be a universal "spirit" or vital salt found in the air and earth, essential for life and growth.</note> does not take more from the earth than it needs, whether that earth is pure or impure. But the fatness <note>In alchemical terms, "fatness" often refers to the oily, combustible principle of a substance, associated with the element of Sulfur.</note> of the water is different, for it is never found pure in nature. Art <note>Here, "art" refers to the practice of alchemy and laboratory techniques.</note> purifies it by a twofold heat and then joins it together.

# THE
# EPILOGUE, or CONCLUSION
### OF THESE
# TWELVE TREATISES.

->FRIENDLY READER,<-

I wrote the twelve preceding treatises out of love for the <term>sons of Art</term> <note>original: "sonnes of Art"; a traditional term for practitioners of alchemy</note>, so that before they begin the work, they may understand the operation of Nature—namely, how she produces things through her activity. I did this lest they should attempt to enter in at the gate without keys, or to draw water in a sieve. For he labors in vain who puts forth his hands to work without the knowledge of Nature in this sacred and most true Art. He lies in nocturnal darkness to whom the sun does not shine, and he is in thick darkness to whom the Moon does not appear after nightfall. 

Nature has her own proper light, which is not <note>original: "obvious"; here meaning "plainly visible" or "placed directly in front of."</note> visible to our eyes; the shadow of Nature is the physical body before our eyes. But if the light of Nature enlightens anyone, the cloud is immediately removed from before his eyes, and <term>without any let</term> <note>original: "let"; meaning hindrance or obstacle.</note> he can behold the point of our <term>loadstone</term> <note>original: "loadstone"; a naturally magnetized mineral, used here as metaphor for the alchemical principle that attracts or directs influence</note>, which corresponds to each center of the rays—namely, those of the Sun and Earth. For so far...

<summary>In this epilogue, the author explains that his twelve treatises were written to teach aspiring alchemists the inner workings of Nature, warning that without this "light of Nature," their efforts will be as futile as drawing water with a sieve.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, sons of Art, light of Nature, loadstone, natural philosophy, Sendivogius, Twelve Treatises</keywords>

--- Page 364 ---
<meta>The previous page likely ended with the word "eyes," leading into the description of how the light of Nature penetrates beyond what the physical eyes can see.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>A New Light of Alchemy.</header>

...does the light of Nature penetrate and discover inward things; take this as an example of that. Suppose a twelve-year-old boy and a girl of the same age are dressed in garments of the same style and placed side by side. No one can tell which is the male or which is the female; our eyes cannot penetrate that deeply, so our sight deceives us and mistakes false things for true. But when their garments are taken off and they are naked—so that it may appear as Nature made them—they are easily distinguished by their sex.

In just the same way, our intellect creates a shadow of the shadow of Nature; for the naked body of man is the shadow of the seed of Nature. Therefore, just as a man's body is covered with a garment, so too is man's nature covered by the body—which **God** reserves for Himself to cover or uncover. I could speak at great length and philosophically here about the dignity of man, his creation, and his generation; but since those topics are irrelevant <note>original: "impertinent"</note> to this context, I will pass over them in silence. I will only discuss the *Life of Man* a little. 

Man was created from the *Earth* and lives by the power <note>original: "vertue"</note> of the *Air*. For there is a secret food of life in the *Air*, which we call *dew* at night and rarefied water during the day; its invisible, congealed spirit is better than the whole *Earth*. O holy and wonderful *Nature*, who does not allow the sons of Wisdom to err, as you demonstrate in the life of man daily! 

Furthermore, in these twelve treatises, I have provided so many natural reasons that anyone who desires the art and fears **God** may more easily understand all things which—through **God's** blessing and my...

It is a thing of little value, yet most precious, which, though it has been described many times, I now repeat again. 

Take 10 parts of air and 1 part of living gold or living silver; put all these into your vessel. Boil this air first until it becomes water, and then until it is no longer water. If you are ignorant of this and do not know how to boil air, you will undoubtedly fail, since this is the primary matter of the ancient Philosophers.

In the first place, know what the thing is you seek after—whether it is the first or second matter <note>original: "first, or second matter"; in alchemy, the "first matter" is the raw, universal substance, while the "second matter" is that substance after it has been partially processed by nature or the artist</note>—so that you might have **Nature**, her light, and shadow revealed to you. Do not be displeased if you sometimes encounter contradictions in my **treatises**, as it is the custom of **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophers"; here referring specifically to alchemists and natural philosophers</note> to use them. You have need of them; if you understand them, you shall not find a rose without thorns <note>original: "prickles"</note>.

Weigh diligently what I have said before: namely, how four **elements** distill into the **center** of the earth a radical moisture, and how the **Central Sun** <note>original: "Centrall Sun"; a theoretical internal heat source within the Earth, believed to drive the growth of minerals</note> of the earth, by its motion, brings it forth and sublimes <note>term: sublimes; the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, or more generally, to elevate a substance to a higher state</note> it to the surface of the earth. I have also said that the **Celestial Sun** has a correspondence with the **Central Sun**. For the **Celestial Sun** and the **Moon** have a peculiar power and virtue of distilling into the earth by virtue of their beams; for heat is easily joined to heat, and salt to salt.

And as the **Central Sun** has its sea and crude <note>original: "crude"; raw, unprocessed, or coarse</note> water that is perceptible, so the **Celestial Sun** has its sea and subtle water that is not perceptible. On the surface, the beams of the one are joined to the beams of the other and produce flowers and all things. Therefore, when rain is made, it receives from the air that power of **life** and joins it with the salt-nitre of the earth. This is because the salt-nitre of the earth is like calcined **tartar** <note>original: "calcined Tar-tar"; potassium carbonate, a substance known for its ability to draw moisture out of the air</note>, drawing to itself by reason of its dryness the air, which within it is resolved into water. Such attractive power has the salt-nitre <note>term: salt-nitre; saltpeter or potassium nitrate, which alchemists believed contained a "universal spirit" or "aerial nitre" essential for life and growth</note> of the earth, which also was once air and is joined to the fatness of the earth. By however much more abundantly the beams of the **Sun** beat upon it, the greater quantity of salt-nitre is made, and by consequence, the greater plenty of...

Corn grows and increases, and this happens every day. I thought it was good to explain this much to those who are ignorant of the correspondence or harmony between things, and the power of the sun, moon, and stars; the wise do not need this instruction. Our subject is presented to the eyes of the whole world, yet it is not recognized. O our Heaven! O our Water! O our Mercury! O our Salt-nitre <note>original: "Salt-nitre"; likely referring to a purified salt or potassium nitrate, symbolizing a universal solvent</note> dwelling in the sea of the world! O our Vegetable! O our Sulphur, both fixed and volatile! O our *Caput Mortuum* <note>Latin: "dead head"; the useless residue or dregs left after distillation or sublimation</note>, or the feces of our Sea! Our Water does not wet our hands; without it, no mortal can live, and without it, nothing grows or is generated in the entire world! 

These are the epithets <note>titles or descriptive phrases</note> of the bird of Hermes <note>A famous alchemical symbol representing volatility and the transformative nature of Mercury</note>, which is never at rest. It is held in very small account, yet nobody can be without it. Thus, a thing has been revealed to you that is more precious than the whole world. I tell you plainly that it is nothing else but our sea water, which is solidified in silver and gold, and extracted out of gold and silver with the help of our *Chalybs* <note>Latin: "steel"; in alchemy, this often refers to a specific mineral catalyst or "magnet" used to attract the celestial spirit</note>, through the Art of Philosophers in a wonderful manner by a wise son of Art. 

It was not my purpose, for several reasons mentioned before in the Preface, to publish this book. However, a desire to be of service to those who are devoted to the liberal and philosophical arts prevailed with me. I wished to demonstrate to them that I possess an honest mind and to declare to those who understand the Art that I am their equal and fellow, having attained their same knowledge. I do not doubt that many men of good conscience and character enjoy this gift of God in secret; these people, being warned by my example,
