### TO THE
# SONS OF TRUTH:
## A Preface
### To the Philosophical ENIGMA,
### Or RIDDLE.

**SONS OF WISDOM,**

I have now revealed <note>original: "opened"</note> to you everything from the very first source <note>original: "rising"</note> of the universal fountain, so that nothing more remains to be discovered. For in the preceding treatises, I have sufficiently explained Nature by way of example: I have shown the theory and practice <note>original: "Theorie and Praxis"</note> as plainly as was permitted <note>original: "lawfull"</note>. The reason that encouraged me to make it so plain was this: namely, because when I had conversed with many men who thought they understood the writings of the philosophers very well, I perceived that they explained those writings far more subtly than Nature—which is simple and plain—required. Indeed, all my true sayings seemed to them (being profoundly wise, or concerned with "high things") to be of no value and unbelievable. It happened sometimes that I would explain the Art <note>original: "intimate the Art," meaning to suggest or make known the secrets of alchemy</note> to some word for word, but they could by no means understand me, not believing there was any water in our Sea <note>The "Sea" in alchemy often refers to the prime matter or the universal solvent from which all things arise.</note>; and yet they would be accounted as philosophers. Since, therefore, they could not understand my words which I delivered by word of mouth, I do not fear (as other philosophers were afraid) that anyone can easily understand what I have written. It is, I say, the gift of God.

It is true indeed, if in the study of alchemy <note>original: "Alchymie"</note> there were required subtlety and quickness of wit, and if things were of such a nature as to be perceived by the eyes of the common people <note>original: "the vulgar," meaning the uneducated general public.</note>, I saw that their fancies or wits were apt enough to find out such things. But I say to you: be simple or plain, and not too clever until you have found out the secret; when you have it, it will of necessity require wisdom enough to use and keep it. Then it will be easy for you to write many books, because it is easier for him who is in the Center and sees the thing itself than for him who walks in the Circumference and only hears of it.

You have the "second matter" <note>In alchemical theory, the "second matter" typically refers to a substance after it has undergone its first stage of purification or transformation, making it ready for the Great Work.</note> of all things most clearly described to you. But let me give you this caution: if you would attain to this secret, know that first of all God is to be prayed to, then...

He emphasizes that true understanding is a gift from God and easier for those who grasp the "Center" of the art.</summary>

Your neighbor is to be loved; and lastly, do not imagine for yourselves subtle things of which *Nature* knew nothing. Instead, abide—*I say*, abide—in the plain way of *Nature*, because you may sooner feel the truth in plainness or simplicity than see it in subtlety. In reading my writings, therefore, do not get stuck on the literal words, but while reading them, consider *Nature* and its possibilities.

Now, before you set yourselves to work, consider diligently what it is you seek, and what the scope and goal of your intention is; for it is much better to learn through the mind <note>original: "brain"</note> and imagination than through physical labor and expense <note>original: "charges"</note>. And this *I say* to you: you must seek for some hidden thing out of which is made (in a wonderful manner) a moisture or humidity that dissolves *Gold* without violence or noise—indeed, as sweetly and naturally as ice melts in warm water. 

If you find this, you have that substance from which *Gold* is produced by *Nature*. Although all *metals* and all things have their origin in this, nothing is as compatible <note>original: "friendly"</note> with it as *Gold*. While impurities cling to other things, none stick to *Gold*; furthermore, it is like a *Mother* to it. 

And so, finally, I conclude: if you will not be wise and cautious following these my writings and warnings, please excuse me, for I desire only your well-being. I have dealt as faithfully as was lawful for me, and as becomes a man of good conscience. If you ask who *I am*, *I am* one who can live anywhere. If you know me and desire to show yourselves to be good and honest men, you shall keep silent <note>original: "hold your tongue"</note>. If you know me not, do not inquire after me, for I will reveal to no <meta>The sentence concludes on the next page with the word "more."</meta>

# THE PARABLE,
### OR
## PHILOSOPHICAL RIDDLE.
### Added by way of Conclusion and Supplement. <note>original: "Superaddition"</note>

It happened once, after I had sailed almost all my life from the Arctic pole to the Antarctic, that by the singular providence of God I was cast upon the shore of a certain great sea. Although I well knew and understood the routes and properties of the sea of that coast, I did not know whether those shores bred that little fish called the <term>Remora</term> <note>The Remora was a legendary fish believed to have the power to stop a ship in its tracks; in alchemical texts, it often symbolizes the "fixed" or stable substance that captures and holds the "volatile" spirit.</note>, which so many men of great and small fortunes have until now so diligently sought after. 

But while I was watching the sweet-singing mermaids swimming up and down with the nymphs, and being weary from my previous labors and burdened with various thoughts, I was overtaken by sleep from the noise of the waters; and while I was in a sweet sleep, there appeared to me in my sleep

This goodly island was situated towards the South, being replenished with all things respecting the necessity and delight of man; **Virgil’s Elysian Fields** <note>In Roman mythology, the final resting place for the souls of the heroic and the virtuous.</note> might scarcely compare with it. The other brought forth fruit that was most white—yes, whiter than the lilies—and the leaves thereof were as fine silver. Now

These trees were called by **Neptune**, the one the tree of the **Sun** <note>original: "Sun"; represents gold in alchemical symbolism</note>, the other the tree of the **Moon** <note>original: "Moon"; represents silver</note>. There were indeed many who partly endeavored to bring it there by pipes, and partly drew it out of various things. But their endeavors were in vain, because in those places it could not be obtained by any means or method <note>original: "medium"</note>; and if it were obtained at any time, it was nonetheless useless and poisonous, unless they fetched it (as few could do) from the beams of the **Sun** and **Moon**. He who was fortunate in doing so could never get more than ten parts; and that water was most wonderful. Believe me, for I saw it with my own eyes and felt it: that water was as white as snow. While I was contemplating the water, I was filled with great wonder.

Therefore, **Neptune**, being in the meantime weary, vanished from before my eyes, and there appeared to me a great man, upon whose forehead was written the name of **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturne"; in alchemy, Saturn often represents lead or the initial "black" stage of the process known as the nigredo</note>. This man, taking the vessel, drew ten parts of water and immediately <note>original: "presently"</note> took from the tree of the **Sun** and put it in. I saw the fruit of the tree consumed and dissolved <note>original: "resolved"</note> like ice in warm water. I asked <note>original: "demanded"</note> of him, "Sir, I see a wonderful thing: water appearing as if from nothing. I see the fruit of the tree consumed in it with a most sweet and gentle <note>original: "kindly"</note> heat. Why is all this?"

But he answered me most lovingly, "My son, it is true this is a thing to be wondered at; but do not wonder at it, for so it must be. For this water is the **Water of Life** <note>original: "Water of life"; likely referring to the universal solvent or *mercurius philosophorum*</note>, having power to better the fruit of this

<meta>catchword: tree</meta>

<summary>In this alchemical allegory, the narrator observes the extraction of a miraculous "Water of Life" from the beams of the sun and moon, which the figure of Saturn uses to dissolve the fruit of the "tree of the Sun." This process represents the purification and dissolution of gold into a primal state during the alchemical Great Work.</summary>

"Please answer my question," I said. But he replied, "It is true, my son, all generation occurs through Male and Female. However, because of the distinctions between the three Kingdoms of Nature <note>The three kingdoms are Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral.</note>, a four-footed animal is produced in one way, and a worm in another. Although worms have eyes, sight, hearing, and other senses, they are produced by putrefaction <note>original: "putrefaction"; early modern science believed in 'spontaneous generation,' where organisms like worms or insects emerged naturally from decaying matter.</note>. The place, or the earth, in which they decay serves as the Female.

"So, in this philosophical work, the mother of this thing is that water of yours, which has been mentioned so often. Whatever is produced from that water is produced like worms through putrefaction. Therefore, the Philosophers have created a Phoenix and a Salamander <note>In alchemy, the Phoenix symbolizes the transformation and rebirth of the prime matter, while the Salamander represents a substance that can endure the fire of the furnace.</note>. If it were created by the union of two bodies, it would be a thing subject to death. But because it revives itself alone—the former body being destroyed—it rises as another, incorruptible body. This is because the death of things is nothing more than the separation of one part from another. And so it is with this Phoenix: its life separates itself from a corruptible body by its own power.

"Moreover, I asked him, 'Sir, are there diverse materials, or is there a mixture of things in this work?'

"But he said, 'There is only one substance, with which nothing else is mixed except the Philosophical Water shown to you so many times in your sleep. There must be ten parts of this water to one part of the body. Believe strongly and without doubt, my son, that those things shown to you by me and Saturn <note>In alchemical symbolism, Saturn represents lead, as well as the 'Nigredo' or black stage of the process, often associated with melancholy and dreams.</note> through dreams—as is the custom on this island—are not merely dreams, but the absolute truth. Experience, the only mistress of all things, will reveal this to you with the assistance of God.'

"I asked him several more questions, but he, without giving any further answer, took his leave of me and departed..."

# A DIALOGUE

**U**pon a time, several alchemists <note>original: "divers Alchymists"</note> assembled together and held a council to discuss how they should make and prepare the **Philosopher's Stone**. They concluded that everyone should state his opinion under a vow of truth. This meeting took place in the open air, in a certain meadow, on a fair, clear day. Many agreed that **Mercury** was the "first matter" <term>materia prima; the fundamental substance of all things</term> thereof; others argued that it was **Sulfur**, and others suggested different things. 

But the most prominent <note>original: "chiefest"</note> opinion favored **Mercury**, especially because of the sayings of the **Philosophers** <note>The "Philosophers" refers to established alchemical authorities like Hermes Trismegistus or Geber</note>, because they hold that **Mercury** is the first true matter of the **Stone**, and also of metals. For the philosophers cry out and say, *OUR MERCURY*, etc. <note>Alchemists often distinguished between "Common Mercury" (quicksilver) and "Philosophical Mercury" (their secret substance)</note>. And so, while they were contending among themselves for...

During various operations (everyone gladly expecting a conclusion), there arose meanwhile a very great tempest, with storms, showers of rain, and an unheard-of wind, which dispersed that assembly into diverse provinces, each person apart from the others, without reaching a conclusion. Yet every one of them imagined for himself what the conclusion of that dispute should have been. Everyone, therefore, set to work as before—one on this thing, another on that—seeking the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> <note>original: "Philosophers Stone"</note>. This continues to this day without anyone giving up. 

Now, one of them, remembering the dispute and the idea that the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> must necessarily be sought in <term>Mercury</term>, said to himself: "Although no conclusion was reached, I will work with <term>Mercury</term> anyway, and I will reach a conclusion myself by making the blessed Stone." For he was a man who was always accustomed to talking to himself, as indeed all *Alchemists* usually do. 

He therefore began to read the books of the philosophers and came across a book by *Alanus* <note>Alanus de Insulis (Alain de Lille), a 12th-century scholar whose name was often attached to alchemical texts.</note> which deals with <term>Mercury</term>. Thus, that Alchemist became a philosopher, but without reaching any conclusion. Taking <term>Mercury</term>, he began to work. He put it into a glass and applied fire to it; the <term>Mercury</term>, as it is prone to do, evaporated away. The poor, simple Alchemist, not knowing its nature, beat his wife, saying: "No one could have come in here besides you! You took the <term>Mercury</term> out of the glass!" 

His wife, crying, excused herself and spoke softly to her husband: "You will make a <term>fir-reverence</term> <note>original: "fir-reverence," a corruption of "sir-reverence," a common Renaissance euphemism for human excrement. She is mocking his failed experiment as being worth nothing but filth.</note> of these." The Alchemist took <term>Mercury</term> again and put it back into his vessel; and lest his wife should take it away, he watched it. But the <term>Mercury</term>, as is its nature, evaporated away again. The Alchemist, remembering that the first matter of the...

He could think of nothing else but common mercury <term>mercury</term><note>Also known as quicksilver, mercury was a central element in alchemical theory, believed to be a component of all metals.</note>. He swore to him, saying, "My good old Master, do not be afraid, for I am truly asleep; look at my eyes and see for yourself!" After so much labor and the loss of all his wealth, the poor alchemist <term>alchemist</term><note>A practitioner of alchemy, the medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on transforming base metals into gold or finding a universal elixir.</note> finally went mad from constantly thinking about the old man.

Well, that is true, but what has the Alchemist done?
*Merc.* Oh my Master, he has done many evil things to me, for he has mixed me—poor wretch that I am—with things contrary to my nature. Because of this, I shall never be able to recover my strength, and I am almost dead, for I am tormented almost to the point of death.
*Alch.* Oh, you deserve those things, for you are disobedient.
*Merc.* I was never disobedient to any true philosopher, but it is natural for me to mock fools.
*Alch.* And what do you think of me?
*Merc.* Oh Sir, you are a great man, a very great philosopher—indeed, you are greater than Hermes <note>original: "Hermes himselfe"; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> himself.
*Alch.* Truly, so it is. I am a learned man, but I will not praise myself. My wife also said to me that I am a very learned philosopher; she knew this from me.
*Merc.* I am inclined to believe you, for philosophers must be like that—men who, because of too much wisdom and hard work, go mad.
*Alch.* Come now, tell me therefore what I shall do with you; how I shall make the Philosopher's Stone <note>A legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> from you.
*Merc.* Oh my master philosopher, I do not know. You are the philosopher, and I am the servant of philosophers; they make of me what they please, and I obey them as much as I am able.
*Alch.* You must tell me how I must proceed with you, and how I may make the Philosopher's Stone from you.
*Merc.* If you know, you shall make it, but if you do not know, you shall achieve nothing. You shall learn nothing from me if you do not know it already, my master philosopher.
*Alch.* You speak to me as if to some simple man. Perhaps you do not know that I have worked with Princes and was considered a philosopher among them.
*Merc.* I am inclined to believe you, my master, for I know all this very well.

<summary>This page features a satirical dialogue where an arrogant Alchemist tries to bully and flatter the personified spirit of Mercury into revealing the secret of the Philosopher's Stone, while Mercury subtly mocks the Alchemist's incompetence.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Mercury, Philosopher's Stone, Hermes Trismegistus, Alchemical Satire, Laboratory Practice</keywords>

--- Page 389 ---
<lang>en</lang>
<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>A New Light of Alchemical Knowledge</header>

"I am still foul and unclean because of those mixtures that you have used." 

*Alch.* "Therefore tell me, are you the Mercury of the Philosophers?" <note>The 'Mercury of the Philosophers' refers to the idealized, pure substance required for the Philosopher’s Stone, as opposed to common quicksilver (liquid mercury).</note> 

*Merc.* "I am Mercury, but whether or not I am the one the Philosophers seek, that is for you to know." 

*Alch.* "Just tell me if you are the true Mercury, or if there is another." 

*Merc.* "I am Mercury, but there is another." 

And with that, he vanished away. The Alchemist cries out and speaks, but nobody answers him. And thinking to himself, he says, "Surely I am an excellent man; Mercury has been pleased to talk with me, surely he loves me!" And then he began to <term>sublime</term> Mercury, <term>distil</term>, <term>calcine</term>, make <term>Turbith</term> <note>original: 'Turbith'—specifically 'Turpeth mineral,' a yellow form of basic mercuric sulfate.</note> of him, <term>precipitate</term>, and <term>dissolve</term> him in various ways and with various waters; but as he labored in vain before, so now also he has spent his time and money to no purpose. 

Therefore, at last, he begins to curse Mercury and revile Nature because she made him. Now, when Nature heard these things, she called Mercury to her and said to him, "What have you done to this man? Why does he curse and revile me for your sake? Why don't you do what you ought to do?" 

But Mercury modestly excuses himself. Yet Nature commands him to be obedient to the sons of wisdom <note>A common alchemical term for true practitioners of the art.</note> who seek after him. Mercury promises that he will, and says, "Mother Nature, but who can satisfy fools?" 

Nature went away smiling, but Mercury, being angry with the Alchemist, also went back to his own place. After a few days, it occurred to the Alchemist that he had omitted something in his operations, and again he has recourse to Mercury; and now he resolves to mix him with hog's dung. But Mercury, being angry that he had falsely accused him before his mother, Nature, says to the—

<meta>catchword: Al- (likely continuing 'Alchemist')</meta>

<summary>In this allegorical dialogue, an Alchemist unsuccessfully attempts to process Mercury, eventually blaming Nature for his failure. Nature rebukes Mercury, but Mercury argues that it is impossible to satisfy foolish practitioners who do not understand the true chemical arts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a dialogue between an Alchemist and the personified spirit of Mercury, who is challenging the Alchemist's knowledge and motives.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

<header>->A New Light of Alchemy.<-</header>

*Alchemist*, What do you want from me, you fool? Why have you thus accused me? *Alch.* Are you he that I have longed to see? *Merc.* I am, but no man who is blind can see me. *Alch.* I am not blind. *Merc.* You are very blind, for you cannot see yourself; how then can you see me? *Alch.* Oh, now you are proud! I speak civilly to you, and you despise me. You do not know, perhaps, that I have worked with many Princes and was esteemed as a philosopher among them. *Merc.* Fools flock to the courts of Princes, for there they are honored and fare better than others. Were you also at the Court? *Alch.* Oh, you are a devil and not a good <term>Mercury</term><note>original: "Mercury"; in alchemy, this refers to both the physical element quicksilver and the spiritual "First Matter" of all things, often personified as the Roman messenger god.</note>, if you will speak thus to philosophers; for before, you also seduced me in this way. *Merc.* Do you know philosophers? *Alch.* I myself am a philosopher. *Merc.* "Look at our philosopher," (he said, smiling, and began to talk further with him, saying:) "My philosopher, tell me therefore what you seek after and what you want; what do you desire to make?" *Alch.* The <term>Philosopher's stone</term><note>original: "Philosophers stone"; a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the elixir of life.</note>. *Merc.* Out of what matter, therefore, will you make it? *Alch.* Of our <term>Mercury</term>. *Merc.* Oh, my philosopher, now I will leave you, for I am not yours. *Alch.* Oh, you are but a devil and want to seduce me. *Merc.* Truly, my philosopher, you are a devil to me, not I to you; for you deal most shamefully with me, after a devilish manner. *Alch.* Oh, what do I hear? This certainly is a devil indeed, for I do all things according to the writings of philosophers and know very well how to work. *Merc.* You know very well, for you do more than you know or read of; for the philosophers said that <term>Nature</term><note>original: "Nature"; here personified as the guiding force and organic laws governing the transformation of matter.</note> is to be mixed with

<meta>catchword: Natures</meta>

<summary>This page contains a satirical dialogue where the spirit of Mercury mocks an Alchemist for his spiritual blindness and false claims of philosophical expertise. Mercury rejects the Alchemist’s methods, accusing him of working against Nature despite his claims of following the masters.</summary>
